## H: head basher–home detention

### head basher
 
 <i>n.</i> [(cricketers')]

>  <b>KiNROSS</b> Bodyline was not born with Jardine as is often imagined; ... the nut ball, bean ball, or head basher, as it was variously called, was resorted to as early as 1908, to my knowledge, in breaking a stubborn partnership. <i>(1963,Please to Remember,32</i>)

### head basin
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a basin-shaped depression high in mountains and hills, that drains to another basin or lake.

>  <b>AXBEY</b> it was a fairly typical head basin, with a small lake and tussock benches dropping away to secondary growth ... <i>(1991,Along the Track: a collection of NZ hunting yarns,43</i>)

>  <b>AXBEY</b> We finally broke out into the head basin and pitched camp just on the bush edge. <i>(1991,Along the Track: a collection of NZ hunting yarns,63</i>)

### head cook and bottle washer
 
 <i>phr.</i> usu. a head/chief cook, a general rouseabout; cf. also all hands and the cook. [with variants]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> [1861] 1947 Beattie (quoting) Roberts' Diary ER. 113 <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,113</i>) [NOTES: We read that the manager was acting as cook and b.w. (presumably this stands for `bottle washer')]

>  Before we can have that long-awaited holiday, Christmas day with its familial overtones must be negotiated, and a very exhausting business it can be - especially for the chief cook and bottle washer. <i>(Dec 6, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),82</i>)

### head crush
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a contraption for holding the heads of a calf or cow for castration or branding.

>  Farm equipment wanted: Head crush ... <i>(Mar 9, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,25</i>)

### head matter
 
 <i>n.</i> [(whalers')]

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... the case or occipital part [of the sperm whale is] opened and the spermaceti, which is entirely pure is then baled out. This head matter bears the highest price in the market. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/420</i>)

### head money
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) money paid per head of stock, for grazing.

>  i think [the Maoris] should be paid some small rental for the land in the first instance, with an additional sum as head money on all stock put on the run. <i>(1868,McLean Papers,XXViii - 18</i>) [NOTES: (J.E. Fitzgerald)]

### head over turkey
 
 <i>phr.</i> variant of head over heels [1984 16 F E10 Pakuranga Coll. 16 HEAD OVER ARSE -->1984 17 F E51 Pakuranga Coll. 16 HEAD OVER ARSE -->1984 16 M E67 Pakuranga Coll. 16 HEAD OVER ARSE -->1953 13 M A1 Thames DHS 16 HEAD OVER HEELS -->1953 14 M A9 Thames DHS 16 HEAD OVER HEELS -->1953 14 M A14 Thames DHS 16 HEAD OVER HEELS -->1953 14 M A15 Thames DHS 16 HEAD OVER HEELS -->1953 14 M A16 Thames DHS 16 HEAD OVER HEELS -->1984 16 M E67 Pakuranga Coll. 16 HEAD OVER TAIL -->1984 17 M E97 Pakuranga Coll. 16 HEAD OVER TIT -->1953 14 M A17 Thames DHS 16 HEAD OVER TURKEY -->1953 14 M A12 Thames DHS 16 HEAD OVER TURKEY]

>  <b>MASTERS</b> However, with Jim at the wheel and the rest of the party barring myself, hanging grimly onto a rope, first one side and then the other, to prevent the truck from sliding off the track and going head over turkey into gullies the wet, cold and risky job was eventually accomplished. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,90</i>)

### head puller
 
 <i>n.</i> [(shoplifters')]

>  One acts as `head puller', distracting a shop assistant's attention with interest in a particular item. This allows the hoister - always leigh in this duo - to go in quickly, take what she wants and leave. <i>(Oct 23, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,17</i>)

### head rope
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>W.H.S. ROBERTS</b> At Macfarlane's the men saddled fresh horses and rode in some cattle which we had a great bother to yard, as everything was new ... The yard was divided into three - one large and two small - but as there was no crush pen, or drafting race, we had to head rope those requiring branding, and after hauling them up to a corner post, leg-roped and threw them on [44] their side, placing a long lever on their neck and under the lowest rail so that they would not move much ... When released the poor beast is not in an amiable temper ... So anyone in the yard has to climb the rails quickly ..., and the idea that it may catch you before your legs are safely over the cap rail certainly accelerates one's movements. <i>(Dec 18, 1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Dec 18 W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 43]

### head sample
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>McARA</b> Head sample: A sample of the ore taken as it entered the plant in order to determine its average value. A tail sample was taken as the tailings were discharged, so that the percentage and quantity of the precious metals being recovered were always known. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),324</i>)

>  The secretary of the Maoriland Mine at Waitekauri, received the following telegram from the mine manager on Saturday, the 13th:- "No. 3 level: Winze Young New Zealand Reef battery head sample from ore from winze for week, GBP11 per ton. Width of ore exposed, 5ft." <i>(Aug 17, 1910,Ohinemuri Gazette,2</i>)

### head shepherd
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> "i used to be head-shepherd on Grant's station". <i>(1905,Return of Joe,19</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... her husband is the head shepherd on the next station. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,152,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> <i>(1907,Return of Joe,8</i>)

>  Head shepherd: He takes charge of mustering, yards and all sheep work when the manager is not present, and very often when he is. He cannot as a rule sack a man, but can send him in to the boss, who very soon will. Cf. h. ploughman, rabbiter, fencer, etc. (C.) <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The,17</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>MULGAN</b> They were each given a station to manage; ...the thinking was mostly done by competent head shepherds ... <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,92</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> .. the man who had picked them up was named Ned Lucas and that he was the foreman, or head shepherd [of the station]. <i>(1970,Tat,94</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> `i suppose Dad will make him head shepherd next season,' he mused. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,44</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [large farms and stations] the stock manager next in line to a farm manager .

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Mr Shipley engaged me as head shepherd at GBP2 per week. <i>(Jun 1910,Alexander Mowat Diaries,26</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> He started as a cowboy at Tablelands, rose to head shepherd at Lagoon Hill and in 1949 went on to Glenburn as manager. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North island,24</i>)

>  A Head Shepherd is required for a 22,000 SU farming partnership 25 km east of Masterton. <i>(Mar 17, 2001,Dominion,65</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> Her husband is the head shepherd on the next station. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,152</i>) [NOTES: (1870)]

>  <b>STACEY</b> in those days a few of the big stations had head shepherds who relied on handy dogs to head with. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,82</i>)

### head sherang
 
 <i>n.</i> an overseer or boss [aka head serang, big serang] (See [also sherang](../A#also-sherang))

>  Cut to a really old, on-his-last-legs head-sharang, brother and keeper of all sexual-spiritual knowledge who explains the meaning of having your cake and eating it too. <i>(Nov 27, 1999,Dominion,23</i>)

>  i'm gaffer boss to every gang, / On me they're always doating [sic], / They make me stumping head serang / To engineer the voting. <i>(Jul 19, 1900,Otago Witness,30</i>)

>  At the time we all looked flabbergasted and we had Clay Oliver.. Ross Blackman.., all the head sherangs, plus the sponsors... <i>(May 11, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>)

>  ...for a start, they can demand to see the head sherang and chuck Banksie's name around. <i>(Apr 12, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  <b>DAViS</b> When they're all set up nice, the big serang gives her the nod and they pump boiling water down the outside pipe. <i>(1964,Watersiders,16</i>)

>  Every club boasts some connection with 'Ray'. He is head sherang of the tennis club (plays a great game, too), kept the R.S.A. going, and was a very busy bee helping to start the golf club. <i>(Jul 15, 1926,NZ Truth,4</i>)

>  Tank Tarrant the head sherang. Tank Tarrant the greatest thing since sliced bread. Fit as a buck rat <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,172</i>)

>  That's our boss - Tiny Freyberg, the head sherang. <i>(Aug 1958,RSA Review,7</i>)

>  He is head sherang of the tennis club (plays a great game, too), kept the R.S.A. going, and was a very busy bee helping to start the gulf club. <i>(Jul 15, 1926,NZ Truth,4</i>)

>  ... the `head serang' was in the store <i>(1917,Digger Transport Magazine, The,4</i>)

>  <b>HAMiLTON</b> For the first three weeks [in prison] i was almost exclusively concerned with the head serang <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,98</i>)

>  Jack, the head sherang, arrived, looked the site over thoroughly and set to. <i>(1966,Sea-cockies of the Manukau,121</i>)

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person in charge of a team of rural workers.

>  a person in charge of a team of rural workers -a head spanner. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### head spring/springer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person in charge of a team of rural workers.

### head stall, tubes
 
 <i>n.</i> a footbag play

>  Expect to see.. <i>head stalls, tubes</i> (using the outside of the foot to kick the sac over your head and onto the outside of the foot). <i>(Oct 1, 2001,Capital Times,9</i>)

### head station
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>JOLLiE</b> we all returned to the head station near the mouth of the river, ... <i>(1873,Reminiscences,14</i>) [NOTES: [c1846] 1873 Ed Jollie Reminiscences [MS] 14]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a principal part of a property or properties where the main homestead is located.

>  <b>TiFFEN</b> This vast property was unique in having three homesteads. Molesworth was the head station and there were houses at Tarndale, twenty-five miles away, and at Rainbow, forty miles distant. <i>(1845,Journal,119</i>) [NOTES: in Cresswell (1949) Early New Zealand Families]

>  Keriori is the head-station of Messrs. Studholme and Co who have leased the Murimotu and other blocks from the natives. [head station] <i>(May 5, 1888,Egmont Star,9</i>)

### head them
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  (phrase) from game of two-up, to make two pennies land with heads up,to make two pennies land with heads upward whence heading them. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  head them (Phr.) from game of two-up, (q.v.) to make two pennies land with heads upwards; whence heading them. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

### head up
 
 <i>v.</i> [Transf. ? from farming.]

>  As we came out [of the Snares islands], flocks of sea-birds flew and swam around us, and we headed up a great herd of nellies (<i>Ossifraga gigantea</i>, giant petrel or breakbones), and chased them awhile. <i>(1891,TrNZi,23/492</i>)

### head-and-hunt
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [joc.] an unreliable dog.

>  Head and hunt: a low-class dog that heads for home and hunts for tucker. <i>(Oct 15, 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,24</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a handy dog [mix of huntaway and heading dog].

>  <b>GORDON</b> Among the noisy and handy (head and hunt) dogs that came here, one of the most important was a half-beardie. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,75</i>)

### head-centre
 
 <i>n.</i> a large tussock set blazing first to light flax-stick torches from and to serve as a central point for burning off tussock [Cby].

>  <b>BARKER</b> `sic' ... quote. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,195</i>)

### head-ditch
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a central or feeder ditch in a furrow irrigation system.

>  in the furrow method of irrigation small furrows or corrugations are made in the direction of the slope and connected with a head-ditch supplying water .. Water is taken from the head-ditch, which follows approximately the contour of the surface, into the distributing-furrows, which may be parallel with the ditch or diverge from it. in turning the water from the head-ditch into the furrows the common practice in Central Otago is to make openings in the lower bank with a shovel. <i>(Aug 20, 1924,NZ Journal of Agriculture,108</i>)

>  One of the most frequent and heaviest operations in the preparation of land for irrigation is the formation of head-ditches. <i>(Aug 20, 1924,NZ Journal of Agriculture,115</i>)

### head-gate
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a barrier at the front of a milking stall or cattle crush.

>  <b>GORDON</b> .. my hand was jammed in the head gate's trip mechanism. [head gate] <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,17</i>)

>  The first thing he did was to open a head-gate and let an unmilked cow slip through amongst the milked lot. <i>(Sep 26, 1925,Hawera Star,16</i>)

### head-log
 
 <i>n.</i> [(sawmillers,) ? Is this a log from (thickest part of) a butt; ? or one nearest the top of the tree - left as worthless by original bushfallers and millers. -]

>  [Kauri milling gang which cuts mainly old fallen trunks] We prefer to cut head logs and fallen logs - not standing timber. <i>(Jun 17, 1986,Country Calendar (TVNZ)</i>) [NOTES: 1986 June 17 TV2 Country Calendar]

>  <b>MASSEY</b> Head-log A saw log cut from the tree trunk above the first branch. <i>(1987,Woodturning in New Zealand,93</i>)

### head-on hold
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Head on hold: in a Long Pull event the dog finishes his run by holding his sheep in a marked ring. To gain full points for this the sheep must stand facing the dog - `head on' - when being `held'. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,168</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary)]

### head-race
 
 <i>n.</i> the race which brings water into a sluice, cf tail-race. [also headrace. goldmining]

>  <b>PYKE</b> ... head races ... . represent about two hundred sluice heads <i>(1863,AJHR,D-6-15</i>) [NOTES: Repts Gfds.]

>  <b>AMOS WiLBY</b> Great flood ... - dam, headrace, and tailrace swept clear away - <i>(Feb 3, 1877,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1877 Feb 3 Diary in 1914 Pfaff The Diggers' Story 116]

>  <b>REiD</b> The lead may bear a considerable depth below the surface, and the tail race must be of the same depth, and with sufficient fall to enable the stuff from the claim to travel without blocking it up. Then a water-race is brought in to the claim from a higher level. This is termed the head-race. <i>(1884,Rambles on the Golden Coast,151</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> From the base of the fluming that makes a bridge/ To carry the head-race from ridge to ridge./ <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,76</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> The job of building the new fluming to take place of the missing head-race wasn't simple for the footings on which the structure must rest were a long way down and in ground that was anything but solid. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,119,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NOLAN</b> Head race: a channel constructed to bring water to a mining site. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,112</i>)

>  Tokaanu headrace tunnel nears completion with only 1500ft remaining to be excavated. <i>(Jun 1972,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,32/17</i>)

### headbang
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He's a controversial broadcaster maybe sometimes i've been controversial as well. So there's been a bit of headbang. <i>(Feb 2, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B1</i>)

### header
 
 <i>n.</i> heading dog. [(station)]

>  <b>BURDON</b> Dogs are usually kept in the proportion of three or four huntaways to one header now; ... <i>(1938,High Country,107</i>)

>  <b>DARE</b> Being a header he would run round the outside of any field and collect the sheep and drive them towards me. <i>(1956,Rouseabout Jane,161</i>)

>  <i>(1958,Landfall,xii 17</i>)

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> The one in the middle's a header, or an eye-dog he's sometimes called. <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,81</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> You should go [to the dog trials] and give your header a go, anyway. <i>(1970,Tat,117</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a heading dog trained to head off stock and drive them towards its master.

>  <b>BURDON</b> Dogs are usually kept in the proportion of three or four huntaways to one header now. <i>(1938,High Country: the evolution of a NZ Sheep Station,107</i>)

>  Cross a header with a huntaway and you get a 'handy.' <i>(Jun 14, 1980,Southland Times,18</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> On the drove home the huntaway would always remain at the rear of the flock while the header would be watching the wing and holding the lead so it would not swing out too far. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,26</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> i had three headers and for Otairi, dumb huntaways. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,173</i>)

>  <b>MiLLS ET AL</b> Such natural headers are generally natural pulling dogs also. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,33</i>)

### headframe
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>McARA</b> Headframe: The poppet-head erected above a shaft was also called a headframe. it had to be very strong to withstand the shock and weight of two cages plus the weight of over 1000 ft of heavy wire-rope, with a wide margin of safety, and high enough to allow double-deck cages to be used if necessary and to have at least 10 ft clearance above the detaching hook to the detaching-ring. Most of the headframes were of steel but Nos 5 and 6 were of kauri timber. The back stays were placed midway in the angle between the winding-rope and the line of the shaft. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),324</i>)

>  Alice Fell Shaft. -The collar set is now in position and the temporary headframe erected. <i>(Jul 30, 1934,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  No. 2 shaft-Concrete foundations for headframe havenow been run, and nearly all timber delivered. <i>(Nov 20, 1934,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  Other equipment consists of power plant, pumping station, headframe and mine building. <i>(Apr 6, 1938,Auckland  Star,13</i>) [NOTES: very faint image so commas are assumed.]

### headgear
 
 <i>n.</i> [(shearers')]

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> i had a quick breakfast and returned to the shed to oil the headgear, set up my handpiece and grind all the gear for the six shearers. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,136</i>)

### Headhunters
 
 <i>n.</i> a gang

>  Bealk cautiously mentions "clubs" such as the Tribesmen and the Headhunters. <i>(Apr 6, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

### heading
 
 <i>n.</i> [(farmers')]

>  ~ (1) People do not as a rule say (e.g.) `fifty cattle' but `fifty h. of cattle'. Up country people usually confine this idiom to cattle and sometimes horses. But i see to-day in `The Press' (June 27, 1933) the editor speaks of a decrease of so many h. of sheep disclosed by this year's sheep return. (C.) (2) A dog goes round to the far side of a mob of sheep and stops them. This is called heading. Hence heading dog, one whose work this is. if he does it neatly he makes a good h See also sheep dog. 3. The owner would say, `i can head with him'.(C; [1874 Kennaway Crusts],p. 45) <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The,17</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]



 
 <i>n.</i> (sheepdog trials)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Heading. in the long head the competitor stands with his dog in a ring (1 chain in diameter) facing a hillside near the top of which three sheep are set loose. When time is called the dog is dispatched, either on a left-hand or a right-hand cast, up the hill for some 800 yards to a point behind the sheep. On command the dog `lifts' the sheep and, keeping them together, `pulls' them in a direct line back to his master in the ring. To allow the dog to show complete control over the sheep, the run ends with a `hold in the ring'. This involves stopping the sheep in the ring and holding them there. The first stages of the short head are much the same, but over half the distance, 400 yards. At the point where the sheep reach the shepherd, man and dog must drive them ahead down a `drive' which is 60 yards long and 1 chain wide and defined by short poles, to a set of hurdles which are 9 ft apart and parallel with the line of the drive. The sheep are driven between the hurdles and then down a second drive, similar to the first, to the pen or yard, which is 6 ft square. After opening one side, the competitor must stand by the pen gate until the sheep are yarded. The dog must do virtually all the work and his master's right of movement and intervention are strictly limited. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/493</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(coalminers')]

>  Bords, or headings, are tunnels driven in the coal. ... lead off on both sides from the heading or main drive. <i>(1901,NZ illustrated Magazine,iV/612</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> unpayable dirt removed from a hole and piled near the head of a claim. [goldmining]

>  They are now working the old ground, composed principally of headings and tailings, and even this yields from GBP2 10s to GBP3 per week[.] <i>(Apr 11, 1870,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> The white heaps of `headings' marked the existence of holes clearly enough on the spurs, but in the bottoms of gullies, where ... the heaps of `headings' [129] were very much smaller, [would require a sharp look out]. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,128</i>)

>  The former holders had worked the headings only, leaving the real wash-dirt beneath them, but separated by a layer of barren drift from the upper auriferous strata. <i>(Oct 31, 1865,West Coast Times,2</i>)

### heading 'em
 
 <i>phr.</i> the game of two-up (See [head them](../H#head-them))

>  The Law defines the classic game of `two-up' as a gamble punishable by a magistrate, but that does not stop men from including in the pastime and losing their hard-earned gonce. There are a few `schools' in Christchurch, and a good deal of money changes hands occasionally. `Heading 'em' as it is called, is carried on in a certain licensed billiard saloon, and the patrons include some pretty hot individuals who are known to copperdom. <i>(May 18, 1907,NZ Truth,100/1/6</i>)

>  This is how the toiling and moiling son of the soil, the chap who wears bowyangs and wields a pick, and swears and sweats in so doing, particularly when in camp, loves to while away an idle and sometimes expensive hour in `heading 'em',. . . <i>(Jan 20, 1912,NZ Truth,343/7/3</i>)

>  `Two up' or `Heading 'Em' <i>(Mar 1912,Magpie, The</i>)

### heading course
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a competition course for heading dogs in sheep dog trials.

>  On a heading course, put your sheep into the designated paddock or 'spent pen' quickly and quietly. <i>(1991,Constitution of NZ Dog Trial Assoc,5B.</i>)

### heading dog
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep dog that pulls sheep towards a shepherd or handler. [also called eye dog.]

>  A driving dog is a heading dog, and therefore he should always look after the lead of the sheep, the tail usually looks after itself. <i>(May 1933,NZ Farmer,317</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Heading dogs (also called eye dogs) work by stalking, the threat of ambush and going in for the kill. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,135</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> A new hand .. is wise investing in a good huntaway or heading dog. <i>(1913,Country Work,33</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> She was just a born natural hill heading dog. <i>(1996,Boss's Story, The,141</i>)

>  You have to have good systems to guide them into the yards and you need good heading dogs. <i>(May 27, 2004,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

### heading dog man
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dog triallist who specialises in heading-dog breeds.

>  <b>KERR</b> Some blokes are heading-dog men and some are huntaway men and some go both ways ... <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,141</i>)

### heading hill
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheepdog trial course for heading dog competition.

>  Both Otairi's heading hills are excellent with very good sheep. <i>(Jun 15, 1984,NZ Journal of Agriculture,13</i>)

### heading work
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the gathering of sheep and shepherding them towards the shepherd or musterer.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Catching those Merino wethers, with a downhill getaway, called for heading work of a high degree, especially as we had to cope with four or five basins all of which might hold sheep. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,82</i>)

### headings
 
 <i>n.</i> unpayable dirt removed from a hole and piled near the head of a claim, cf. heads and contrast tailings. [(mining)]

>  [The shaft] had been partially filled up with `headings', or refuse dirt from some of the claims close by. <i>(1869,Punch or the Auckland Charivari,163</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> i started them [the diggers] to cradle some `headings', or dirt thrown out of deserted holes, which had not been considered worth washing by the lucky boys who had originally sunk them and made their little `piles'. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,18</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> The white heaps of `headings' marked the existence of holes clearly enough on the spurs. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,128</i>)

### headkicker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Unexpectedly, the Labour party's headkicker is on the side of the angels and he's enjoying this novel experience. <i>(Jun 16, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A11</i>)

### headless chookery
 
 <i>n.</i> [also headless bechookery] (See [like a headless chook](../L#like-a-headless-chook))

>  The number of officials shoehorned into the Beehive theatrette for the announcement gave you some idea of the headless chookery that has been going on. <i>(May 31, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  Mr Lange said the Cabinet had not discussed assistance for insurable damage this week, because it had not known the extent of the damage before it met. `We rely on facts, not some sort of headless chookery,' Mr Lange said. <i>(Mar 11, 1987,Dominion,2</i>)

>  All part of Bill Ralston's and ian Fraser's alleged grand plan or just more headless bechookery. <i>(Oct 22, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),70</i>)

### headlessly bechooked
 
 <i>phr.</i> Paul Holmes' description of his TVNZ bosses on his departure from the company (See [headless chookery](../H#headless-chookery))

>  [Holmes's] description of TVNZ executives as "headlessly bechooked" was pretty good, too. <i>(Oct 14, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C14</i>)

>  Eschewing anything as pedestrian as "brain dead" (his sometime colleague Lindsay Perigo's parting shot at the state broadcaster), Holmes superbly declared his former bosses to be "headlessly bechooked". <i>(Nov 20, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),70</i>)

>  Best insult: hard to go past <i>Coro Street's</i> "pie-faced slapper" and "pig's melt" (!?), but first place to Paul Holmes for "headlessly bechooked". <i>(Dec 25, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),71</i>)

 <i>n.</i> police

>  <i>Headlice</i> is also used by some to refer to the police (from 'po-LiCE'). <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 1</i>)

### headline
 
 <i>n.</i> [(dredging)]

>  <b>KiTTO</b> To lay your headline on to the dredge, you should coil the [wire] rope ... into the boat. if ... it be too hard to lay in coils, you could `flakey' it down. This is done by simply letting it lie as it falls, and it will afterwards uncoil all right. <i>(1900,Practical Dredgeman's Manual,11</i>)

### headman
 
 <i>n.</i> [(whalers') Cf gunsman]

>  <i>(1836,Piraki Log</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> A whaleboat ... soon boarded us; and the `headsman', or commander of the boat, piloted us ... . 1845 ibid. 1-123 ... the `headsmen' or leaders had less trouble in keeping their parties together. 1845 ibid 1/317 The [whalers] are enrolled under three denominations: headsman boat steerer and common man. The headsman is ... the commander of the boat; and his place is at the helm except during the moment of killing the whale, which task falls to his lot. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/110</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> i ... found 4th mate had written a letter dated 31st July to John Guard requesting the employ in his service as a boat headsman <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,453</i>) [NOTES: 1836 Aug 9 Capt. Joy Mary Mitchell log in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 453]

>  The Headsman begins to Build thar House <i>(Mar 29, 1837,Piraki Log,50,(ed. Anson 1911 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Each [whale] boat ... is furnished with a tub of whale line or rope ... the headsman being placed at the steer oar, which is used in place of a rudder to a whale boat, giving the steersman unlimited command over <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/419</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> Each [whaleboat] has either five or six oars, and a crew accordingly. The boat-steerer and headsmen are the principal men in the boat, and are generally Europeans; the rest are natives. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/49</i>)

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> A Maori crew of whalers, under a Scotch headsman, left the schooner to walk to their shore station, west of Jacob's River [Otago]; ... <i>(May 21, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 May 21 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 222]

>  <b>FiTTON</b> [Natives] became skilful ... also as `headsmen' in managing the boats, and harpooning and killing the whale. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),13</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> This Thomas was the headsman of the whaling party. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,108</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> An old-time bay whaling station consisted ... of at least two boats, with their crew of six men each. The headsman, or mate, four ordinary oarsmen, and the harpooner, or `boat-steerer,' who pulled the bow-oar, and drove in the harpoon, and who, when the whale had been struck and `fastened on,' went aft and steered the boat, while the mate took his place, and by repeated lance-thrusts killed it. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,75</i>)

>  He employed Robinson Clough as his headsman at the whaling, and seems to have worked the two `stations' at ikoraki and Oahoa in a sort of partnership ... <i>(1911,Piraki Log,163</i>) [NOTES: F.A.Anson Glossary-index]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Joe Millar was as good a headsman as ever ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,53</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(deercullers')]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> A headman must have character as well as ability above the rest of the party, for he is in charge and it is he who must knit the members together so that there are no divisions and no favourites. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,23</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> As we got closer to the reef [of Jackson's Keys] we could hear the wailing cry of the penguins - like babies crying; then the `coblers' from the rocks would come wheeling round us. Suddenly the headsman makes a sign for `oars' and they are laid in noiselessly. <i>(1982,Whaling and Sealing,5</i>) [NOTES: 1843 May 2 in 1863 C.Heaphy `A Chapter on Sealing' Southern Monthly Magazine Vol i No.4 June pp 173, 176 in 1982 Richards Whaling and Sealing 5]

### headrope
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to lassoo or tie a rope around the neck of a cattle beast or other farm stock.

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> .. as there was no crush pen, or drafting race, we had to headrope those requiring branding and after hauling them up to a corner post, leg-roped and threw them on their side ... <i>(Dec 18, 1856,Roberts Diary,43</i>) [NOTES: in Beattie (1947) Early Runholding in Otago]

### heads
 
 <i>n.</i> the chief people, those in authority; big-wigs [check this in OED]; ca1949 who are the heads of the War Memorial Appeal (the Heads of Government Departments, prob. from Permanent Head, as a term) [[Part: more general in Colonies than England esp.AIF P8: more gen. Colonial than English: very common in the AIF.]]



 
 <i>n.</i> the entrance to a natural harbour, where it meets the open sea, often marked by two headlands [ca1950 The Wellington etc. Heads]

>  <b>CRUiSE</b> Divine service having been read over the bodies ... by the Rev. Samuel Marsden, at the request of the sailors, they were taken outside the heads, and committed to the deep. <i>(Nov 27, 1823,Journal of Ten Months' Residence in NZ,175,1957 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,66</i>) [NOTES: 1833 Sep 5 in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 66 [cf Sydney Heads]]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> At 3 pm came to an Anchor at Parkinneigh [Pakanae] a Village and had i suppose 150 Natives on board and a par [pa] or Fort about 12 miles from the Heads. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),29,1963 ed.</i>)

>  this day we was put in suspense at dusk ... five Boats came into the heads ... we first took them for the fight coming from Cloudy Bay but they proved to be boats from Otago ... <i>(Jan 9, 1838,Piraki Log,64,(ed. Anson 1911) ed.</i>)

>  All the land on the Eastern side [of Wellington harbour] is exceedingly steep and hilly down to the beach, from the Heads to the mouth of the river. <i>(Jun 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,229,1966 ed.</i>)

>  [Wgton] The harbour ... entrance is not easily distinguished until you arrive close off the heads ... <i>(1841,GBPP,1841 (No.311) 119</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Oct 10 Shortland to Hobson in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 119]

>  <b>EARP</b> As the ship emerges from `the heads', the two deep bays are [91] seen within the [Wellington] harbour. <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),90</i>)

>  <b>CARTER</b> Erect a lighthouse at the `heads' ... <i>(1866,Life & Recollections of an Early Colonist,2/16</i>)

>  <b>CARTER</b> We entered Port Nicholson from the south, by a wide passage called the `Heads'. <i>(1878,Round the World,72</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Otago - strictly Otakou - was appropriated to the whole block, but really relates to a small district within the Heads, where there was a Maori village and a whaling station. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,80</i>)

>  Once outside the heads, the long slow surge took hold of us, ... <i>(1978,All Part of the Game,12</i>) [NOTES: 1942 Gaskell `The Cave' in 1978 All Part of the Game 12]

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> The barbecue was held on a sandy spit about a mile, by water, from the place where the town met the tidal creeks; there the creeks became sea, and the sea swelled out to the harbour heads. <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,32,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (The Woman's Story)]

### heads and tails
 
 <i>phr.</i> sleep heads and tails, to sleep, one person's feet at the other's head, to double up in a bed in this manner (when beds are few); cf tail in, top and tail. [ca1963 R.M. quoting N.T Moor to sleep head and foot, also used as vt to head and foot (eg children in a bed = to top and tail.]

>  Stopped at `Tommy's' [a Maori]. Kikied the `tiners'and the fish. Slept on a Maori mat, wrapped in a blanket: 4 in a bed-place - heads and tails. <i>(Aug 15, 1842,Piraki Log,143,(ed. Anson 1911) ed.</i>)

>  ... all slept heads and tails in the kitchen. <i>(Jan 9, 1851,Journal of Edward Ward,109,1951 ed.</i>)

>  ... when `the heroes of a hundred drunks' are helplessly prone on the floor [of a Kaipara bar] ... a couple of stretcher men speedily whip away the `dead drunk' off to an outbuilding in the rear ... known as `the dead house', there to sleep off the effects of the debauch. On one Sunday, 18 bushmen and gumdiggers were lying `heads and tails' in the deadhouse ... <i>(Jun 10, 1876,Weekly Herald</i>) [NOTES: 1876 June 10 Weekly Herald in 1987 Scott Seven Lives on Salt River 52]

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... on turning in we lie `heads and tails' in our blanket bags. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,90</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> We slept `heads and tails' in the half - tent. <i>(1945,Camping and Bushcraft in NZ,10</i>)

### headwater country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) high country land where the headwaters of rivers arise.

>  <b>ELLMERS</b> in the tougher headwater country, some farms were run by managers. Perhaps the owners, having pioneered them to get a start, had moved down country to better farms. <i>(1993,Schoolhouse Under the Sky,9</i>)

### headworks
 
 <i>n.</i> Sc. of a river,? the streams etc. which form the watershed at its head.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> i spent most of my time in the headworks of the Wilberforce and Unknown Rivers. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,132</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... they had also been leasing Baton Tops a block of high rugged bushbound country between the Baton headworks and a branch of the Karamea. <i>(1973,Big Country,12,1977 ed.</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [high country] the joining of mountain streamlets to form a river [as in headwaters.]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The station, which is just on 60,000 acres in extent, takes in the headworks of the Hope, Doubtful, Lewis and Boyle rivers, and with its miles of river flats and great stretches of open bushbound tops, vies with the Lake Sumner country in its attraction. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,171</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Nokomai station .. is along narrow block taking in the headworks of the Nokomai and Nevis rivers. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,118</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Huiarau Station takes in a big area of the country at the headworks of the Mata river, and runs back to Mt Arowhana ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North island,206</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The bushbound country beyond the run boundaries and the headworks of the Ant and the Esk, were also a great possie for wild cattle. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,146</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The station carries between 600 and 700 head of cattle (300 breeding cows) and with stock running right to the very headworks, there is a lot of river work. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,173</i>)

### heady
 
 <i>adj.</i> shrewd, clever

>  After the All Blacks had beaten Wales in the first match of their grand slam, i was startled to find a sports writer lambasting commentator Murray Mexted for applying the word "heady" to rugby, when "heady", in his view, only meant things like "intoxicating" etc. News to me. Phrases like "He plays a heady game" or He's a heady player" have been in New Zealand sporting vocabulary all my life, meaning "thinking, intelligent, or analytical". <i>(Dec 3, 2005,Otago Daily Times,45</i>) [NOTES: Civis' column]

>  Mex Files: "He's always been a heady player." - Murray Mexted may have been trying to describe Leon McDonald as "intoxicating, impetuous, or overbearing". Or he might simply have got the wrong word. <i>(Nov 14, 2005,Otago Daily Times,21</i>) [NOTES: hayden.meikle@odt.co.nz See quoted discussion from Civis 03 12 2005.]

### health camp
 
 <i>n.</i> (Politics) a camp for the improvement of children's physical or emotional health; a (usu. summer) camp orig. for undernourished or physically deprived, extended later also to emotionally troubled children [see also Davin Gloss]. [Health camps in the US were for tuberculosis; camps for children were set up in Britain but run in entirely different from those in New Zealand. The first to be held in NZ catered for 55 children at Turakina where children sleep in tents, eat meals out of doors, practised daylight saving, and which was run on military lines, the children acting as corporals, sergenats, buglers, etc. New Zealand health camps focussed on prevention rather than cure.]

>  Take, for instance, the cases [sc. of undernourishment] which have come to light at the Campbell's Bay Health Camp. <i>(Jul 2, 1947,NZ Observer,3</i>)

>  This photograph shows sun bathing in progress at Dr. Gunn's Health Camp, Turakina. <i>(Nov 27, 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,1</i>)

>  With a background like this, Banks was a candidate for the health camps. Set up after World War One, they were originally intended for malnourished and tubercular children. After World War Two they specialised in children who were nervous, solitary and emotionally troubled... We would never admit to the other kids that we were going to a health camp, because, supposedly, any kid who went to health camps was diseased. <i>(1997,John Banks : A Biography,22</i>)

>  <i>(May 1, 1925,NZ Education Gazette,68/1</i>) [NOTES: [get quot]??]

>  <b>WiLY & MAUNSELL</b> [caption] Maraetai The mission station occupied the flat area beyond the Waikato Children's Health Camp shown above. <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,64</i>)

>  <b>BLANC</b> HEALTH CAMPS. Just as the milk-in-schools scheme was the first line of attack on malnutrition in children, so is the Health Camp a second line. <i>(1949,Money, Medicine and the Masses,155</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> `Miss Vontofoff', claims Waiwini, ... `i'm going to write a letter to Mr Reardon when i go to Health Camp. <i>(1958,Spinster,25</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> The originator of health camps was Dr Elizabeth Gunn who, as school medical officer at Wanganui, organised small summer camps for undernourished children in 1919 ... . <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/527</i>)

>  <b>McELDOWNEY</b> ... i had a cup of tea at the Phys. Ed. School where Lilian ... told me two riddles: ... `Where would you take a sick fairy?' `To an elf camp.' <i>(1966,Warm South,187,1983 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (1966) 1983 McEldowney Warm South 187 [1966 Sept. 8]]

>  <b>HUTCHiNS</b> ... the other eighteen children in his family were decidedly scrawny, and indeed several spent periods in health camps to build them up to acceptable proportions. <i>(1983,Rugby Rabbits,31</i>)

>  The days of `Matron' being `in charge' of a health camp have disappeared. <i>(1986,Health,38(4): 13</i>)

>  Every so often a few Whistlers disappear and you know they've been sent off to health camp. <i>(1986,New Outlook,21/49</i>) [NOTES: Macrae (The Kingsland Kid)]

>  health camp NZ outdoor camp for short-stay accommodation of children of below-average health. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,346</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 227]]

>  Government cost cutting is reducing the number of children who can be cared for in health camps... New Zealand's seven health camps have been providing a six-week break for children from disadvantaged backgrounds for more than 80 years. <i>(Aug 1, 2001,Dominion,15</i>)

>  The Nelson health camp opened last week at Tahuna school. <i>(Jan 12, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,51</i>)

>  Dr Gunn asked for the Board's assistance in conducting a health camp for boys and girls between the ages of 11 and 12 during the summer for a period of three weeks. <i>(Aug 28, 1919,Wanganui Chronicle,5</i>)

>  The chairman reported that Dr Elizabeth Gunn had arranged, with the sanction of the Board and the Education Department, to hold a health camp at Turakina in grounds lent by Mr Lethbridge. <i>(Nov 18, 1919,Wanganui Chronicle,6</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### health stamp
 
 <i>n.</i> an annual postage stamp which includes a levy for support of health camps; a postage stamp, formerly issued annually, part of extra value of which went towards supporting health camps. [The health stamps were first produced in 1929, following an appeal in 1928 by Danish immigrant Kirstine Nielsen.]

>  The sale of health stamps that year [sc. 1931] was the lowest on record. <i>(Jan 29, 1947,NZ Observer,8</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> By 1929 ... summer health camps [had been] held in various localities by voluntary organisations, funds being raised by voluntary subscriptions, supplemented by the sale of health stamps which first appeared in 1929. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/527</i>)

>  health stamp NZ postage stamp the cost of which includes a levy for support of health camps. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,346</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 227]]

>  i sold Health Stamps - and we had fun - from 11.30 a.m. to 1.30 p.m. <i>(Nov 18, 1953,NZ Observer,14</i>)

>  .. it was explained that the health stamps now on sale at all post offices, besides having the usual postage value, also provide a penny for the fund which is needed to subsidise the health camps ... <i>(Nov 28, 1931,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  A Danish immigrant mother provided the inspiration for New Zealand health stamps .. The 2011 health stamp is expected to be released about July. <i>(Mar 26, 2011,Manawatu Standard,A2</i>)

>  New Zealand Post sponsorship manager Cath Hopkin said yesterday that 3000 children would benefit from the sale of the 1996 children's health stamps. <i>(Oct 21, 1996,Press, The,B5</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> of central Ni country, not bush-sick; sound.

>  in the neighbouring healthy areas the sands are practically absent, and the ash is underlain by the upper Te Kuiti limestone with its attendant residual clay ... <i>(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,12/4</i>) [NOTES: Taylor]

### heaps
 
 <i>n.</i> a lot, everything; as an intensifier esp. give (someone, something) heaps, ?occas. =taking the mickey. [usually in the phrase "give it heaps" 1984 14 F E134Wgton Girls C. 9 HEAPS BETTER -->1984 16 F E36 Pakuranga Coll. 18 HEAPS GOOD -->1984 16 M E88 Pakuranga Coll. 13 HEAPS: GIVE IT HEAPS 1987 Feb L.Bauer [Bus Rodeo - on PA system - announcer to people not listening] There's some blokes out there giving me heaps. [In a contest - supporters to their entrants - give them heaps] give it heaps, to make a supreme effort]

### heartland
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The cheeky title and the suggestive poster of Conrad Newport's The Hand Job appear on the surface to have little tp do with his light comedy's theme of pitting the old, "heartland" New Zealand against the cold, cash-driven new New Zealand. <i>(Dec 5, 2000,Evening Post,12</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> <i>Dracophyllum longifolium.</i>

>  <b>HAAST</b> Fortunately there were many shrubs of <i>Dracophyllum longifolium. </i> growing in this locality, which even in their green state burn freely, and which enable the traveller in those regions, so much exposed to rain, to light a fire with comparative ease. This plant has needle-shaped leaves, generally of a brownsh tint,and small bell-shaped white flowers. it [115] is generally called a native heath by the Europeans. As some of the bushes grow to a height of ten feet with thick branches, it offers wood enough to keep up a cheerful fire. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,114</i>)

### heather
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PYKE</b> (And) *`The rose and the laburnum intermingle their blossoms with those of the clianthus and the mimosa; the english primrose and the scottish blue- bell nestle amidst lovely New Zealand ferns: the modest gowan mingles with sweet-scented native heather; honey-suckles twine around cottage-porche s, in company with the native clematis and the thick-spreading native con vulvulus: and fragrant violets bloom beneath the shade of picturesque palm-trees.' <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The</i>)

>  <b>DEWAR</b> The grass playing area [of the Southland school] was backed by a great area of `boxwood' or native heather. <i>(1953,Chaslands,19</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> introduced spp. of <i>Erica</i> (=heath) and <i>Calluna</i>

>  <i>heather [=] heath [s] ... heather, [s] Calluna vulgaris</i> (L.) Hull <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,37</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>heather, bell [s] Erica cinerea</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,37</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>heather, Scotch [=] heather [s]</i> <i>ibid.</i> 68 <i>Scotch heather [=] heather [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,37</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Calluna vulgaris</i>

>  in parts of the central North island and eastern South island, ling heather (<i>Calluna vulgaris</i>) is a serious threat to alpine plant communities. <i>(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 53/54</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PYKE</b> The modest gowan mingles with sweet-scented native heather; honey-suckles twine around cottage-porches, in company with the native clematis and the thick-spreading native convolvulus. <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,21</i>)

### heatwave knot
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>OWEN & PERKiNS</b> (Bob Edwards, bushman) He [the swagger] said to me, `Well, i'm going through to the coast, and if you like you can roll up your bluey and come along with me.' ... i had a light bedroll, and it was good weather at the time, so i was down to what swaggies call a `heatwave knot' - down to a very light bedroll. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,70</i>)

### heave
 
 <i>n.</i> [[Mining term.]]

>  FAULT.- A fissure, accompanied by a displacement of the strata, same as a slide, heave, hitch, throw, and trouble. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,54</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> ... `heave' is a term only applicable to a longitudinal shift of rock or lode <i>(1889,Miner's Guide, A,63</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> ?a winch load. [(watersiders')]

>  <b>DAViS</b> Snapper Joe was our midship's winchman, and ... he displayed a keen sense of repartee. Particularly when he had dropped a heave at the moment and just missed someone. <i>(1964,Watersiders,41</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> in various whaling uses; to flock together [1838]



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to peak oars

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ</i>)

### heave  ahead
 
 <i>v.</i> to get on with one's story. [(imperative) [Part 2. (naut) gen. in imper. Get on with one's job or story; mid 19-20 c.],]

>  <b>WHiTWORTH</b> Well, heave a-head and tell me, how do you like the city? <i>(1869,Comic Guide to Dunedin,43</i>)

### heavies
 
 <i>n.</i> threats [(prisoners')]

>  <b>BEATON</b> They all have to come to you. The Boss! But you're startin' to be a bore. So piss off with the fuckin' heavies, 'cos you don't scare me ... <i>(1984,Outside in,40</i>)

### heavily-bushed
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Usually in this heavily-bushed country, with a rainfall of over 200 inches a year, it is so damp that one has difficulty in getting enough dry kindling wood to light a fire. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,146</i>)

### heavy
 
 <i>adj.</i> Sc. water, under heavy pressure; ?plentiful. [goldmining]

>  Where the water is heavy and there are no means of cutting a tail-race, water-wheels have been erected ... . water ... is conducted by means of ditches or `races' to the scene of operations. <i>(1863,AJHR,D-6-14</i>) [NOTES: Rept. Drummond, mining surveyor]



 
 <i>adj.</i> (of land)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> in the heavy back-country, the soil was being swept off steep faces and down the big rivers. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,13</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> The fact that fine-wool sheep are usually run on hard country is another reason why these sheep are slower to shear. They do not suit good country because their feet go quickly on heavy or wet land. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land] liable to retain water or contain debris from forest growth.

>  Ben Lomond was then merely ring-fenced and the flats were heavy with rushes and buried timber. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol i,20</i>)

>  <b>PLUMMER</b> it was 300 acres, all good heavy country and with really no waste ground on it. <i>(1994,From Drover to Director,65</i>)

### heavy bush
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) thick, almost impenetrable native forest and undergrowth.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> This block, which is mainly heavy bush, takes in some wonderful flats ... <i>(1952,High Country Journey,165</i>)

>  <b>TAiT</b> After 1905, 600 acres were milled from heavy bush and sown down in English grasses ... <i>(1961,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol iii,151</i>)

>  <b>MACiNTOSH</b> The Chaslands road was extremely winding with sharp bends and heavy bush either side. <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,107</i>)

>  Although this from a distance looks like heavy bush, it is, in fact, all cut-over bush ... <i>(Mar 9, 1967,NZ Farmer,8</i>)

>  ... heavy bush country for that reason did not lend itself to marked dispute except incidentally. <i>(1906,TrNZi,38/2</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> The bulk of this is cut with a slasher in the same manner as underscrubbing heavy bush. <i>(1913,Country Work,31</i>)

### heavy ewe lambs
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural)

>  The best of the male lambs sold to $82, with one pen of heavy ewe lambs knocked down at better than $83. <i>(Jun 27, 2005,NZ Farmers' Weekly,21</i>)

### heavy gold
 
 <i>n.</i> gold found in heavy particles [DNZE at heavy adj.]

>  On Saturday week ... rumours were current in town ... that [127] heavy gold had been discovered at the back of the hospital, ... <i>(Jan 5, 1866,West Coast Times</i>) [NOTES: 1866 Jan 5 West Coast Times in 1914 Pfaff The Diggers' Story 126-127]

>  They've struck a big old placer somewhere, you bet. They are on heavy gold, too, for i saw some of the stuff that they traded away down to the township with one of the providers of garments for the Gentiles. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/i/75,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

### heavy wet
 
 <i>n.</i> the worst of a rainstorm

>  <b>TiNNE</b> ... a sheet of corrugated iron to keep the `heavy wet' off. <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,55</i>)

### heavy-conditioned
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of fleecewool] containing extreme amounts of grease.

>  Heavy conditioned wool contains more than the required amount of grease for the preservation of the fleece. <i>(1919,Wool in NZ,19</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> .. provision might have to be made in the classing plan for accommodation of heavy-conditioned fleeces. <i>(1965,Wool & Woolclassing,115</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Shorter heavy-conditioned wool from about the legs and perhaps belly can be swept to one side as shearing proceeds ... <i>(1965,Wool & Woolclassing,103</i>)

### heavy-timbered
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of stag-heads] having antlers with many points.

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> The stag was a heavy-timbered 12 pointer of noble lines - a "royal" head that far surpassed anything he had ever expected to secure. <i>(1996,Great Hunting Yarns,187</i>)

### heavy-woolled
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>BOWEN</b> A few second cuts are quite often made in opening up the neck. These should not be judged too harshly as on wrinkly or heavy-woolled sheep second cuts are very easily made in this place. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,108</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep] bred with thick, long wool.

>  <b>PERRY</b> .. the general trend with breeders has been to stick to the very heavy-woolled animal. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,43</i>)

### hebe
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) <i>Hebe salicifolia</i> and related spp.; see koromiko.

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> (<i>Hebe</i> from the Greek, the cup-bearer of the gods). Maori name, <i>Koromiko</i>. The evergreen, shrubby habit, in addition to other botanical characters, as well as the distribution, separate the genus <i>Hebe</i> from <i>Veronica. </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,393</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> it has ... speedwells or veronicas which in England are modest little blue flowers ... but which here in New Zealand are not only an enormous family of numerous species with a range of colours varying from white to dark purple, but, in the form of shrubs and trees (known as Hebes), reach a [137] height of forty feet. <i>(1951,Confessions,136</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Hebe .. .. koromiko .. .. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> There were the scrublands of blackened second growth, the pastures of danthonia and browntop for the Romney flocks, the marginal land of bracken, hebe and flax, the reproachful columns of burnt-over bush. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,20</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Hebe</i> This is a large genus of shrubs related to <i>Veronica</i>; of the 80 or more species only a few grow naturally outside New Zealand although many ... are popular garden plants in many parts of the world. <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,68</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> He moved away, paused by a low-growing hebe, ... <i>(1984,Murder in Vain,68</i>)

>  <i>hebe n.</i> any of a large group of evergreen shrubs, mostly native to New Zealand, with spikes of flowers; also called <i>koromiko</i>. [name of Gk goddess] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,348</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Similar in general appearance to the hebes with spreading four-ranked leaves are a number of species of <i>Pimelea</i> or New Zealand `daphnes'. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,168</i>)

>  Another reason might be that the plants have coloured foliage such as cultivars of flax, Pittosporum, Coprosma and Hebe <i>(Mar 25, 1993,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  Coastal shrub species such as flax, toetoe, hebe and manuka were planted. <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 7</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [mistaken use by non-N.Z. writer.]

>  <b>ARMFELT</b> `. . . wrenching her ankles on springy tangles of hebe-veronica' <i>(1971,Catching Up,208</i>)

### heckashin/heckashun
 
 <i>adv.</i> = a hell/heck/hang of a

>  <b>TURNER</b> Children are notable users of slang ... and in these good old days the most current included hang of a, hangashun, heck of a and heckashun, all intensives. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,159</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> 1978 editorial note 191: `hellashin'. Originally in the expression `hellashin hot' to match `perishing cold'. The more aceptable variations were `hangashin' and `heckashin'. <i>(1978,All Part of the Game,191</i>)

### heckler's veto
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Hdg] Media react to `heckler's veto'/ Are the views and `messages' of these [special interest pressure groups] being given undue prominence too often? Are they tending to beat into the ground opposing views, equally or more valid but less well organised? Are they imposing, in fact, a form of censorship. ... The term `heckler's veto' was coined by the incoming president of the Auckland Council for Civil Liberties in September. He was publicly voicing concern over the action of police in seizing anti-Black pamphlets from the pro-white Nationalist Workers' Party. <i>(Nov 29, 1984,Evening Post,2</i>)

### Hector burgoo
 
 <i>n.</i> rice and raisins [(obs. trampers')]

### Hector's dolphin
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cephalorhynchus hectori</i> [(obs. trampers') 1920's late - early 1930's, R.M. fide Norman Elder.]

>  Hector's dolphins, tutumairekurai or special ocean dweller, are considered taonga by local Maori, she says. <i>(Feb 22, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A11</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> HECTOR'S DOLPHiN .. Confined to New Zealand . . . <i>Other names: Cephalorhynchus hectori</i>; native dolphin, New Zealand porpoise. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,305</i>)

>  But the world's smallest and probably rarest oceanic dolphin, found nowhere outside New Zealand waters, is also in grave danger. The Hector's dolphin population around Banks Peninsula, the largest concentration ... will decline. <i>(Jun 11, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  New Zealand's only native dolphin is under threat of extinction ... The total population of the rare hector's dolphin is 3000 to 4000, with about 660 living in the Banks Peninsula area ... <i>(May 22, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,1</i>)

>  Beyond the swells pounding the Catlins Coast were hector's dolphins, the world's rarest and smallest marine dolphin at only 1.4 metres long. <i>(Aug 14, 2004,Dominion Post,E16</i>)

>  Fishermen have nicknamed Hector's dolphins "puffing pigs." <i>(Jan 2, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  it [Maui's dolphin] is closely related to Hector's dolphin, of which only about 7,200 remain. <i>(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,57</i>)

### Hector's tree daisy
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Olearia hectorii</i> and <i>Olearia fimbriata</i> (See [tree daisy](../T#tree-daisy))

>  A significant number of hector's [sic] tree daisy, or Olearia hectorii [sic]and Olearia fimbriata [sic], were identified on three properties in the Coal Creek catchment last year... <i>(Jan 24, 2006,NZ Herald,A2</i>)

 <i>n.</i> as a distinguishing epithet in names of plants (distinguish plants found in or under hedges (e.g. bedstraw) from plants used as or suitable for hedges (e.g. Laurel), see bedstraw, laurel, mustard, nettle, stachys.

### hedge laurel
 
 <i>n.</i> mapau

>  <b>MORRiS</b> Hedge-Laurel, n. a name given to the tree Mapau(q.v.), an evergreen shrub of New Zealand, of the genus Pittosporum (q.v.). it has dark glossy foliage and handsome flowers, and is planted and cultivated in the form of tall garden hedges. See also Laurel. <i>(1898,Austral English,195</i>)

### hedgie
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) the introduced hedge sparrow. [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> HEDGE SPARROW <i>Prunella modularis</i> Local names: Dunnock, Hedgie. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,215</i>)

### heel
 
 <i>v.</i> bite (usu. cattle) in the heel to make them move. Of a (usu. farm-)dog.

>  <b>LiLiCO</b> He was a powerful dog to head or heel cattle. ... i have often wondered how Teddy came to be such a fine natural heeler. <i>(1934,Sheep Dog Memoirs,6</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> The dog `Trojan' ... would head, hunt, lead, back, heel cattle, catch a horse, pick up cast sheep, open and close gates, retrieve sacks ..., strike sheep with his paws, walk on his hind legs, ring a bell and perform many other minor tricks. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,2</i>)

>  Heel: Bite (usually cattle) in the heel to make them move. May be a command to return to the owner's side. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,24</i>)

>  <b>RENNiE</b> Heeling Most strains of Border Collie have plenty of eye and will also heel cattle. <i>(1985,Working Dogs,39</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the corner of a shear blade, next the grip.

>  Heel. The corner of a shear blade, next the grip. (C.) <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The,17</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]



 
 <i>n.</i> the base of the water side of a dam or the earth side of a retaining wall.

>  Heel- The base of the water side of a dam or the earth side of a retaining wall. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,62</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES RANSOM</b> Heel- Not found elsewhere, but its counterpart, toe is in Webster. <i>(1989,Personal Communication</i>)

### heel up
 
 <i>v.</i> to bite or nip the heels (of cattle) to drive them on as a heeling dog does.

>  <b>REiSCHEK</b> When the animal stops one [dog] will heel it up. <i>(1889,Wonderful Dog,35</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Old Jim had two good dogs, one of which frightened the beasts by heeling them up (biting their heels), and the other by pulling their tails, ...also (1954) 138 <i>(1940,Te Waimate,121</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [cattle dogs] to control cattle by nipping their heels.

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Old Jim had two good dogs, one of which frightened the beasts by heeling them up and the other by pulling their tails. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,138</i>)

### heeler
 
 <i>n.</i> a dog that works stock by snapping at their heels; heeling dog. [(farmers')]

>  <b>LiLiCO</b> He was a powerful dog to head or heel cattle. ... i have often wondered how Teddy came to be such a fine natural heeler. <i>(1934,Sheep Dog Memoirs,6</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Wag was a heeler, small, quick, and savage, and Tot, a heading dog, small and as quick as lightning, one of the type that are apt to whip in occasionally and make a boar sit down. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,203</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cattle dog trained to work cattle from behind, noising and nipping beasts from the rear. (See [header](../H#header))

>  heeler - a small quick cattle dog that stirs things along by 'hock hunting.' <i>(Oct 15, 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,25</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> There are two main kinds of cattle dogs. Headers and heelers. The heading dog takes a swoop around the cattle while the heeler barks loudly, nips in behind and often bites the heels of a straggler to keep the mob moving forward. <i>(1978,Mimiwhangata Station,37</i>)

>  <b>MiLLS ET AL</b> Spring, gentle with sheep but a hard heeler of cattle when necessary, was following at a discreet distance behind cows with calves moving around a hill-face. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,104</i>)

### heeling
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RENNiE</b> Heeling Most strains of Border Collie have plenty of eye and will also heel cattle. <i>(1985,Working Dogs,39</i>)

### heeling dog
 
 <i>n.</i> a cattle dog which bites the heels or legs of recalcitrant beasts, cf. heel up. [(farmers')]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Some of the riders, ... went after the bullock which was galloping in the direction of the bush. Two heeling dogs were also in the hunt; they promptly attacked the beast, who, in attempting to rush one of them, stuck his horn into the ground and broke it off short. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,139,1954 ed.</i>)

>  Jim had the severest heeling dog i have seen. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,101</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Heeling dog: A dog which when working cattle will nip them in the fetlocks (heels) to shift them. There is a specific breed - the Australian blue heeler - which has a natural instinct to heel. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,168</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dog that controls cattle by nipping their heels.

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Two heeling dogs were also in the hunt.. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,139</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Jim had in his team the severest heeling dog i have ever seen. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,101</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> He always kept a heeling dog for the purpose of keeping his mules on the track. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North island,27</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> it took the united effort of two men to get the horses on to the bridge, one leading, and the other behind with a manuka stick, aided perhaps with a heeling dog. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,79</i>)

### heeling up
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He tied a rope round her nose and horns, got in the car, set it going, and had the dog "heeling up" behind. <i>(Jul 11, 1918,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

### hei aha
 
 <i>exclam.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, Ryan: = never mind, don't worry]

>  But, hei aha! i'm proud to be Maori and there's a lot of labelling out there about "plastic Maoris", too. <i>(Jul 13, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,2</i>)

### hei tiki
 
 <i>n.</i> a tiki, usually of greenstone, worn as a pendant [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>DONNE</b> The jade hei-tiki was suspended on the neck. <i>(1927,Maori Past and Present,31</i>)

>  There are two hei tiki linked to Maori chief Te Rauparaha's family. <i>(May 20, 2003,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

>  <b>ihimaera</b> When he turned he saw that Harper had picked up the greenstone pendant. 'What's this?' 'it's a hei tiki... it's supposed to protect me.' <i>(2000,Uncle's Story, The,62</i>)

>  The museum also received a number of hei-tikis of the rarer feminine type. <i>(Feb 7, 1934,Feilding Star,4</i>)

### heifer
 
 <i>n.</i> [(whalers' obs)]

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/318</i>)

>  heifer n. (Obs.) whaler's term for a woman. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a two-year-old cow

>  CAME ASTRAY. About two years ago, on the run of R.J.S. Harman, Esq., a heifer. <i>(Aug 8, 1857,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> when the calves are no longer fed ... they are called weaners till the next batch come along, then yearlings springers, heifers when they first calve at two years ... <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,150</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a girl, sheila. [(occas., esp. adolescents')ca1945 Wellington]

### heifer dust
 
 <i>n.</i> occas. humorous var. of bulldust. [ca1941 (Silverstream)]

### heifer paddock
 
 <i>n.</i> a weaning paddock.

### heifer station
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  the track of downs and vallies between [the Hurunui] ... and the Waiau-ua, now in the occupation of Mr.Caverhill as a heifer station, is the finest and richest cattle run i have seen in either island . <i>(Feb 21, 1851,NZ Government Gazette,30</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Jan Weld Letter in 1851 Feb 21 NZGG 30]

### heihe
 
 <i>n.</i> ?he ihe, see ihe (garfish).

### heihei
 
 <i>n.</i> chicken, poultry

>  Heihei - oven-roasted free-range heihei rubbed with piripiri, filled with a mini-flavoured cranberry and orange farce, garnished with a fresh avocado and corn relish. <i>(Feb 28, 2004,Dominion Post,E6</i>)

### heiva
 
 <i>n.</i> [Description is a haka.] =?

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Some of them being in remarkable good spirits, gave us a heiva, or dance, on the quarter-deck. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/220</i>)

### heke
 
 <i>n.</i> one who migrates, party of emigrants, migration, a migration, a body of migrating or travelling people, cf hikoi. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Got a little consolation from Matiu te Huia, who declares that the supposed taua (or war party) is really a heke (or migration) of women & children. <i>(Aug 1, 1864,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/117</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  Rauparaha ... made preparations for his great heke or exodus from Kawhia to this district. <i>ibid.</i> it was by these two war-trails that the three hekes or migrations ... came to assist their kinsman Te Rauparaha in holding Kapiti ... <i>(1906,TrNZi,38/3</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> Accordingly [Te Rauparaha] and the whole of his people set forth on that heke or migration ... <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,39</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRiE-SMiTH</b> esp. of the great migration Descent is claimed by the Ngati-kahungunu from Rongo-kako, whose son Tamatea arived in ... one of the most famous canoes of the great heke or migration from the mythical Hawaiki. <i>(1921,Tutira,54</i>)

>  Moriori traditions do not go very far back of the heke people ... <i>(Mar 22, 1933,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/73</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>BUCK</b> The great migration (heke) from Hawaiki is the most famous event in Maori history, because all the tribes trace their aristocratic lineages back to the chiefs of the voyaging canoes. <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,36</i>)

>  <b>JENKiNS</b> They [several Maoris] were going on an up-country heke or migration and in doing so had to cross a combined rail and road bridge over the river. <i>(1970,NZ Mysteries,102</i>)

>  <b>MiKAERE</b> in the winter of 1877, with more than a hundred followers, the prophet set out for Omarama. The people of the heke (migration) called themselves israelites, ... Te Maiharoa's migration followed the coastal trail south to the Waitaki River. ... The heke moved at Te Maiharoa's pace and direction ... <i>(1988,Te Maiharoa,69</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> rafter [ORIGIN: Maori, cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 49.]

>  The heke [rafters] will be structural and go to the ground. <i>(Aug 10, 2005,NZ Herald,B6</i>)

### Heke('s) War
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARBERRY</b> Anecdotes of the old war at the Bay of islands The war in the North commonly called `Heke's war' differed in many respects from the present campaign ... <i>(1865,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1865? Carberry Journal (MS ATB)]

>  The 58th Regiment.. engaged in what is termed "Heke's War," in 1845-46. <i>(Sep 29, 1899,Auckland Weekly News,2</i>)

>  The capture of Ruapekapeka Pa put an end to what was known as the Heke War. <i>(Dec 21, 1900,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 6</i>)

### hekeretari
 
 <i>n.</i> a secretary [ORIGIN: Maori, a transliteration]

>  WESTERN EXECUTiVE COMMiTTEE .. Na te hekeretari KH Kane <i>(Sep 12, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,10</i>)

### heketara
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Olearia rani</i>, a tree daisy with sweet-scented flowers [Maori]; wharangi.

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Eketera, <i>D'Urville. Lepidium oleraceum. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),764</i>) [NOTES: 1867 Hooker Handbook (List of Names) 764]

>  [Native name] Heketara .. [Family] Compositae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>[Olearia Cunninghamii, Hook.f.] Heketara. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,287</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> The heketara (<i>Olearia Cunninghamii</i>) produces multitudes of daisy-like flowers in the spring. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,37</i>)

>  Compositae <i>Olearia rani</i> heketara <i>(1975,Tane,21/8</i>) [NOTES: (Wright Vegetation Mt Maungatawhiri)]

>  <b>MOORE & iRWiN</b> <i>OLEARiA RANi, heketara.</i> A tall, openly growing shrub or small tree with smooth hard trunk. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,130</i>)

>  HEKETARA/ <i>Olearia rani</i>/ <i>(1982,Field Guide to Common NZ Trees & Shrubs,31</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Accompanying species may be the two common larger-leaved coprosmas both known as karamu: <i>C. robusta </i> and <i> C. lucida </i>, the bubbly-leaved ramarama (<i> Lophomyrtus bullata </i>), wharangi (<i> Melicope ternata </i>), the `tree daisies' heketara (<i> Olearia rani </i>) and the familiar large-leaved rangiora (<i> Brachyglottis repanda </i>). <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,103</i>)

>  Scattered about beneath are heketara (the one with the pale, veiny underleaves), five-finger, rangiora and a selection of coprosmas. <i>(Jan 5, 2004,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

>  ..the descent begins through pigeonwood with it's bright orange berrries, the daisy heketara, and young kamahi. <i>(Jan 6, 2006,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

### helapi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MiCALFE</b> Auntie Herotia had sent a message that she could get a hundred tins of elapi don't ask how - [fn] helapi: Hellaby's: canned beef <i>(1985,Hey Hey Hey</i>)

### Helengrad
 
 <i>n.</i> Wellington [After Helen Clark - reference to her supposed overall control of Government]

>  Labour Maori have to go home to their hapu each weekend... Then they return to Helengrad each week, knowing their duty as Labour members is to support the Prime Minister's majoritarian strategies. <i>(Sep 16, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  "This certainly isn't Helengrad, " one [party insider] said, referring to the prime minister's omnipotence." <i>(Mar 10, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C6</i>)

>  When announcing the weather on his <i>Politically incorrect</i> show, [Perigo] says" "Auckland fine, Hawke's Bay showers, and rain for Helengrad, formerly known as Wellington." <i>(May 9, 2000,Dominion,10</i>)

>  Welcome to the New Zealand of Helengrad and "flag-bearing" art. <i>(Jan 13, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>)

>  Does she know the Capital's earned the nickname, Helengrad, such is her total command of issues, initiatives and air time? <i>(Apr 27, 2000,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Mrs Shipley replied that thanks to the autocratic style of Ms Clark, Wellington was now known as Helengrad... <i>ibid.</i> "Double standard abound on the Government's side of the House. Wellington city has already been named Helengrad, and it is for very good reason," Mrs Shipley said. <i>(May 4, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  The full might of Helengrad was marshalled - against the public's right to know about the likely fate of millions of taxpayer dollars, and millions more of poor people's savings. <i>(Mar 3, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  it has been dubbed 'Helengrad' and only half-jokingly. This Prime Minister runs things her way, with her people... But just when Beltway Wellington was beginning to despair of finding any remaining pockets of good, old-fashioned abusable power and influence, some wit coined the term "Helengrad"... <i>ibid.</i> 21 No old hand in Wellington will argue that "Helengrad" is just a catchy insult. <i>(Apr 14, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  Take the recent rumble in Helengrad over the PM's husband. <i>(Apr 7, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>)

>  Ceremonial flypasts in Helengrad just won't be the same with Cessnas. <i>(Apr 21, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  The last few weeks have been tough weeks in Helengrad," shadow leader of the House Roger Sowry told delegates. <i>(Aug 19, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  A fierce bogywoman stalks the dreams of National MPs. She dwells in Helengrad, communicates with a handful of devoted acolytes (the press) via cellphone, and savages any who dare to question her. <i>(Aug 21, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Clark is more controlling. When they call it Helengrad, that's what it is. No one comes on the show without Helen Clark's approval, unless they've a mind to cock a snook at her. <i>(Oct 7, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  The messy boys playing with rocks in the national sandbox found some unexpected friends this week - the formerly stand-offish senior boys from Helengrad High school. <i>(Nov 4, 2000,Dominion,14</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the Labour Government of Helen Clark

>  But don't think Scroogism is solely the domain of the right. He'd enjoy Helengrad, too. <i>(Nov 7, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C10</i>)

>  Your July 1 edition shows just how little connection the socialist government of Helengrad has with ordinary workers. <i>(Jul 14, 2003,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  By 1999, the civil unrest and humanitarian crisis reached a peak, and Helengrad invaded. <i>(Oct 27, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  One of [National's] most successful barbs is the creation of "Helengrad" - where to step out of line is to be punished. <i>(Jul 15, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  This scheme is a natural one for the new state of Helengrad. <i>(Jan 18, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C8</i>)

 <i>n.</i> vomiting

"Technicolour yawn" is used for an instance of vomiting, as is "liquid laugh', i believe. <i>(Aug 24, 1998,Letter,00</i>)

### hell's teeth
 
 <i>exclam.</i> ? var. of hell's bells. [1984 16 F E47 Pakuranga Coll. 35C HELLS TEETH -->1953 16 M A31 Thames DHS 5 HELLS TEETH -->1953 16 M A32 Thames DHS 5 HELLS TEETH -->1984 16 F E18 Pakuranga Coll. 5 HELLS TEETH]

### hellashin
 
 <i>adv.</i> an intensive, cf hangashun (not merely children's (as Part, quot B 1942 for Aust) often used as mild slang by adults). [1950 LF. 13-38 Gaskell It's hellashin funny]

>  <b>GASKELL</b> `He thinks he's hung his clothes up and they fall down ... it's hellashin funny. <i>(1950,All Part of the Game,158</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> it's hellashin funny. 1978 editorial note 191: `hellashin'. Originally in the expression `hellashin hot' to match `perishing cold'. The more aceptable variations were `hangashin' and `heckashin'. <i>(1984,Some Other Country,96</i>) [NOTES: 1950 Gaskell in 1984 Some Other Country 96]

>  <b>HULME</b> i'll be hellashing popular if i sent it home drunk. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,29</i>)

### hellashun
 
 <i>adv.</i> (See [hellashin](../H#hellashin))

 <i>n.</i> Russell/Kororareka (c. 1840s)

>  The port that developed here may have been a bit more respectable than Kororareka (now Russell), whose grog shops and fleshpots earned it the reputation as 'Hell-Hole of the Pacific'. <i>(Nov 2003,AA Directions,58/33</i>)
