### hemp
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Phormium tenax</i>, an obs name, used by early writers then reintroduced as a commercial name ?for the manufactured product of flax; flax, also native hemp, New Zealand hemp. [1773 CVS (Andersen) Native hemp 1841 PAS (Andersen)]

>  <b>W.B. MONKHOUSE</b> The materials of this sort [of mat] thinks [sic] is of true hemp - it looked like it and had the proper smell. There grows spontaneous every where a kind of very broad pladed [sic] grass like flags of the nature of hemp of which might be made the very best Cordage Canvas etc. There are two sorts [sic] the one finer than the other, of which the natives make cloth, rope, lines, netts etc. [Footnote: the New Zealand flax or Phormium tenax]. <i>(Oct 11, 1769,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1769 Oct 11 W.B. Monkhouse in 1955 Cook Journal 1/573]

>  <b>ELDER</b> i should wish you to bring as much hemp as you possibly can, and such spars and timber as you may, with Mr. Hall, judge valuable. Pork, if it is to be obtained, and salt fish, rosin, or any other natural productions. <i>(1934,Marsden's Lieutenants,55</i>) [NOTES: 1814 Marsden in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 55]

>  A change of name to `hemp' in 1871 was of considerable advantage to the New Zealand flax industry in general. <i>(Jun 1955,NZ Geog. Society Rec.,23</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cannabis sativa</i>

>  <i>hemp [s] Cannabis sativa </i>L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,37</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  (And) <i>(1773,Voyage Towards the South Pole,1779 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WiLKES</b> The native hemp (<i>Phormium tenax</i>) is a most useful plant; ... <i>(Mar 1840,U.S. Exploring Expedition,1/316</i>)

>  <b>PETRE</b> (And) (2) New Zealand hemp. <i>(1841,Account of the Settlements NZ Company</i>)

>  New Zealand Hemp (or Flax) ... . This product is commonly called New Zealand Flax. [N.Z.J. thinks `hemp' more applicable] <i>(1840,NZ Journal,1/1/7</i>)

>  <b>WALLACE</b> NEW ZEALAND FLAX or Phormium fibre, designated in the home-market [? = British] as New Zealand hemp <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,249</i>)

>  PHORMiUM TENAX (New Zealand Hemp). The preparation of Phormium for export is an industry of New Zealand which has not made vast progress ... <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,153</i>)

>  The refuse accumulates as a waste product in the process of producing the fibre variously known as New Zealand flax, New Zealand phormium, or New Zealand hemp. <i>(Jan 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,16</i>)

>  <b>PiNFOLD</b> Good business was often done with New Zealand hemp, the world's best fibre for binder-twine. it belongs to the lily family and is popularly termed `flax'. <i>(1930,Fifty Years in Maoriland,19</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> The manufacture of fibre from phormium - `New Zealand hemp', as it is now called - has become one of the staple industries of the Dominion. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,109</i>)

>  New Zealand hemp, or `flax' as it is termed locally, is one of the so-called `hard' or leaf fibres, as contrasted with the `soft' fibres from the bast of stems. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,537</i>)

>  new zealand flax or new zealand hemp n, usu cap N & Z 1 : a tall New Zealand herb (<i>Phormium tenax</i>) having erect, sword-shaped leaves and scarlet or yellow flowers 2 : the strong fiber from the leaves of New Zealand flax used chiefly for cordage, twine, and mattings <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1524</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Phormium tenax</i> J.R. & G. Forst. Common names: New Zealand flax, New Zealand hemp <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,36</i>)

### hen and chicken(s) fern
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Asplenium bulbiferum.</i>

>  Many a bathroom is home to a hen and chickens fern, <i>Asplenium bulbiferum</i>, but did you know that its unfurling koru can be steamed, slathered in butter, seasoned with pepper and salt and served on toast for a quintessentially Kiwi brunch? <i>(May 23, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),15</i>)

>  Common ground species include hen and chickens fern, shining spleenwort, hairy fern (<i>Lastreopsis hispida</i>) and gully fern (<i>Pneumatopteris pennigera</i>. <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/76</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> <i>A[splenium] bulbiferum</i> ... the broad spear-shaped fronds ... have ... leaflets which often produce bulblets near the top of the leaf on the upper surface which develop into young plants while still on the frond. <i>(1952,Chatham islands,11</i>)

>  <b>MARTiN</b> The HEN AND CHiCKEN FERN (<i>A[splenium] bulbiferum</i>) is a ubiquitous species in New Zealand forests ... Under humid conditions, small plants may be observed growing from near the tips of the pinnae, whence popular name. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,103,1961 ed.</i>)

>  Aspleniaceae <i>A[splenium] bulbiferum</i> hen & chickens fern <i>(1975,Tane,21/8</i>) [NOTES: (Wright Vegetation Mt Maungatawhiri)]

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Asplenium bulbiferum</i> Forst f. Common name: <i>Hen and chickens fern</i> Maori names: <i>Mauku, mouku </i>... `Mauku' has been identified as <i>Asplenium bulbiferum, Cordyline pumilio</i>, and <i>Hymenophyllum</i> spp. by Williams and as <i>Asplenium bulbiferum</i> by Best. `Mouku' has been identified as <i>Asplenium bulbiferum</i> by Williams and as <i>Marattia salicina</i> by Tregear. <i>Asplenium bulbiferum</i> is a common fern throughout New Zealand forests. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,25</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Asplenium bulbiferum</i> HEN AND CHiCKENS FERN ... Mauku ...most fronds sprouting tiny plants (bulbils) which drop off ... <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,36</i>)

>  The most familiar is the production of <i>bulbils</i> by the hen and chickens fern (<i>Asplenium bulbiferum</i>). <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/69</i>)

>  Below the stately tree ferns, hen and chicken ferns line the track, carrying their young progeny on their leaves until they're ready to bud off. <i>(Jan 3, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  Under one bench in the nursery, in a flat tray, leaves of hen-and-chicken fern, Asplenium bulbilferum [sic] are sprouting ferns on a mix of pumice and peat. <i>(Sep 15, 2000,Dominion,14</i>)

>  More palatable are the young fiddleheads of many species, especially the hen and chickens fern, kiokio (<i>Blechnum novae-zealandiae</i>), gully fern (<i>Pneumatopteris pennigera</i>, shining spleenwort (<i>Asplenium oblongifolium</i>) and the shield ferns (<i>Polystichum richardii</i> and <i>P/vestitum</i>). <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/77</i>)

 <i>n.</i> <i>Lamium amplexicaule</i>

>  <i>henbit [s] Lamium amplexicaule</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,37</i>)

### hencackle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  in Switzerland the "impossible" has graduated to the "easy day for a lady" ("hencackle" in our patois). <i>(1934,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,iii/9</i>)

>  Just as difficult peaks sometimes fall to the common denomination of "hencackle" so do inaccessible valleys suffer the indignity of being traversed between excursion trains. <i>(1935,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,iV/3</i>)

>  in passing, i must relate that it was on the winter slopes of the Blimit that the famous word "hencackle" was coined by Evan Wilson... it was also suggested that [Mt] Oates was too far away and that it was too big an effort under such bad conditions to which Evan replied: "Oates is a hencackle" and a Hen-cackle, Oates has remained to this day. <i>(1935,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,iV/8</i>)

>  if hencackles are hencackles then the outcrop we were on was yet easier, a "chicken-cheep." <i>(1935,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,iV33</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(mountaineers').]

>  in Switzerland the `impossible' has graduated to the `easy day for a lady (`hencackle' in our patois) ... 85-6 [2 Apr.] This route is easy but long and ought not to be called a `hen-cackle'. <i>(Aug 1934,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,9</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> For the cost of perhaps four shillings a party may spend a week-end climbing to a 6000 or a 7000 feet top, whence they traverse and descend snowburnt and happy. All the lasting alpine partnerships on Canterbury have been formed in the easy expeditions. `Hencackles' we call these expeditions. <i>(1939,Unclimbed NZ,33</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Hen-cackle ... is applied by mountaineers to a mountain that is easy to climb. Doubtless there is a wider application of the term since a mere hen cackle, a trifle, seems to have been the origin of the application. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,57</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> Hen-cackle. A mountain easy to climb: New Zealand mountaineers': C. 20. B., 1941. Difficult enough to cause a cackle among the women. Cf. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1075</i>)

>  hen-cackle NZ colloq. mountain considered easy to climb. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,350</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hen cackle a mountain rated easy by climbers; any trifle. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,56</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the easiest mustering beat of a particular muster.

>  <b>GORDON</b> But the bloke who got an easy hill for his day's work would have a "hen cackle." <i>(1983,Other Side of Farming, The,1</i>)

>  <b>MCGiLL</b> steep as a hen's face: a hillside to be mustered, but the opposite of a hen cackle, which is an easy climb. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,96</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> usu. in it's only a hencackle; not worth a hencackle; a mere hencackle a small or trivial thing (esp. of a minor wound etc.), a trifle. [ca1938 to fellow `easy' please]

### henrietta
 
 <i>n.</i> a mouse [(hist.)]

>  Beattie it is well known that the southern Maori call the mouse hinereta (henrietta) because a vessel of this name (`Elizabeth Henrietta'= 1823) introduced these little creatures to their notice. <i>(1921,TrNZi,52/74</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Pastor Wohlers, long a missionary working among the natives on Ruapuka in Fovaux Straits, states that mice were first brought to the island in the `Elizabeth Henrietta', which was wrecked there in 1824, and that even as late as 1873 they continued to be known as `henriettas'. <i>(1922,Naturalisation of Animals and Plants</i>) [NOTES: (R. Mason)]

### Henry
 
 <i>n.</i> [(WW1)Is this a nonce, or fictional use; or is it a real WW1 term.]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> At the war there were Froggy soldiers that always carried round little boxes - just little wee things less than the size of a matchbox. And inside there was a model of Christ on the cross - just a tiny thing. And over the top there was a word you could hardly read but it looked like H-E-N-R-i. Well, Mr Anderson said, i could never work it out why they were called Henrys - that's what us diggers called them, little Henrys. D'you know, i've always meant to ask somebody why they were called that name. And it was on the tip of Dave's tongue to explain, but he thought no. Because he'd have to explain why the i's weren't J's, and --[note 271:] ... His explanation of `little Henry' would have been based on i.N.R.i., Jesus of Nazareth, King of the Jews, from Lat. iesus Nazarenus, Rex iudaeorum. <i>(1949,i Saw in My Dream,140,(1974 ed. Rhodes) ed.</i>) [NOTES: (WW1) Is this a nonce, or fictional use; or is it a real WW1 term.]

 <i>adj.</i> very strong

### herd
 
 <i>n.</i> Cf and contrast mob, etc.

>  A Captain Lethart, of Sydney, also here [at Akaroa] since the 10th of November last, has established a cattle run, with about thirty head of horned cattle, and had two stockmen in charge of them. From the appearance of this herd, i am inclined to believe the pasturage much better than at the Bay of islands <i>(1841,GBPP,(No.311) 106</i>) [NOTES: 1840 June 28 Thomas Bunbury's Report in GBPP House of Commons 1841]

>  <b>HAY</b> When we have arranged to do so, there is a grand muster of the herd. Mob after mob is brought up and enclosed in the fenced clearings ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/209</i>)

>  Herd. All a man's cattle. All the cattle on a station. A number of cattle together is a mob. H.cattle and run cattle are expressions used to distingush them from stud or dairy cattle. (C.) <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The,17</i>)

### herd manager
 
 <i>n.</i> a farm worker hired to manage and milk a dairy herd, usually in excess of 500 cows on a corporate or investment property.

>  Herd manager $20,000 plus per year. Contractor or herd manager is needed for 1987-88 season <i>(Feb 1987,Dairy Exporter (NZ),19</i>)

>  That's for people who want to be herd managers and covers feed budgeting and planning and other skills. <i>(Nov 11, 1999,NZ Farmer,7</i>)

>  After work, Roy and herd managers Jason Walker and Michael Wardlaw go to Otorohanga Workingman's Club for a bit of 'team building.' 2000 Press Nov 18. 43 Two herd managers are required to assist farm manager with 1400 cows. <i>(Sep 12, 2000,Waikato Times,5</i>)

>  Currently my husband is on $600 a week for herd manager and milk harvesters are on hourly rates and take home more money on a regular basis ... [herd supervisor] <i>(Nov 5, 2001,Fencepost.com</i>)

### herd test
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to measure the productivity of each cow in a herd. (See [herd-testing](../H#herd-testing))

>  The Scotts have used the Dairy Board's artificial breeding service for 35 years and they also herd test. <i>(Mar 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,42</i>)

>  Pauline says she will probably herd test next season for the first time. <i>(Apr 19, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,13</i>)

### herd tester
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a worker who visits farms to assess total herd productivity by testing individual cows' milk for milk fat content. (See [herd-testing](../H#herd-testing))

>  Most folk [get] the herd-tester to do their cows. <i>(Jan 22, 1927,NZ Dairy Exporter,35</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We have been rather keen on herd-testing in this district and most folks [get] the herd-tester to do their cows. <i>(Jan 22, 1927,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,35</i>)

>  <b>McALPiNE</b> He became a herd tester so he could travel and look at the land. <i>(1998,Passionate Pen, The,114</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> He told me ... all about one of his sisters who was training to be a herd-tester. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,94</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> A herd-tester is a man or woman who goes from farm to farm sampling milk to test cows for their productivity and the fat content of their milk ... <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,149</i>)

>  herd-tester NZ & Austral. person employed in herd-testing; herd-testing NZ & Austral. testing of the butterfat content of the milk from a dairy herd. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,351</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 230]]

>  The boys' suggestions on how to be a 90s man and get women - lie, but sensitively - and the cross-dresser in the dairy herdtester's tunic, freezing worker's white gumboots and black fishnet stockings do more to tickle the fancy of the almost exclusively female audience than the promise of a suffrage medal apiece. <i>(Dec 18, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

### herd walk
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an inspection of stock on farms by a group of breeders or buyers. (See [deer walk](../D#deer-walk))

>  i have had a look at what's on offer in Hawke's Bay this year and was very impressed, but unfortunately i missed the national herd walk at the end of April as well, so i've had to rely on the advice of agents this year. <i>(May 31, 2002,Dominion,30</i>)

### herd-testing
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  A striking case of the value of testing the individual members of a herd is reported from a district where a herd testing association has been established. <i>(Jul 15, 1911,NZ Journal of Agriculture,1/26</i>) [NOTES: 1911 July 15 NZJAg 1/26 in 1966 Turner 45]

>  The herd-testing movement is worthy of special mention. Practically 20 per cent. of the dairy cows in the Dominion are tested for butter-fat production monthly during the milking season. <i>ibid.</i> 346 Although herd testing and milk recording societies existed in Germany, Denmark, England and other countries many years earlier, systematic herd testing appears to have been first developed in New Zealand about the year 1920, when a model Herd Testing Association was established by the New Zealand Department of Agriculture in the Wairarapa. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,26</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the testing of dairy herds for disease, or to estimate milk production, etc. [cf. cow-testing]

>  We have been rather keen on herd-testing in this district and most folks [get] the herd-tester to do their cows. <i>(Jan 22, 1927,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,35</i>)

>  <b>POWDRELL</b> Herd-testing. - it is strongly maintained that herd-testing - the periodical recording of the weight of each cow's produce together with a butter-fat test from which data the production of the cow for the period is calculated - is the surest way of distinguishing the drones ... <i>(1920,Dairy Farming in NZ,46</i>)

>  As the result of herd testing, greater attention to culling and breeding and improved farm management, the average butterfat yield per cow increased from 154.25 lbs. per annum in 1920-21 to 218.80 lbs. per annum in 1929-30. <i>ibid.</i> 345 The major function of Herd Testing is primarily to determine the milk and butterfat producing ability of individual cows, and from this information to secure accurate data for the elimination of low-producing cows and also to provide a more scientific basis for the breeding of dairy stock. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,28</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> A cow with `a good test', that is, milk rich in butterfat, may be more valuable than another cow that gives more milk. About 1910 the practice rose of smpling milk from all the cows in a herd individually and testing the milk for its fat content. This process is called herd-testing. ... it is still not decided whether the word is hyphenated or not. i find it without a hyphen in a publication of the Department of Agriculture in 1950, with a hyphen in a textbook of dairying in 1955. Pronunciation is with one stress, on herd. <i>ibid.</i> 149 in New Zealand, everyone uses the term herd-testing, not milk-testing, because everyone thinks of the dairy industry from a producer's, not a consumer's, point of view. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,45</i>)

>  Herd testing helps farmers to maintain the standard of their herds - it shows up the lower producers so that they can be culled from the herd. <i>(1974,NZ Journal of Agriculture,92</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the testing of dairy herds to assess quality and quantity of milk in each cow.

>  The herd-testing association means more money and better homes for dairymen. <i>(Dec 11, 1920,Hawera Star,10</i>)

>  it is satisfactory to know that farmers are taking a great interest in herd-testing as a means for increasing the yield of butterfat. <i>(Oct 1, 1926,NZ Farmer,1335</i>)

>  Herd-testing in New Zealand since the close of the Great War has been increasingly very rapidly year by year, and it would seem that a stage has now been reached when a steadying-up in the progress of the movement is likely to be evidenced. <i>(Oct 20, 1926,NZ Journal of Agriculture,217</i>)

>  Dairy insight will want at least $10, AB, herd-testing, shed power, shed expenses and animal health will take out the best part of $100 [per cow] ... [herdtesting] <i>(May 2, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,18</i>)

>  A striking case of the value of testing the individual members of a herd is reported from a district where a herd-testing association has been formed. <i>(Jul 1911,NZ Journal of Agriculture,26</i>)

### herdal
 
 <i>adj.</i> herdlike

>  The other night i overheard someone referring disparagingly to humans as "herdal" creatures. i pricked up my ears, to check i hadn't heard a misplaced description of humans as "herbal" creatures because we eat so much stuff classified as herbal. But no, "herdal" was the word, intended to specify our inclination to follow the herd instinct instead of thinking for ourselves. <i>(Feb 18, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### Here Come Two Nuns
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> There are games recorded in this period for which there is no record before 1900 though they may well have existed under other names. West Coast: Dunk and Davey, Spang Weazling; Wellington: Who Goes Round My Stone Wall, Beg o' My Neighbor, Bellahonie, Run a Mile, On and Off the Bridge, Here Come Two Nuns, Colours, isaac and Rebecca. Scrag, Diabolo, Bumble Puppy, Homai, Fives; Auckland: My Fair Young Lady, Rushes and Reeds, Surrender the Tower, Walls, Ball Hopscotch, ball games, Solomon Says; Hawkes Bay: Wolf Wolf Go Home, Homai; Canterbury: Poor Old Tom, Rats and Rabbits and Rips, Steps, Skin the Snake, Steal a Peg, Toodle-em-Buck, The Gag; Golden Bay: Who's That Walking Round My House Tonight, i've Come to See Poor Mary Jane, Steps and Stairs, Creeping, Branding, O'Leary; Otago: Sir Roger de Coverley, Who's That Knocking at My Door. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,231</i>)

### here's a go
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>McGiLL</b> here's a go comment on anticipated happening; eg a run-out in cricket. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,56</i>)

### here-firster
 
 <i>n.</i> original settler

>  More rights implies they either have earned that right by hard work, cherishing the environment and New Zealand as an ideal place and community to live, or just by their birthright as the "here-firsters". <i>(Sep 2003,North & South,14</i>)

 <i>n.</i> spelling of harakeke

### herekopere.
 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

### Hermann
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Mick ... abused them [the Canadian platoon ] ... for firing their bren at nothing in particular. Silly sods said Evan, that sort of thing gives us a bad name with the Hermanns. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,56</i>)

### hermit
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> A sheep which has been badly tutued and recovers, loses its gregarious habits, and becomes what the shepherds call a `hermit'. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,212</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> These were at once bled, and so far recovered that they would eat and even fatten, but they lost their gregarious instinct, and became what they called hermits. The hermits were kept in a special paddock, and supplied the station mutton. <i>(1925,Random Recollections,20</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  Hermit. A single sheep which for some reason takes to living by himself, away from the mob. A hatter sheep, in fact. (C.) <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The,17</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  hermit n. sheep, poisoned by tutu shrub, which then wanders on its own. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  <b>LETHBRiDGE</b> A hermit is a sheep which had missed the muster for several years and has staked out a claim for himself, usually on a rather inaccessible piece of land, and there stayed put. <i>(1971,Sunrise on the Hills,95</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Up in the mountains, young Roy Mytton so proudly succeeded in rounding up twenty-odd old hermits, defiant woolly sheep also known as `double deckers', which had been wily enough to dodge last season's muster. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,80</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> usu. hermit sheep

>  <b>GLEN</b> Sometimes they brought in a `hermit' sheep which lived by itself and had been overlooked in the last muster. <i>(1917,Six Little New Zealanders,70,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MiLLS</b> ... a famous hermit sheep was successful in eluding all mustering activities for many years ... <i>(1960,Sheep-O,124</i>)

>  <b>SEVERiNSEN</b> it's mother, a double-decker fleeced hermit ewe, picked its way through the bush. <i>(1970,Hunt the Far Mountain,25</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high country sheep that grazes alone and always evades a muster, or one that lost its mother when young and becomes attached to a particular location.

>  <b>STRONACH</b> My share was to go up on to the bluffs by the lake, chase one old hermit sheep to his death over a cliff, kill one kea with my stick and go back to camp. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,28</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Lambs which lose their mothers when they are young sometimes form an attachment for an inanimate object like a bush or rock and become what are known as 'hermits' sometimes called 'placer sheep.' <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,110</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Hermit: a sheep that has lost its gregarious nature and lives on its own. Quite often it was a motherless lamb which adopted a rock or a tree in loco parentis. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,41</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> i slipped a heading dog out and he was soon in the lead but the hermit, being completely blind with wool and piripiri seed, ran over the dog and the mob followed. [hermit sheep] <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,61</i>)

>  <b>SiNCLAiR</b> As Boy headed, the hermit shot straight down the slide to the creek. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,57</i>)

>  <b>MCGiLL</b> hermit: a sheep which has lost interest in being with the flock, as a result of being 'tooted,' eating the poisonous tutu plant. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,44</i>)

### hermit sheep
 
 <i>n.</i> a sheep which has escaped and lived apart from the main flock (See [hermit](../H#hermit))

>  it's not enough to have had one Shrek - organisers of next month's national agricultural expo at Mystery Creek near Hamilton have rounded up a couple more hermit sheep. <i>(May 27, 2004,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

### Hero
 
 <i>n.</i> short for Hero Parade

>  "The end plan is the Hero of old without the Hero of old's difficulties, which were essentially involved with money." <i>(Mar 10, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)

### Hero Parade
 
 <i>n.</i> the annual Auckland gay parade and festival

>  "The bloody hero Parade. God. i get furious about it. There are two words i can never use again : gay and hero." <i>(Mar 22, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  When your only role models wear skirts and play netball is it any wonder that the Hero Parade is Auckland's social event of the year. <i>(Apr 15, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A9</i>)

>  it is one thing for her to solicit votes at the Hero Parade. <i>(Apr 1998,North & South,29</i>)

>  And then there's the Hero Parade: "that great new gay tribe defying social and sexual conventions". <i>(Jan 21, 2001,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

>  it is a tribute to Auckland that gays and lesbians can now, mostly, get on with their lives and that when they celebrate by staging the Hero Parade, thousands of straights are happy to cheer them on. <i>(Dec 1999,North & South,107</i>)

>  Things were looking pretty sick for the annual showcase of Auckland's gay community - the Hero Parade - last year. <i>(Mar 10, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C10</i>)

>  They were in their own unquiet way the first walking, talking Hero Parade who every inch of the way, seemed so terribly glad to be gay. <i>(May 8, 2001,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  When your only role models wear skirts and play netball is it any wonder that the Hero Parade is Auckland's social event of the year. <i>(Apr 15, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A9</i>)

>  His progress is not helped by the Hero Parade traffic. <i>(Mar 10, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  A couple of guys decide to make their own TV documentary. They have the event - the Hero parade. <i>(May 5, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),77</i>)

>  According to conventional wisdom, the Kiwi male is either an emotional tomb or a Queen St cavorter - an unfeeling brute or a Hero Parade marching boy. <i>(May 19, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

### Heroic Gardens
 
 <i>n.</i> short for Heroic Gardens (or Garden) Festival (see citations)

>  Alex and Grahame's garden will be familiar to visitors to the annual Heroic Garden Festival, held in Auckland each February. <i>(Feb 2007,NZ Gardener,50</i>)

>  Sat 7 March .. <b>Heroic Gardens</b> The Heroic Gardens Festival, which was co-founded by The Fifth Season and gaba and is a stalwart fundraiser for causes relevant to our community. .. Check out heroicgardens.org.nz <i>(Jan 2009,Express (Gay & Lesbian Express),5</i>)

>  <i>Heroic Gardens</i> .. The Heroic Gardens Festival is the closing event of Pride '09, so come and enjoy and unwind in these beautiful and tranquil places after a hectic month of fabulousity! [sic] <i>(Jan 2009,Express (Gay & Lesbian Express),17</i>)

>  GAY AUCKLAND BUSiNESS ASSOCiATiON FiNANCiAL STATEMENTS .. Heroic Gardens is run by a Partnership of Fifth Season inc. and GABA inc. in 2008 $28,500 was raised and donated equally between the Positive Women, Body Positive and Cartier Bereavement Charitabel Trust, (2007 $40,000) <i>(Mar 31, 2008,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.gaba.org.nz/images/store1351/File/GABA%202008%20Accounts%20signed.pdf]

>  The first Heroic Gardens Festival took two years to come to fruition, finally taking place in 1997. <i>(2000,Heroic Gardens,7</i>)

>  With Heroic Gardens held in February, the hottest month of the year, Chris jokes that the water bill afterwards was enough to induce heart failure ... <i>(2000,Heroic Gardens,45</i>)

>  Soon after the new landscaping was completed, the courtyard was used as the setting for an outdoor summer performance by Auckland's Gay and Lesbian Singers (GALS) and a 'dynamic duo' of opera singers, Helen Medlyn and Sue Boland. <i>(2000,Heroic Gardens,29</i>)

>  A flowering <i>Vriesea hieroglyphica</i> competed for attention with balls of red and yellow gerberas during the 2006 Heroic Garden Festival. <i>(Feb 2007,NZ Gardener,49</i>)

>  The seed of the Heroic Gardens Festival took root back in 1995, when a group met to discuss the possibility of a gay garden festival. They envisaged the event being part of the Hero festivities, with the proceeds going to Herne Bay House. This was an Auckland City Mission residential service, offering quality care and support for those living with HiV and Aids. <i>(Feb 2008,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: Ponsonby News p. 49 see http://www.ponsonbynews.co.nz/files/february_2008.pdf]

>  The 9th annual <i>Heroic Garden Festival</i> was held on the weekend of February 19th and 20th. <i>(Mar 2005,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: p 2 of Board Member's [sic] Report. pdf. http://0-www.aucklandcity.govt.nz.www.elgar.govt.nz/council/members/boardmeetings/western/20050316_1830/west-1603-opn-%2316ii.pdf]

>  MERCEDES BENZ DESiGN MARQUEE .. <b>Bronze</b> Heroic Garden Festival, design by Fiona George, Auckland Lincoln University ... <i>(2003,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.gaarden-nz/latest-news/ellerslie-flower-show-2003/awards.html]

>  Co-founded by The Fifth Season and GABA, Heroic Gardens was started as a fund-raiser for Auckland City Mission's Herne Bay House, a respite and treatment centre for people living with HiV and AiDS. The festival has become an annual event ever since. Over the last eleven years Heroic Gardens Festival has given over $415,000 to two charities who assist those living with or affected by HiV. <i>(2008,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://heroicgardens.org.nz/]

>  First held in 1997, Heroic Gardens is a yearly event which has become a much enjoyed part of the Auckland summer calendar. it appeals to a broad spectrum of the community and provides an opportunity to see a diverse range of gardens and to meet the garden owners. <i>(2008,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.heroicgardens.org.nz/]

>  We are delighted to announce that hospice will be the beneficiary charity for Heroic Gardens 2009. <i>(2008,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.heroicgardens.org.nz/]

### heron
 
 <i>n.</i> [Spec. use of heron for any of several large wading birds with long necks and legs, of the fam. Ardeidae.] (See [also crane; kotuku](../A#also-crane;-kotuku),[matuku(-moana)](../M#matuku(-moana)))

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We saw two shags & white bird which removed from tree to tree ... it seemed to be a Heron. [ed. fn] White Heron ... <i>(Mar 29, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/243</i>)

>  A small slate-colored [sic] Heron (<i>Ardea Matook</i>) inhabits our coasts ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/16</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  Bird enthusiasts are in for a treat with a good chance of seeing .. spoonbill, herons and the rare brown teal in their natural habitat at the Okupe Lagoon ... <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 7</i>)

### heron of one flight
 
 <i>phr.</i> [cf. Maori description of kotuku as 'bird of a single flight' (fig. use of that here evidently)]

>  The Papal Legate.. was described as "the heron of one flight." <i>(Feb 10, 1940,Otago Daily Times,16</i>)

### herpolirion
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Herpolirion novae-zelandiae.</i>

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> (And) <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>)

### herring
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [Picton herring](../P#picton-herring),[aua](../A#aua),[and sardine](../A#and-sardine))

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Herring (<i> Clupea harengus </i>). To the popular mind the introduction of the herring into New Zealand waters is the most desirable form of acclimatisation work which could be undertaken.] <i>(1922,Naturalisation of Animals and Plants,185</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Agonostoma forsteri</i> (cf Picton herring), aua, sea mullet, yellow-eyed mullet, awa, tawa tawa; a popular name for the aua or the yellow-eyed mullet in NZ.

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The bream, ling, gurnard, herring, sole, rock-cod, and a species of mackarel, are all in abundance, as are also many fish unknown elsewhere [than Wellington]. <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),49</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> There are also several other kinds of fish, which are called by the sailors, from some slight resemblance to the English sorts, <i>Herrings, Horse-mackarel, Cole-fish</i>, and <i>Ling</i>. The latter is often taken of the weight of forty pounds. <i>ibid.</i> 212 Fish of all kinds ... abound off the mouth of the [Wanganui] river; and the <i>kawai</i>, as well as smelts, and the <i>aua</i>, or so-called herring, ascend the river ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,161</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> There are also several other kinds of fish, which are called by the sailors, from some slight resemblance to the English sorts, <i>Herrings, Horse-mackarel, Cole-fish</i>, and <i>Ling</i>. The latter is often taken of the weight of forty pounds. <i>ibid.</i> 212 Fish of all kinds ... abound off the mouth of the [Wanganui] river; and the <i>kawai</i>, as well as smelts, and the <i>aua</i>, or so-called herring, ascend the river ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,161</i>)

>  <b>Vol. iX: 125</b> English Name Native Name .. ... Herring Tawa tawa ... <i>(1849,NZ Journal</i>) [NOTES: (quoting Anglo Maori Warder Oct 5)]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The Fam. <i>Clupeadicae</i> is represented by the Aua, herring, which plentiful in all the tidal rivers, and may be taken to any extent. <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,624</i>)

>  <b>ASKEW</b> The `herring,' for shilling, is doubtless derived, like many other of their slang words, from the sailors, and others settled in the [347] island from the metropolis. <i>(1857,Voyage to Australia & NZ,346</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON & HECTOR</b> ... the Aua (sea mullet) <i>(1872,Fishes NZ,114</i>)

>  <b>TiNNE</b> ... my Maori landlord ... charged me only `five heringi' (shillings) for my tea, bed, and breakfast ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,23</i>)

>  [Native or Common Name] Aua, Mullet, Herring .. [Scientific Name] <i>Agonostoma forsteri</i> <i>(1877,TrNZi,9/485</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  [Maori or Settlers' Name] Herrings, Makawhiti .. [Scientific Name] <i>Agonostoma forsteri</i> <i>(1878,TrNZi,10/326</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  [Native or Settlers' Name] Makuwhiti, Mullet, or Herring .. [Scientific Name] <i>Agonostoma forsteri</i> <i>(1879,TrNZi,11/382</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>HAY</b> We have, indeed, a wealth of fish in all the tidal waters. Sharks, schnapper, rock-cod, mackerel, mullet, herring, sole, halibut, albacore, barracouta, king-fish, and others. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/230</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND & HUTTON</b> The warehou, tarakihi, and gurnet are common in the north, and the moki, butterfish, and frost fish in the south, while the kahawai, hapuku (the groper of Dunedin), blue cod, barracouta, the little sea mullet, sometimes called the herring, and the flat fish are found all round the coast. <i>(1902,Nature in NZ,71</i>)

>  <i>Agonostoma forsteri</i> Bloch. The sea-mullet or herring is one of the commonest fishes in the harbour, <i>(1906,TrNZi,38/551</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Aua, ... Agonostoma forsteri; herring. ... <i>ibid.</i> 121 <i> Kataha, n. Agonostoma forsteri, herring. = aua. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,25</i>)

>  AGONOSTOMUS FORSTERi (Bloch and Schneider). <i>Common Mullet; Aua</i>. Common in the estuaries of all tidal rivers, often entering fresh water in the summer. Called `herring' at Golden Bay and on the west coast of the South island, but not identical with Picton herring. ... Distribution: New Zealand harbours and estuaries. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,iV/120</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  it must also be mentioned that in several of the references to herring in New Zealand waters, the common sea-mullet, <i>Agonostoma forsteri</i> , is the fish intended... <i>(1926,TrNZi,56/315</i>) [NOTES: (Young and Thomson)]

>  <b>SPEiGHT</b> Mugiloidea. An intermediate stage in the forward movement of the pelvic fins is represented in the little sea-mullet (<i>Agonostoma forsteri</i> (Plate i. fig. 2)), popularly known as `herring.' it is a shore and estuarine form and feeds on the small shrimps which come in with the tide. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,198</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 198]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Agonostoma forsteri</i> (Cuv. & Val.). YELLOW-EYED MULLET, HERRiNG; AWA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,30,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Agonostoma forsteri</i> : Yellow-eyed mullet, South island mullet, `herring.' <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <b>MANNERiNG</b> ... small herring (or what we in New Zealand call a `herring') <i>(1943,Eighty Years,157</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> YELLOW-EYED MULLET (<i>Agonostoma forsteri</i> ) Awa of the Maoris, is usually called the Herring, but it is not related to the English fish of that name. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,67</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> Yellow-Eyed Mullet <i>Agonostomus forsteri </i> Skipper; Herring; Sea Mullet <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,170</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Aua ...Agonostomus forsteri (Aldrichetta)</i>, yellow-eyed mullet, so-called herring. <i>ibid.</i> 102 <i>ataha, ... Agonostomus forsteri, herring,. = aua. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,21</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> YELLOWEYE MULLET ...<i> Other names: Aldrichetta forsteri</i>; herring, common mullet, sprat; sea mullet, river mullet (Australia); aua (Maori). The name herring in widely used in New Zealand for the yelloweye mullet but the true herring is a very different kind of fish. There are two species of true herring in our waters. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,220</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> YELLOWEYE MULLET [<i>Aldrichetta forsteri</i>] ... Other names are herring, common mullet, and sprat. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,48</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>MULLET, YELLOW-EYED</i> (a) Scientific name: <i>Aldrichetta forsteri</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Sea Mullet; New Zealand Kipper (smoked); Rollmops (pickled). (c) Other common names: Herring (in error and best dropped); Common Mullet; Sprat (in error). (d) Principal localities where taken: Tauranga, Kaipara, Lake Ellesmere. ... The name `Herring' has been applied widely and for a long time to the New Zealand Yelloweyed Mullet which is not a Clupeid and therefore only very distantly related to the true Herring of European waters. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,39</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> The Maoris knew to a nicety when to expect the Aua, or herring, or to be correct, the yellow eyed mullet ... <i>(1970,Way Up North,24</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> HERRiNG More often than not a Yelloweye Mullet, but the fish that is widely known as the `herring' in our coastal waters. Also known as `sprat', though this term also covers the young of several species including anchovy, yelloweye mullet and several other small fish. There are only two, `true' herrings in our waters. <i>ibid.</i>164 The Yellow-Eye has a much narrower head than the Grey and is the ubiquitous, but misnamed `sprat' or `herring' of our wharves. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,120</i>)

>  [species name] herring .. [scientific name] -- [other name(s)] This may be SPRAT [<i>Sprattus antipodum</i>] or YELLOW-EYED MULLET [<i>Aldrichetta forsteri</i>]; Hastings region = Yellow-eyed mullet.... [species name] sprat .. [scientific name] <i>Sprattus antipodum</i> .. [other name(s)] sardine, herring <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Yellow-eyed mullet Aldrichetta forsteri</i> ... Often incorrectly called herring or sprat in New Zealand. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,113</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] herring 509-38, 594, 1635-59 HERRiNGS 502-42 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>COWLEY</b> A rush of herring sprayed away from the boat and plopped some distance away, making circles on the dark green water. <i>(1991,Bow Down Shadrach,23</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an anglicisation of a Maori rendering (hereni [hereni]) of Eng. `shilling,' with the -ing from Eng. shilling, the whole approximated to the Eng. word herring also herren. [(obs.) (A shilling seemed to be a standard Maori charge for work done or goods sold to the early settlers; but cf money gold.)]

>  (Jas Harper, Nelson) The [natives] ask one herring ... that is one shilling <i>ibid.</i> 154 (Gillingham) ... a herring (or shilling) <i>(1843,Letters from Settlers,81</i>) [NOTES: (1842) 1843 Letters from Settlers 81]

>  <b>HODDER</b> The chief wanted five herren (shillings) ... <i>(1862,Memories of NZ Life,68</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> ...' hereni' shilling ... herring is the ... white man's corruption. <i>(1866,Twenty-Five Years in NZ,11</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>10/327</b> The herring (<i> Clupea sagax </i>) was brought to market in good quantity. <i>(1878,TrNZi</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  The Herring,Clupea sagax, did not turn up during the past summer as usual.The Kingfish, Seriola lalandii, also put in no appearance this year. <i>(1879,TrNZi,11/381</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> anchovy.

>  <b>SORENSON</b> ANCHOVY (a) Scientific name: <i>Engraulis australis</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Anchovy. (c) Other common names: the following names of related Clupeids and Engraulid fish are sometimes applied to anchovy - Herring, Sprat, Sardine, Pilchard, although the true Herring of European waters (<i>Clupea harengus</i>), does not occur in New Zealand. (d) Principal localities where taken: Hauraki Gulf, Gisborne, Tasman Bay, Marlborough Sounds. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,10</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> pilchard, sardine (also New Zealand herring)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> PiLCHARD (a) Scientific name: <i>Sardinops neopilchardus.</i> (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Pilchard; Sardine. (c) Other common names: Sardine; Sprat; Anchovy; Herring. (d) Principal localities where taken: Auckland, Gisborne, Tasman Bay, Marlborough Sounds. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,40</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> With regard to its popular names, [the New Zealand sprat] has frequently been referred to as the Pilchard, New Zealand Herring, and by other local names. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,28</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> With regard to its popular names, [the New Zealand sprat] has frequently been referred to as the Pilchard, New Zealand Herring, and by other local names. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,28</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> grayling, upokororo

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> Finished making a kupanga, or net, which is about fifty feet by four. ... took a draught of about fifty good-sized fish with it, called the upokororo, or fresh-water herring. [fn] upokororo, grayling. <i>(1847,Great Journey,25,1952 ed.</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Sardinops neopilchardus.</i>

>  <b>PARROTT</b> With regard to its popular names, [the New Zealand sprat] has frequently been referred to as the Pilchard, New Zealand Herring, and by other local names. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,28</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> herring [(?An error by Hector repeated by Sherrin.)]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> 58. SEA MULLET. ... The Makawhiti or Aua of the Maoris (<i>Agonostoma Forsteri</i>) ... is a common fish ... it is commonly called Herring, from its general resemblance in size and form to that fish, but is easily recognisable from the true Herring by having two fins on the back, ... The Picton Herring, a dried fish commonly known roughout the colony, is the Aua preserved by smoking. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,114</i>)

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> MAKAWHiTi (<i>Agonostoma Forsteri</i>). ... Called the sea-mullet in other places, but the makawhiti or aua by the Maoris. it is a common fish, Dr. Hector says, obtained at all seasons of the year by fishing from the wharves in the harbours. in Dunedin it is known as the mullet. ... The Picton herring, a dried fish, commonly known throughout the colony, is the aua, preserved by smoking. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,65</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> Picton Herring, n. a name for several fishes when dried (like `kipper'), especially for the Sea Mullet, or Makawhitior Aua(q.v.) (Maori names'; and for the New South Wales fish called Maray(q.v.) <i>(1898,Austral English,351</i>)

>  in Queen Charlotte Sound this fish is known as the `Picton Herring', and is cured, ... <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/137</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  SARDiNA NEOPiLCHARDUS (Steindachner). <i>Pilchard; Mohimohi </i>. Sold in a smoked condition in Wellington as `Picton herring'. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts (unknown, Bay of islands). <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,iV/118</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  During the month of July, 1924, Wellington harbour was visited by an enormous shoal of pilchards, or sardines (<i>Sardinia neopilchardus</i>). ... This fish is identical with the well-known `Picton herring', which is sold in a smoked condition every winter in Wellington. <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/191</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Picton herring .. .. mohimohi .. ..<i>Sardinia neopilchardus</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  ~ n. New Zealand dried fish [Place name]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),622</i>)

>  <i>Picton herring</i> n. N.Z. dried fish [From place name]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> The Fisheries (General) Regulations 1950 (Reprint) defines `Sardine' as the fish with the scientific name <i>Sardinia neopilchardus</i>, also known as the Picton Herring. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,10</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] herring, Picton 528, 532, 539, 1641 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> pilchard, formerly smoked and cured.

>  <i>Sardinia neopilcharda</i>: Sardine, pilchard, or `Picton herring.' <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,71</i>)

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> in the case of Queen Charlotte Sound it has been suggested that the amount of decayed seaweed and black floating ooze is related to the non-appearance of the pilchard [<i>Sardinops neopilcharda</i> (Steindachner)], or Picton herring as it is there named. <i>(1940,Fishes NZ,7</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> PiLCHARD (MOHiMOHi) <i>Sardinops neopilchardus</i> (Steindacher) The Pilchard is known from the northern end of the South island as the Picton Herring, and off Picton large shoals of these fish have from time to time been recorded. it has no relation to the so-called New Zealand Herring or Yellow-eyed Mullet (<i>Agonostomus forsteri</i>). <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,104</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> Published records show that [Pilchards] have appeared in huge shoals in Queen Charlotte Sound, where they are also known as `Picton Herrings'. <i>ibid.</i> 169 Pilchard <i>Sardinops neopilchardus</i> Sardine; Picton Herring <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,31</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Pilchard Sardinops neopilchardus</i> ... Sometimes called the sardine. Also occurs around southern Australia. ... in the 1880s a fishery in the Marlborough Sounds produced salted and smoked `Picton Herring', and small fisheries have developed briefly elsewhere. ... Pilchard are caught for groper bait (`bloater'), and have been used also for tuna. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,40</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Emmelichthys nitidus</i>, red baitfish.

>  EMMELiCHTHYS NiTiDUS (Richardson). <i>Red bait-fish</i>. Rare, and only occasionally utilized as a food fish. Taken in seine among mackerel, and commonly known as `red herring'. Distribution: i have recorded specimens only from the northern coasts of the South island. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,iV/115</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>WiLSON</b> BAiTFiSH, RED <i>Emmelichthys nitidus</i>: A less common species in New Zealand waters this fish turns up unpredictably in vast schools that give the water a red appearance - and the fish its name. Grow up to about 16 inches and known also as picarel, pearl fish and red herring. Colours light pink to dark red. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,35</i>)

>  [index to common names 283-293] herring, red 1446 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### herringbone
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [some noun citations in set below (and the rest are attrib.?)]

>  Senior members depart for that Great Herringbone Shed in the Sky. <i>(Feb 15, 1992,Dominion,10</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> Peter built a herring bone cowbail at Otara... He converted the old cowbail into a herring bone shed.<i>ibid.</i> 180 First they had to rebuild the cowbail.. and then.. they altered it into a herring-bone. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,26</i>)

>  i was called in here to give advice on Herringbone Sheds. <i>(Sep 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,66</i>)

>  Though not an entirely satisfactory substitute for a sight-glass, a length of clear plastic pipe.. is of considerable help in herringbone sheds. <i>(Feb 1960,NZ Dairy Exporter,53</i>)

>  Most of these articles have been concerned with the characteristics of the [rotary] shed in comparison with the herringbone shed. <i>(1972,NZ Dairy Exporter,2</i>)

>  <b>McMEEKAN</b> The herringbone is a New Zealand development of the tandem type... in this type the cows are angle-parked in two rows separated by the milker's "well" which is 30 inches below the cow platforms... The cows are milked in batches, those on each side being milked alternately. <i>(1960,Grass to Milk,150</i>)

>  it's a reference to the communicating and preferably concrete causeways by which stock move from paddocks to herringbone. <i>(Mar 28, 1992,Dominion,10</i>)

>  We have yet to meet a farmer who hasn't found the herringbone shed a big saver of work and time. <i>(Apr 13, 1966,Weekly News,44</i>)

>  `Herringbone' dairies, developed in New Zealand, are increasing rapidly in number. <i>(1974,NZ Journal of Agriculture,81</i>)

>  i had long heard of the herring-bone milking shed, but the rotaflow system.. was a new development. <i>(May 8, 1993,Dominion,17</i>)

>  [Hdg] Take your partners for the herringbone waltz... Smith, weaving back and forth along the pit, does the ritual dance they call the herringbone waltz. The trick is to make sure that the only substance you ever get splattered with is milk. <i>(May 29, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),25</i>)

>  Although John's shed is old and dingy, he tells me that when it was converted from a walk-through to a herringbone in 1966, it was one of the biggest and smartest around... <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/26</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a design of milking shed in which the milker works centrally between two queues of cows.

>  Mr Greg Aldridge said that stepping from walk-throughs to herringbones had made the difference for members of the group .. A rotary would probably cost from $2500 to $3000 more than a herringbone. <i>(Jul 1971,NZ Dairy Exporter,9</i>)

>  10,972 are highline herringbones. 775 are lowline herringbones. <i>(Jun 1977,NZ Dairy Exporter,16</i>)

>  <b>DAViS</b> .. before long almost every new shed was a herringbone. The herringbone became the national shed just as the walkthrough had been. <i>(1994,Lab Coats to Gumboots,62</i>)

>  <b>MCMEEKAN</b> The herringbone is a New Zealand development of the tandem type - a good example of the farming ingenuity to which i have so often referred. <i>(1960,Grass to Milk,153</i>)

>  <b>WASWO</b> As a group Dairy Board consulting officers were the ones mainly responsible for encouraging people into herringbones and it had become clear the system offered opportunity for higher throughputs than the standard walk-throughs. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,89</i>)

### herringbone (milking) shed
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also herringbone](../A#also-herringbone))

>  But the consequent increase in cow numbers would bring another problem - the farms' [<i>sic</i>] 40-bail herringbone milking shed would not be big enough to cope. <i>(Dec 8, 2005,Dominion Post,C9</i>)

>  <b>McMEEKAN</b> There are three commonly used types of milking shed in New Zealand - the walk-through, the tandem, and the herringbone. <i>ibid.</i>150 The herringbone is a New Zealand development of the tandem type ... . in this type of shed the cows are angle-parked in two rows separated by the milkers `well' which is 30 inches below the cow platforms. <i>(1961,Grass to Milk,148</i>)

>  Now he looks as much at home coaxing reluctant cows through a herringbone milking shed as he must have been in a Dutch lecture theatre. <i>(Jul 23, 1972,Sunday Times (NZ),9/3</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> i think they may help you with some machinery; talk of putting in a herring-bone milking shed; make things a bit easier. HiNGAWARU: Ana! [Yes!] Herring-bone! <i>(1974,Hand on the Rail, The,42</i>)

>  <b>HiLL</b> There may be a mystery bus trip to Peter Longley's new herringbone shed ... <i>ibid.</i> 37 At herringbone shed. 150 Jersey ... waiting patiently by paddock gate. ... 600 hooves pass placidly through herringbone ... <i>(1988,More from Moaville,12</i>)

>  He built a 40-a-side herring-bone milking shed... <i>(Jul 22, 2004,Dominion Post,C8</i>)

### herringbone cowshed
 
 <i>n.</i> a shed in which cows are milked in two rows standing at an angle to the milker who is in a pit at udder level.

>  Herringbone cowshed. Cows are milked in two rows standing at an angle to the milker who is in a pit at udder level. Cup put on from the side. All the cows enter and leave together. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,39</i>)

### herringbone waltz
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the movement required of a milker when removing cups from a cow on one side of a cowshed to place on a cow on the opposite side.

>  <b>YEREX</b> The process of removing cups from cows on one side of the pit and transferring them to those on the orher side was developed to the status of an art form known as 'the herringbone waltz.' <i>(1989,Empire of the Dairy Farmers,116</i>)

### herringboner
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dairy farmer or worker who is an advocate of herringbone milking sheds.

>  The 'Exporter' visited some self-confessed 'dyed-in-the-wool' herringboners, members of the Pihautea Discussion group... <i>(Jul 1971,NZ Dairy Exporter,9</i>)

>  .. i love webfoot for a Taranaki farmer, who is also known as a gumbooter or herringboner. <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion,C6</i>)
