### Hickety-Pips
 
 <i>n.</i> the 65th Regiment; the Royal Tigers. [various spellings]

>  His father was a sergeant in the 65th Regiment - the old "Hiketi-Pift," as the Maoris called them. <i>(Dec 31, 1910,NZ Free Lance,5</i>)

>  Dr. Neill will be remembered.. as the well-known staff-surgeon of the "Tigers" of "Hickety-fifth" as their Maori foes were accustomed to call them. <i>(Apr 23, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

>  <b>Grace</b> The Maori replied, `Kapai te Hickety Pipth' (65th). `Good, it is the sixty-fifth regiment.' <i>(1899,Sketch of the NZ War,95</i>)

>  [Headline] Longest military occupation in N.Z.'s history ending. The ghosts of the `Royal Tigers' or `Hickety-pips' will soon be left on their own on Wellington's Mt. Cook as the last soldiers leave the disbanded Home Command HQ at Buckle St. next month. ... they acquired another nick-name, the `Hicket-pips, from the Maori pronunciation of 65th as `hikitipipi.' <i>(Mar 29, 1979,Evening Post,23/3-4</i>)

>  ... joined [in Wellington] about 1846 by the 65th ... . They got their nickname of Hickety-pips from the nearest the Maoris could approximate the name of their regiment - `hikitippi.' <i>(Oct 14, 1981,Evening Post,22</i>)

>  <b>BARBER</b> Called the "Hickety Pip" by their Maori opponents, troops from this regiment [the 65th] were often personally known to their rebel opponents, and sometimes accommodations were made between sentries to allow a maximum of unbroken sleep, with a guarantee of prior warning in the case of attack. <i>(1984,Red Coat to Jungle Green,31</i>)

### hickey
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> Hickey (or Hike). [P8 omits hike] Often elaborated to do-hickey. A `thingummy': New Zealand: late C. 19-20. Perhaps ex a Maori word. B., 1941. [P8: Perhaps influenced by a Maori word, but the form do-hickey has long been common in the mid-west US.] <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1075</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> electricians' tool

>  Used by an electrician on discovering in an old ceiling a smooth piece of 4x2 with a transverse hole in it 2-3' from one end coming out 3-4' down from end on the other side. it was a piece of wood, 3-4 ft long, used to bend the now obsolete iron conduit once used for internal electric wiring. used ca1926 - 1970? [?from hiko Maori electricity; ? hiki M stick <i>(Mar 17, 1982,Personal Communication</i>)

### hickory
 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand hickory, <i>Phyllocladus alpinus.</i>

>  <b>KiRK</b> (And) <i>Phyllocladus alpinus</i> New Zealand hickory <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>)

### hide
 
 <i>n.</i> impudence, cheek; a thick skin [ca1940 W.H.B. Orsman (Havelock)]

>  hide insensitive nature or impudent manner, in phrs `you have a thick hide' or `you've got a hide. Mostly NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,56</i>)

### hidy-go-seek
 
 <i>n.</i> an occas. var. of hide-and-go-seek, the children's game.

>  <b>PEACOCKE</b> ... let's play `hidy-go-seek' <i>(1915,My Friend Phil,37</i>)

### hieke
 
 <i>n.</i> cloak made of flax

>  in return, a gift of a hieke, a type of cloak made entirely from flax, was presented to Ngati Awa. <i>(Nov 16, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,9</i>)

### hieracium
 
 <i>n.</i> OED : hieracium - A large genus of Composite plants, mostly with yellow flowers; called in Eng.,Hawkweed.

>  in more recent years <i>Hieracium</i> species - various dandelion-like hawkweeds - have been a major problem. <i>(Aug 2002,Forest & Bird,305/26</i>)

>  The Mackenzie Country was once best known as the home of rabbits, hieracium, hydro canals and the way to Queenstown. <i>(Jul 12, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  The hillsides here were green-tinged yellow, with just enough rain in spring to boost grass growth and give a blue tinge to the spreading hieracium. <i>(Nov 15, 1999,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  Those of us younger generation types who sneer at the old fogies today will be there ourselves in a few years' time, railing from our gigantic age bubble about pretty much the same stuff. Okay, to them the dreaded weed is hieracium, whereas to us it will be marijuana. <i>(Jul 31, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  We wanted them to address issues like heiracium and rabbits and prove they can look after the land. <i>(Jul 16, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  The hills in this part of Molesworth are covered in briar and heiracium. <i>(Aug 14, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  He arrived before the hieracium, whose coming meant he had to reduce his sheep numbers by a couple of thousand and his cattle by 3000, all feeding on an ever-increasing overdraft. <i>(Mar 1997,Growing Today,49</i>)

>  There cannot be a sheep station or hamlet left untouched by Act evangelists blaming Wellington for everything from the dollar to haeracium infestation of paddocks. <i>(Feb 8, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  The Environmental Risk Management Authority has approved the freeing of three more imported insects species to biologically control the heiracium weed that has taken over areas of farmland on the North and South islands. <i>(Jun 29, 2001,Dominion,18</i>)

### hiff
 
 <i>v.</i> (informal) to throw, toss

>  <b>Geary</b> ...Peter hiffs one of these chains like some rodeo cowboy. Misses by a mile. <i>(2003,Man of the People, A,254</i>)

>  .. smokers driven out on to pub balconies under the new laws would have to "hiff" their butts into the street below. <i>(Dec 8, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Jennifer Green writes that in the 1960s "hiff" was often used when discarding an unwanted item. She even knew of a very large cupboard called The Hiffin, where one hiffed anything that had no other obvious lodging-place. So the magic word "hiff" is not only a South island colloquial verb after all. <i>(Dec 15, 2004,Dominion Post</i>)

### high
 
 <i>adj.</i> as in high country (phs transf. as an illustration there).

>  Near the large lakes, such as Wakatipu, Te Anau, Manapouri, Hauroto, and others, and between these and the west coast, the country becomes very high, often reaching 5,000 ft. and 6,000 ft. above sea-level, ... <i>ibid.</i> 32 ... but owing to the repressive measures adopted there has been a marked abatement of the pest, even on high back-country, ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,31</i>)

### high beat man
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a musterer in a mustering team who works at higher altitudes than other members of the team.

>  <b>MOMATiUK & EASTCOTT</b> Low beat men are calmer. High beat men tend to have a bit of killer instinct, a bit of devil, a bit of hate ... <i>(1980,High Country,74</i>)

### high block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the highest pastoral area on a high country station.

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> The whole high block, known as the Blowholes, was riddled with pot-holes. <i>(1993,Station Country i,45</i>)

### High Candelabras
 
 <i>n.</i> a drinking game

>  Other well-known [drinking] games are Hokonui Swindle, Bottles, Fluffy Ducks, Bunnies are Hovering, Cardinal Huff, All Blacks, Matches, and The Amazing Grimaldi Brothers. All these games involve either a complicated intellectual exercise, tongue twisters, mathematical calculations or aerobatics in a confined space; High Candelabras for example, involves intoxicated individuals attempting to traverse the four walls of a room without touching the floor. <i>(Mar 13, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,16</i>)

### high country
 
 <i>n.</i> high altitude grazing land.

>  New Zealand's high country is legendary in the most literal sense. Myths swirl like mist around its peaks. <i>(Jul 12, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> .. a Scotchman has taken up some high country for sheep ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,138</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A breed now almost extinct, that is peculiar to the high country, is the 'stopping dog' ... <i>(1947,Wayleggo,13</i>)

>  A musterer on high country would not be properly equipped for his work without a good stopping or heading dog. <i>(Dec 20, 1951,NZ Farmer,17 ed.</i>)

>  The high country is a big part of our legend, like Australian outback or the Swiss Alps. <i>(Mar 2, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  Craig Potton restrains himself to two bumper stickers - Save Our High Country and Support Amnesty. <i>(Sep 2000,North & South,105</i>)

>  The OiC calculates that high country land owned by foreigners is 123,000ha. <i>(Oct 9, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [sp. use] [South island ranges] the land on a station block that is high in altitude.

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Squatters whose runs include high country. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,212</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> Every day i walked the ridges and hunted back sheep trying to make their way up to the high country. <i>(1942,Early NZ Families,158</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [sp. use] a remote area of South island high-altitude pastoral leasehold land.

>  The six million hectares of high country comprise 20 per cent of the South island. <i>(Mar 2, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  The high country is the area east of the Southern Alps consisting of properties at high altitude with extensive grazing which are subject to severe weather hazards such as snow loss. <i>(1977,Journal of Tussock Grasslands inst.,36</i>)

>  <b>DOMiNY</b> .. the Grasslands Division of the Department of Scientific and industrial Research (DSiR) defines the South island "high country" in "agricultural terminology" as a remote area of high-altitude pastoral Crown leasehold land occupied by large sheep stations, or runs .. The New Zealand Meat and Wool Board's Economic Service defines the South island high country similarly as "extensive run country" and calculates the carrying capacity in Canterbury, Otago and Marlborough as 0.9 stock units per hectare. Distinguished from the South island high country is South island hill country, characterized by a larger proportion of lowland soil, lower snow risk and less dependence on wool as a source of income ... <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,142</i>)

### High Court
 
 <i>n.</i> formerly Supreme Court, a court of unlimited civil and criminal jurisdiction; see HNZD2.

>  High Court ... 3. (in New Zealand) formerly called: Supreme Court. a court of law that is superior to a District Court. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,528</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 353; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 231]]

>  indeed, they risk losing everything if they persist in ignoring "Pakehatanga", i.e. the High Court. <i>(Apr 22, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),25</i>)

### high lands
 
 <i>n.</i> =high country? (mountain-farmer used shortly after) [also highland adjective (1885 cit.)]

>  <i>Nestor notabilis</i>, Gould. Mountain or Green parrot, Sheep-killer, Kea, Kia, Keha, Keo, Kaieo. - By all these names is this troublesome bird known by the highland shepherds and sheep musterers in alpine districts from Lakes Wanaka and Hawea to the river Rangitata. <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science,52</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  (Dunstan Diggings) The diggings [at Dunstan] extend 80 miles one way and fifteen another. if you want to boil a pot you have to hunt about for dead `Wild irishmen' a nasty prickly little shrub. The high lands abounded with `Spaniards' i suppose from their cut-throat propensities. A kind of very thick grass, grows in a little lump. <i>(Jan 7, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>)

### high school
 
 <i>n.</i> a secondary school

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> At primary school we Campbells were the only pupils to have Polynesian blood, ... At high school i was alone. <i>(1939,island to island,93,1984 ed.</i>)

>  high school ... 2. U.S. & N.Z. a secondary school. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,528</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 353; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 232]]

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> Last winter my sister and i went back for the 75th anniversary reunion at our high school. <i>(1990,Bad Music,53</i>)

### high top
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering and high-country farming] the uppermost grazing land that is used as summer country.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> The machines shear too near the skin and when the almost naked sheep go out into the high tops 'off the shears' the first cold storm ... <i>(1951,NZ High Country,26</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> They live in the lonely back huts while they muster the high tops. <i>(1966,Over the River,132</i>)

>  <b>SiNCLAiR & TROTTER</b> Five of us went away out on to the high tops ... <i>(1994,From Caithness to Southland,16</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> This block, which is fenced off from the high tops, is the station's hogget country. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,158</i>)

>  Snow has again fallen pretty thick on the high tops, and fears are rife that numbers of the sheep shorn during the previous blazing weather have perished. <i>(Jan 14, 1882,Otago Witness,13</i>)

### high-country
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) pertaining to or characteristic of mountainous steep country, mainly near the Southern Alps and Central and Southern South island.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The high-country musterer covers a lot of dangerous country ... <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,94</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. this family must surely have no peers as high-country sheepmen. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South island,204</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> High-country people seem to be invisible to town people, their way of life registering zero on the understanding scale. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,200</i>)

>  Due to a change in farming practice our vendors having purchased this property as a going concern now offer this high country herd. [high country] <i>(May 9, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,29</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> .. most high-country owners try to establish a 'snow fence' ... <i>(1962,Country Occasions,158</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> it took some getting used to these high-country sheep. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,44</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  The high-country tussock grassland of Marlborough has been occupied for pastoral purposes for almost 100 years. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,293</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> The speech of the men is a slow drawl, sometimes flavoured with Scotch burr ... [fn] High country slang on sheep runs has national characteristics such as referring to a man as a `joker', and using the adjective 'crook' to describe bad weather or ill health, eg `i felt crook on the day the crook weather brought the river up'. Regional slang is also of interest, such as use of `dag' (literally wool fouled with sheep's dung) to mean a `hard case' or unusual character, with an implication of approval, even of admiration. `Rooster' is another variant of `joker'. <i>(1952,Land Uplifted High,162,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The Kaweka, built in 1936, was the first of several high-country huts that have been erected by the now famous Heretaunga Tramping Club. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,28</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> The fall muster is one of the big occasions in a high-country year. it is ... one of the few occasions on which New Zealanders use the word `fall' instead of autumn. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,118</i>)

>  The high-country farms are all in the South island. ... The stock on these high-country farms may graze in altitudes as high as about 1500 to 1800 metres, though most of the `runs' utilise areas up to about 900 m only. <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,57</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Though the sunny faces may dry up, the dark faces carry considerable feed, the reverse of usual high-country conditions. <i>(1982,Historic Sheep Stations of NZ,13</i>)

>  High-country tenure is being reviewed. <i>(Jul 16, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  Fiona had married a high country farmer. <i>(Nov 26, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

### high-country dog
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mustering or sheepdog suited to high altitude work by being able to stand up to steep, stony conditions.

>  <b>WHYTE</b> in that period [1850s] of our land settlement history, a faithful, reliable, high-country dog was worth its weight in gold. <i>(1927,Sheep-Dog, The,115</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> He had everything one could wish for in a high country dog. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,89</i>)

### high-country man
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an owner or worker in the high country.

>  Losses in the past three years have been low and many high country men who have been working on a margin for some time have been able to fortify themselves against a winter's knock. <i>(Sep 1, 1949,NZ Farmer,10</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Where we live the mountain beech - which all high-country men call 'white birch' - clothes many of the spurs ... <i>(1969,Open Country,47</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Crawford's younger brother, Sean also a high country man, and a friend, Hugh McKenzie, were also interested in coming in. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,175</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> .. a dedicated high country man was always willing and eager to accept the challenge [of high country farming.] <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> Tell me of a high-countryman who's not attached to his bit of rock. <i>(1989,Frigate Bird,112</i>)

### high-country rock
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the musical style of such rural 1970s musical groups as Daggy and the Dickheads.

>  They cooked skinny critic Colin Hogg bacon and eggs and he coined their sound 'high country rock.' <i>(May 12, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>)

### high-country run
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a grazing property in the high country, usually in the South island.

>  The permanent staff employed on a high-country run is small and the handling of the sheep kept to a minimum. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,50</i>)

>  This [difficulty with mating] is experienced elsewhere on high country runs. <i>(Sep 28, 1961,NZ Farmer,17</i>)

>  <b>THORNTON</b> Many high country runs preferred to retain blade shearing for their Merino fleeces ... [high-country run.] <i>(1986,NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings,237</i>)

>  <b>Owen Marshall</b> He wrote up a piece about high country runs being bought by overseas buyers, and the possible economic and political implications. <i>(2007,Drybread,100</i>)

### high-country station
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large leasehold or freehold property in the land bordering the Southern Alps.

>  <b>MARTiN</b> The winter of 1895 saw one of the most devastating 'big snows' causing heavy stock losses for high-country stations ... <i>(1990,Forgotten Worker, The,31</i>)

>  Tourism shifted the focus of many high-country stations. <i>(Apr 2, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> As with most high-country stations of this sort boundary-keeping after the autumn muster is a regular practice. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,179</i>)

>  Think of a quintessential New Zealand high-country station and chances are you'd imagine something out of the Mackenzie. <i>(2008,NZ Geographic,94/58</i>)

### high-grading
 
 <i>n.</i> dumping low-value fish from the catch

>  Any dumping of fish for what is known as high-grading will not be countenanced. <i>(Jun 16, 2004,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>)

>  He said the ministry was looking "very hard" at which boats were high-grading and which countries they were from... The ministry was investigating some suspected cases of high-grading, Mr Anderton said. ... Sealord Group chief executive Doug McKay condemned high-grading and said those who did it needed to be punishied. <i>(Sep 30, 2006,Dominion Post,A11</i>)

### high-head dam
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> The first [scheme] to be made public was the Horowhenua board's scheme which was, in reality, two proposals: a 'high-head' dam on the upper Otaki.. and a 'low-head' scheme involving a dam, or dams, at Otaki Forks. <i>(1994,Tararua : the Story of a Mountain Range,236</i>)

### high-low
 
 <i>n.</i> mixing glam fashion with grunge

>  The idea of mixing glam with grunge has become so fashionable it even has a name: high-low or high casual... These days high-low is mainstream. <i>(May 9, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),12</i>)

### high-stocked
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of pasture and stock] the placement of heavy numbers on a food supply.

>  The extra income from higher stocking and higher production was mostly counteracted by the extra cost of the high-stocked herd. With predicted future lower milk payouts the high-stocked and cropping groups have lower profitability ... <i>(Jun 15, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,18</i>)

### high-water mark
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRiEVE</b> `High-water marks' are relentlessly disclosed and the miscreants lined up on the floor to the great delight of their Pharisaical brethren. `Necks!' shouts Hauraki ... Raima is certainly running no risks this morning ... annulling for ever my oft-repeated aspersion that he never washed his neck. <i>(1939,Sketches from Maoriland,67,1961 ed.</i>)

### Higher Salaries Commission
 
 <i>n.</i> the commission which sets salaries for MPs and senior civil servants (See [HSC](../H#hsc))

>  The Auditor-General says MPs should not have the power to set their own entitlements and privileges. That role should be handed over to the Higher Salaries Commission... Currently HSC determines pay levels, but Speaker Jonathan Hunt and Minister Responsible for Ministerial Services, Prime Minister Helen Clark, determine entitlements and privileges. <i>(Jul 24, 2001,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  Just one final rumination on the Higher Salaries Commission and the Low Pay Unit. <i>(Sep 18, 1987,Contact (Wgtn),2</i>)

### Highlander
 
 <i>n.</i> a breed of sheep developed by Andy and Hamish Ramsden in Hawkes Bay

>  The Primeras breeding enterprise begun in the 1980s, joined with Woodville farmers Andy and Hamish Ramsden, who had developed a maternal crossbred sheep of their own, the Highlander. <i>(Feb 5, 2004,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

>  Their sheep, the Highlander, is firmly established as part of the Hawke's Bay-based multimillion-dollar Rissington Breedline sheep and beef breeding business... in 2000, the three families joined together and decided to develop the Ramsden breed, newly named the Highlander, as a maternal breed alongside the Primera, which would be developed further and marketed as a complementary meat sire. <i>(Feb 28, 2003,Dominion Post,C5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a breed of sheep

>  So far, the lambs have been mainly sourced from Rissington's client list - farmers using the Hawke's Bay Company's Finn-Texel-Romney maternal composite sheep called Highlander.... "Most of the farmers who have Highlanders are so impressed with them they are holding on to as many lambs as they can to build up their flocks." <i>(Jun 10, 2004,Dominion Post,C7</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred sheep based on Finn, Texels and Romneys.

>  Breedline has approached Waipuna to help develop the Highlander. This breed, Donald Polson says, will be based on Finn genetics, Texels and Romneys .. The Te Tui flock will become a franchise flock for Breedline with Highlander ram hoggets being available for mating in 2002. <i>(Sep 14, 2000,Wanganui Chronicle,12</i>)

### highlander
 
 <i>n.</i> sea perch

>  <i>Helicolenus percoides</i> (Richardson). Sea perch, so called John Dory (scroddie, fivefinger, soldier-fish, Jock Stuart, Higlander). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/416</i>) [NOTES: -->1938 TrRSNZ (Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour)]

>  SEAPERCH (POHUiAKAROA) JOCK STUART, HiGHLANDER, FiVEFiNGER, SOLDiERFiSH or SCRODDiE <i>Helicolenus papillosus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) The seaperch is a fish well known to all fishermen both sports and commercial fishermen alike, who give it quite a number of vernacular names, a few of which are as follows: Jock Stuart, Highlander, Fivefinger and soldierfish. These names have a bearing on some distinguishing characteristic of the fish. For instance the first two names are supposed to be given on account of the colouring of the fish resembling, in some ways, the tartan of a Scot; ... <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,344,(2nd edn) ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> Sea Perch <i>Helicolenus percoides</i> Jock Stuart; Highlander; Soldier-fish; <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,169</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SEA PERCH ...<i> Other names: Helicolenus papillosus</i>; Jock Stewart, scarpee, highlander, fivefinger, Maori chief, scrodie gurnard (Australia); pahuiakaroa (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,287</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> HiGHLANDER See Perch, Sea <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,119</i>)

>  [species name] sea perch .. [scientific name] <i>Helicolenus</i> .. [other name(s)] highlander, jock stewart, perch, ocean perch, five finger, scarpe, scorpion <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] highlander 1165-6 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WiLSON</b> HiGHLANDER Another name for Red Moki. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,120</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a Super rugby franchise based in Dunedin (also in sing. a member of the Highlanders team)

>  ..the Highlanders have plenty to play for as they, too, have a slim chance of making the playoffs. <i>(May 7, 2004,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,7</i>)

### highline
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cowshed with a milkline above the cows being milked.

>  The shed is replacing a 6 double-up highline. [highline shed] <i>(Jun 1982,NZ Dairy Exporter,21</i>)

>  Two seasons ago he was milking 140 cows and spending two and three quarter cows in his 9-aside highline shed at the morning milking. <i>(Dec 1976,NZ Dairy Exporter,06</i>)
