 <i>n.</i> a White Power gang

### Highway Hero
 
 <i>n.</i> (an award for bravery)

>  A man was last night hailed a highway hero after dragging a woman from her burning car wreck in Northland. ... Constable Mayne said he would recommend both men for "Highway Hero" awards. <i>(Nov 18, 2005,NZ Herald,A1</i>)

>  Mr Ratu has been named Highway Hero of 2005 for his actions, an award created by Police, Land Transport New Zealand, Beaurepaires and New Zealand Trucking Magazine. ... it is the second time Mr Ratu has been nominated as a Highway Hero. <i>(Dec 1, 2005,NZ Herald,A1</i>)

### hihi
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) stitchbird [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [W.48 Hihi (i) 9. <i>Notiomystis cincta</i>](../W#w.48-hihi-(i)-9.-<i>notiomystis-cincta</i>),[stitch-bird. ..](../S#stitch-bird.-..))

>  Thrity stichbirds/hihi were successfully transferred to Karori Wildlife Sanctuary in Wellington from their home in the Hauraki Gulf in February. <i>(Jul 2005,Conservation Action,19</i>)

>  Hihi are found on only two conservation islands in the Auckland area - on Little Barrier (Hauturu), which has the country's only self-sustaining popoulation, and Tiritiri Matangi. <i>(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,55</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> [early spg ihi] 7. <i>Ptilotis cincta</i>. ... Kotihe of the natives. <i>Yate</i>. ihi of the natives of Taranaki. -<i> Dieffenbach</i>. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/187</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/187]

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> [One] bird i found here [Taranaki] is of a new species; it is called E ihi, and belongs to the class of honey-eaters. [fn Ptilotis cincta, Dub.] <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/147</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 10. POGNORNiS CiNCTA. Dubus. Stitch Bird. ihi. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,5</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> POGNORNiS CiNCTA (STiTCH-BiRD.) ... <i>Native names. ihi</i> [omitted 1888], Hihi, Tihe, Kotihe, Kotihewera, \{Tiora, and Tiheora\}; male and female are sometimes distinguished as Hihi-paka and Hihi-matakiore or Tihe-kiore. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,98,{1888 2nd edn 1/101} ed.</i>)

>  10. <i>Pognornis cincta</i>, Dubus. Stitch-bird, ihi. - This shy and now rare honey-eater is seldom met with. <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,276</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <i>Pognornis cincta</i>. - Hihi, Matakiore, Stitchbird. North island. <i>(1886,TrNZi,18/112</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> ... to say nothing of Korimako and Pitoitoi ..., we never saw or heard the notes of either the Piopio, the Tieke, or the Hihi, all of which birds were at one time more numerous even than the Huia. The <i>Zosterops</i> was everywhere abundant, also the Grey Warbler and Rifleman, and ... we found the Tomtit comparatively plentiful; the Parakeet chased its mate through the tree-tops with sharp cries of `twenty-eight'; ... and among the tangle of the underwood the ever-present Flycatcher displayed its pretty fan-like tail. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/14</i>)

>  <i>hihi</i>: stitch-bird. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> <i>STiTCHBiRD Notiomystis cincta</i> Maori name: Hihi. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,217</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The Maori knew [the stitchbird] as hi-hi, from its call; scientists, for good reasons of their own, refer to it as <i>Notiomystis cincta</i>. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,217</i>)

>  264. <i>Notiomystis cincta</i> (Du Bus, 1839) <i>Stitchbird (Hihi)</i> ... now confined to Little Barrier island only. <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,69</i>)

>  <i>Stitchbird Notiomystis cincta </i>(DU BUS, 1839) ... OTHER NAME: <i>Hihi. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,289</i>)

>  The last confirmed sighting of hihi on mainland New Zealand was by a government surveyor in the Tararua Ranges in 1883. <i>ibid.</i> 91 Hihi seem to prefer a split-level design in their nests, with the entrance below the living accommodation. <i>(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 53/88</i>)

>  The last record of hihi on the mainland was from the 1880s, somewhere near Tararua. The species was thought to have gone the way of the huia, until a decent-sized population was found on Little Barrier island, which is where 95 per cent of hihi still live. <i>(Jul 2007,NZ Geographic,86/86</i>)

>  So if you think you've seen a bellbird by the Beehive, a hihi in Haitaitai or a kaka in Khandallah, go to our website www.santuary.org.nz and click Report a Bird. <i>(Aug 3, 2006,Wellingtonian,27</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  A Maori named Tenetahi.. later made a sworn statement that he had seen two hihi skins in Robinson's tent. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/22</i>)

### hihiwa
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [silvery paua (1947)](../S#silvery-paua-(1947)))

### hike
 
 <i>v.</i> [(yachties')]

>  <b>FRANCE</b> `i shan't set the jib. All you'll have to do is to stay where i tell you. And hike when i tell you.' `Hike?' `Lean out. if she heels too far.' <i>(1961,ice Cold River,224</i>)

### Hiker's one
 
 <i>n.</i> a cheap crystal set sold in large numbers by the Lamphouse in the 1940s. [was advertised at the time in the Listener]

### hiki
 
 <i>v.</i> to carry in the arms [ORIGIN: Maori, was advertised at the time in the Listener]

>  <b>MANiNG</b> `i tell you what we must do ... we will not carry (pikau) the provisions, we will hiki them' (Hiki is the word in Maori which describes the act of carrying an infant in the arms.) <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(viii) 129</i>)

### hiking
 
 <i>n.</i> [(Auckland taximen's slang)]

>  Sending a taxi a long distance to carry out an order. The only available car may have to hike halfway across town. <i>(Jul 1983,Metro,no.25 81</i>) [NOTES: Auckland Metro]

### hikitapu
 
 <i>n.</i> a tapu lifting [This must be a very modern Maori coinage, not in either Reed's or Williams's Maori dictionaries]

>  Maori believe hikitapu ceremonies must be held to free land and buildings of negative spiritual influences. The word hiki means to raise or lift and tapu means the sacredness placed on a site. <i>(Jul 13, 2001,Dominion,1</i>)

### hikoi
 
 <i>n.</i> a walk, march (popularised in Eng. use when applied to the march to Waitangi, on Waitangi Day, 6 February, 1984). [[Ma. <b>hikoi</B> step out, plod, pace, stepping (netball)] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 52.]

>  The hikoi model left such a dent in our consciousness that it has been re-used twice: by the churches in their 1998 march against poverty, and this year in the foreshore and seabed hikoi, which saw Maori land issues once again brought to the steps of Parliament. <i>(Jul 10, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

>  in two days he will be setting forth on a hikoi to Wellington as part of the protest. <i>(Jul 28, 2002,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  Along with about 50 members of their own families and those of fallen veterans, they were farewelled on a hikoi (journey) to Cassino and El Alamein to farewell their brothers, cousins, and mates who did not return. <i>(Oct 3, 2001,Evening Post,25</i>)

>  They will want to use.. the publicity and emotion that is likely to be engendered by the arrival at Parliament next week of the hokoi-in-a-hurry, as a platform on which to build. <i>(May 1, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  What is a hikoi? The organisers describe it as "a time-honoured journey of expectation, a form of providing a united voice and a physical presence against unjust structures". <i>(Aug 23, 1998,NZ Catholic,9</i>)

>  The feared confrontation between the hikoi and the police didn't eventuate and march leader Eva Rickard had every right to feel victorious. <i>(Feb 25, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  The Taranaki tribes.. organised a commemorative hikoi (expedition) to Dunedin. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

>  i walked the whole length of that hikoi when they were parked up by the bridge all the way along Pahia. <i>(Jul 26, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>)

>  Ms Morehu's hikoi took in Kaitaia, Whangarei, Kaikohe, Wellsford, Auckland, Hamilton, Te Awamutu, Rotorua. Taupo, Turangi, and Levin. <i>(Sep 15, 2005,Wellingtonian,News 5</i>)

>  it's not bad for someone who got puffed walking around the block when he started the Hikoi 2000 walking programme. <i>(May 6, 2000,Evening Post,23</i>)

>  Hikoi marshals were mnore than competent at keeping everyone moving... <i>(May 3, 2004,Salient,8/52</i>)

>  `Give me an `H', give ma an `i', give me a `K', give me an `O', give me an `i'. What have we got? Hikoi (walk),' they yelled as they entered the Taitokerau Maori district. <i>(Feb 4, 1984,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  The group was farewelled from the Turangawaiwai Marae with blessings from the Maori Queen ... which was given in a closed meeting with elders of the hikoi (march). <i>(Jan 29, 1984,NZ Times,1</i>)

>  A hikoi or pilgrimage `in support of the sacredness of all life' is expected to draw Catholic youth from all over the country to converge a Panguru ... in January. <i>(Nov 1989,New Zealandia,10</i>)

>  Those on the hikoi or peace walk under the Kotahitanga banner of Maori unity, had decided before they reached the Waitangi Treaty grounds that they would not be divided. <i>(Feb 7, 1984,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Te Hikoi ki Waitangi will make its 10th annual pilgrimage in stages with a march through Kaitaia planned on February 2... Hikoi organiser Hilda Harawira said today each stage would be lead by team leaders from Ngati Kuri, Te Aupouri, Ngai Takoto, Te Rarawa, Ngati Kahu and Ngapuhi. <i>(Jan 13, 1995,Evening Post,14</i>) [NOTES: There's no show without a Harawira!]

>  All are welcome to join his hikoi home to mahia. <i>(Jul 18, 2005,NZ Herald,C7</i>)

>  He said veteran activist Titewhai Harawira would be better off trying to get a proper job than leading a protest hikoi. "There will probably be hikoi on a bus, paid for by the taxpayer." <i>(Aug 20, 2003,Dominion Post,A1</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [[Ma. <b>hikoi</B> step out, plod, pace, stepping (netball)] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 52.]

>  She hikoied for everything pertaining to the North. <i>(Jun 1998,Cuisine,No. 22: 7</i>)

>  Next week, Bishop Henare Tait talks about the relics of Bishop Pompallier which are hikoiing around the country <i>(Jan 27, 2002,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to go on a (protest) march [[Ma. <b>hikoi</B> step out, plod, pace, stepping (netball)] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 52.]

>  Don't hikoi. Unless it's one of those nice hikoi for world peace or something like that. <i>(May 15, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

### Hikoi of Hope
 
 <i>n.</i> [-]

>  The New Zealand Catholic bishops.. have stated again their strong support for the Anglican-sponsored Hikoi of Hope... The arrival of the Anglican-led Hikoi of Hope in Wellington on October 1 coincides with the anniversary ofthe death in 1926 of Suzanne Aubert.. a candidate to be New Zealand's first saint. <i>(Sep 20, 1998,NZ Catholic,1</i>)

>  The Hikoi of Hope, sponsored by the Anglican Church to focus on what it sees as growing poverty in New Zealand, will reach Wellington just about the time the government is putting the finishing touches to what, in effect, will be a mini-budget. <i>(Sep 27, 1998,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  Where the prophets to inspire us - or be chopped down? The Greens? The hikoi of hope? How time has silenced them! <i>(Dec 1999,Tui Motu interislands,3</i>)

>  Your editorial picks up the same tone with your comments on "the so-called hikoi of hope" and "whether the churches allow their pulpits to be used" - as if we are not in control of what we are doing. <i>(Mar 29, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  The Hikoi of Hope may have flopped in many ways, not least in creating a sardonic backlash from case-hardened liberals, and getting hijacked as a bandwagon for lesser causes than poverty. But, boy, it was a ringing triumph in one very important way. <i>(Oct 17, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

### hikoi pahi
 
 <i>n.</i> = walking (school) bus [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Kura Kaupapa Maori O Wairarapa, lead by Meikura Arahanga, launched their very own Kura Hikoi Pahi. <i>(Mar 22, 2006,Wairarapa News,24</i>)

>  When- Monday 7 November What- Hikoi Pahi - Walk to School Bus & Physical Activities day (various routes to school followed by a morning of traditional Maori and contemporary games) Where- Te Kura Kaupapa Manurewa Cost- No charge, no public Contact- Waitangi on 09 268 2031 <i>(2006,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.cmsport.co.nz/clientuploads/Events/PushPlayDay/PushPlayDay05Activities.pdf?PHPSESSiD=927c81641ec839a42f134923a95b141a Accessed 26/03/06.]

### hiku
 
 <i>n.</i> frostfish

>  A much longer and more delicate fish is the hiku of the Maori, or frost fish. <i>(Jan 30, 1890,Otago Witness</i>)

>  [Native or Common Name] Hiku, Frostfish .. [Scientific Name] <i>Lepidopus caudatus </i> <i>(1877,TrNZi,9/485</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> FROST FiSH (<i>Lepidopus caudatus </i>). ... [30] The frost-fish, or hiku of the Maoris, is esteemed the most delicious fish in New Zealand. it is most commonly found cast up after cold frosty nights on sandy beaches that are exposed to the long roll of ocean swell, and is not in this country obtained by any kind of fishing. <i>ibid.</i> 300 <i>Lepidopus caudatus </i> Euphrasen Frost-fish, Hiku, or Para <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,29</i>)

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> FROST FiSH (<i>Lepidopus caudatus </i>). ... [30] The frost-fish, or hiku of the Maoris, is esteemed the most delicious fish in New Zealand. it is most commonly found cast up after cold frosty nights on sandy beaches that are exposed to the long roll of ocean swell, and is not in this country obtained by any kind of fishing. <i>ibid.</i> 300 <i>Lepidopus caudatus </i> Euphrasen Frost-fish, Hiku, or Para <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,29</i>)

>  39. <i>Lepidopus caudatus </i> Euphrasen. Frost Fish. Maori: Hiku, Para. <i>(1929,TrNZi,61/147</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> FROSTFiSH ... Other names:<i>Lepidopus caudatus </i>; para, hiku, taharangi (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,277</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> HiKU <i>Maori name for Frostfish </i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,120</i>)

### Hilgendorf
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred cultivar of wheat [named after F.W. Hilgendorf, plant geneticist.]

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> Not long ago .. a wheat named Hilgendorf was evolved. <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,84</i>)

### hill
 
 <i>n.</i> attrib use: hill dog, hill sheep.

>  There is a most interesting footnote on the subject [of placer sheep] on p. 383 of Guthrie-Smith's `Tutira' ... which everyone who is interested in hill sheep should read. <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary Addenda]

>  <b>LiLiCO</b> He was a great hill dog ... <i>(1934,Sheep Dog Memoirs,15</i>)

### hill bag
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high-country shepherd or musterer's bag used to carry raincoat, lunch, and binoculars.

>  <b>JARDiNE</b> i took off the hill-bag and started in to my lunch. <i>(1978,Shadows on the Hill,76</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> We all carry a hill bag which will contain a parka, lunch, binoculars and perhaps a camera. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,15</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> i was given a walkie-talkie in my hill bag, i had my old swannie on, and i looked like a real rough diamond. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,105</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> i'd been winging sheep up a face and meant to bring them down, but they rounded a ridge before my huntaway got a head on them, and that's when the walkie-talkie in my hill bag piped up and said "keep him going." <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,60</i>)

>  <b>MACNiCOL</b> Jack's hill-bag .. had given away under strain. <i>(1967,Echoes from Skippers Canyon,151</i>)

### hill block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large area of hill country, grazed by sheep, cattle or goats.

>  <b>GORDON</b> When i first came here, the hill block just ran 1200 to 1400 Merino wethers all year round. The flats are light and stony, the hill block has a thin soil layer on shingle and, like all of Marlborough, Bankhouse is drought prone. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,57</i>)

>  <b>ASPiNALL</b> The hoggets have to be drenched and crutched before they are put on hill blocks. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,148</i>)

>  Properties for Sale: 44 paddocks and 5 hill blocks, all permanent fencing. <i>(Jul 13, 1995,NZ Farmer,30</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> There was a hill block bare, after burning off, and it was like a waterfall, pouring off the terraces, not running in the creeks; it couldn't flow in the creeks, there was just too much water. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,182</i>)

### hill boy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ...Dunedin and Auckland with hilly sections had to use 'leaders' (Clydesdales), which were hooked up with the lighter horses by a 'hill boy' on the steeper inclines. <i>(Aug 2003,NZ Memories,No. 43/42</i>)

### hill cattle
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) station cattle that graze with little attention [often store cattle.]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The calving in the hill cattle is usually about 85 per cent. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,28</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> i had copied my companions in distress, when hill cattle had to be drafted ... <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,75</i>)

### hill chain
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a range of hills.

>  <b>GUTHRiE-SMiTH</b> Consentaneously the hill chains of the run must have been created. <i>(1921,Tutira,2</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRiE-SMiTH</b> Examination of the general physical features of the run has shown a series of parallel hill chains running north and south. <i>(1921,Tutira,12</i>)

### hill country
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) steep and broken pastoral land [more easily and diversely farmed than high country]

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> A great part of the hill country in New Zealand under pasture is devoted to .. grazing of sheep. <i>(1915,NZ Sheep Farmer,106</i>)

>  The property is 1000ha of strong hill country ... <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Dominion,33</i>)

>  <b>COOP</b> in general one would say that store sheep hill country offers greater possibilities for early shearing than fat lamb country. <i>(1950,Shearing Ewes before Lambing,30</i>)

>  <b>DOMiNY</b> Distinguished from the South island high country is South island hill country, characterized by a larger proportion of lowland soil, lower snow risk and less dependence on wool as a source of income ... <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,142</i>)

>  .. the Angus breed is again appearing in the hill country in large numbers and being competitively sourced by farmers on the higher quality lowland finishing country. <i>(Sep 5, 2002,Dominion,Farm Supplement  22</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [high country] the summer grazing areas or areas that are snow-covered during the winter.

>  <b>FOSTER & WRiGHT</b> it is crucially important that the team move on time. Peter Pinckney says that 20 April is the last possible date by which the wethers should be out of the hill country, and only the use of packhorses makes that deadline possible. <i>(1983,Stockman Country,33</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> high ground, esp. when used for sheep farming; steep to rolling country (usu. with only a small proportion accessible by wheeled tractor; and in NZ usu. land previously covered in bush), a mainly Ni use.Cf. [1984 Frame Angel at My Table 175 `That's the Mount.' [Maunganui] i had grown used to the North islanders' reference to hills as mountains.] Compare high country. [(mainly in NI)]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> New Zealand is undoubtedly a hilly country. ... The hill country in New Zealand is at the same time far from being useless; it is always preferred by the natives for their plantations of potatoe and maize, ... <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),26</i>)

>  FULiGULA NOVAE ZELANDiAE, Gml. Papango. Black Widgeon. in the hill-country, a few years since, this was sufficiently common; a small tarn, near Lake Coleridge, yet retains the name of Widgeon Lake, ... <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/73</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>G.M.WiLSON</b> The only land they proclaim is the low-lying land, so that if the station is far up country and all hill country ... the Government would never put it up. <i>(Feb 9, 1873,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Feb 9 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 173]

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> Among the innumerable and entangled ranges of rich hill-country towards the Noth of our island, [pigs] lived ... in numbers whose ... numbers would take a pretty stiff census to record. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,157</i>)

>  [?as distinct from flat or paddocked farmland ?is the collocation `on the hill' or `hill country'] The percentage of increase [in lambing] ... is very high, particularly so in the paddocks, where 100 to 125 per cent. is not uncommon in favourable seasons, while on the hill or unimproved country it varies from 45 to 80 per cent. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,121</i>)

>  Looking to the right is seen perhaps the most extended stretch of hill-country in the whole of New Zealand. Rounded hills, bush-covered for the most part, extend as far as one can see [on the East Coast]. <i>(1897,TrNZi,29/513</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> We had now reached the hill country, winding about [in the train] with many a cunning turn. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,147</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> [often `hill land' used in this book] A great part of the hill country in New Zealand under pasture is devoted ... to the grazing of sheep. <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,106</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The writer will content himself with noting here a few ... points in the management of sheep ... on this type [sc. tussock high country] of country, as differing from the system ... on farm land, or grassed hill country such as that found in the North island, which is heavily stocked. <i>(1922,NZ Sheepfarming,90</i>)

>  At one time both varieties of this hawk were fairly common in all the hill country, but only on rare occasions were they met with on the plains. <i>(1928,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,9/188</i>)

>  With ... the increased work necessary to top-dress this type of hill country, the occupiers cannot hope to farm successfully ... more than ... 300 acres ... <i>(1932,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,13/66</i>)

>  <b>LiLiCO</b> ... i cannot conceive a more perfect dog for hill country than this fine dog ... <i>(1934,Sheep Dog Memoirs,19</i>)

>  A great part of the 24 million acres of occupied, uncultivated land consists of light hill country, well suited to extensive grazing, or broken country on which cultivation is too difficult. <i>ibid.</i> 281 The unavoidable deterioration of some of the hill country pastures has affected wool quality in several ways <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,19</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> My flock was the dry flock wethers. One day i got notice to ... put my flock of 3,000 sheep away back on the hill country of `Lochindorb', near the head of the Owaka river. it was very much infested with wild dogs and wild pigs, ... but it was very good sheep country. <i>(1937,Pioneering in Otago,94</i>)

>  Bracken fern (<i>Pteridium esculentum</i>) was [rest of citation missing] <i>(1946,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,27/432</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> You know, Andy reckons someday the Government'll decide to shut up all this hill country. <i>(1949,i Saw in My Dream,111,(1974 ed. Rhodes) ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> Rotations do not enter into the economy of North island hill country ... <i>ibid.</i> 360 Hill country - Grassland country, other than montane or mountainous, too steep to plough or to work with cultural implements. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,72,1970 ed.</i>)

>  i would include among New Zealand English words: hill-country <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  [cp LOW COUNTRY] The bulk of fertiliser is still used on the low country, but increasing quantities are now being applied to the hill country. <i>(Oct 1952,NZ Geographer,131</i>) [NOTES: (Burns)]

>  <b>DARE</b> i had known only hill country shepherding hitherto. <i>(1956,Rouseabout Jane,160</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> The settlers ... soon learned the art of the `bush burn'. ... in Taranaki and the Manawatu ... good pastures were established in the `bush burn' country, but on much of the hill country throughout the North island there was constant danger of counter attack by bracken ... <i>(1959,Descriptive Atlas,39</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> The story of a ewe fair starts on hill country where sheep do well enough, but on which lambs do not fatten as quickly. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,51</i>)

>  <b>WALLiS</b> On a sheep station in the hill country behind Awatere, Neil ... supervised the last of the second-shear ewes through the yard gate <i>(1963,Point of Origin,181</i>)

>  The fertility and the carrying capacity of much of this `hill country' have been increased through aerial topdressing and the use of modern cultivating machinery. <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,40</i>)

>  Hill country: Steep to rolling country, usually with only a small proportion accessible by wheeled tractor. in New Zealand usually it had previously been covered in bush. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,5</i>)

>  hill country NZ hilly country used for sheep-farming. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,354</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng.Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 232]]



 
 <i>n.</i> [hill country refers to North Island, high country to the South Island.]

>  <b>LEDYARD</b> When he came to understand she was about to sail [he] went up into the hill country and invited the warriors to come down and kill the strangers. <i>(1783,Journal,18</i>)

### hill country farmed
 
 <i>adj.</i> farmed on hill country, a strong selling point

>  Hill country farmed on grass and hay at 1000 ft above sea level break fed through winter and are very well grown and quiet to handle . . . . <i>(Sep 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,40</i>)

### hill country line/sheep
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a group of stock of one type, bred or grazed on hill country.

>  Stratford Cattle Fair - 1000 cattle. This represents many hill country lines. <i>(Jul 13, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,39</i>)

>  The yarding is of very high quality well-bred hill country sheep. <i>(Dec 5, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,19</i>)

### hill dog
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a strong-footed high-country mustering dog.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> i am, of course, referring to him as a hill dog and i saw him under the hardest of conditions. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,36</i>)

>  <b>MCMiLLAN</b> The hill dogs we ran through the shingle and rock, when the runholders mustered the high tops for stock. <i>(2000,Country Bloke and Other Verse, The,229</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. besides being a crack hill dog he was about the top trial dog of his day. He was regarded by many as one of the greatest hill dogs of all time. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,53</i>)

### hill ewe
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [high country] a ewe grazed on country away from a station homestead that need little care and attention.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> There was a big crowd; mainly 'down country' men looking for annual drafts of hill ewes. <i>(1972,Sheep Thief,120</i>)

>  <b>STAPLES</b> The shepherds were away early in the mornings to muster the hill and bring in the hill ewes. <i>(1964,Cooks and Shepherds Come Away,24</i>)

### hill face
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering and dog trials] a steep hillside.

>  He has found already that it pays to use super on some of the rougher hill faces. <i>(Apr 29, 1932,NZ Dairy Exporter,36</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> in this area the bush is noted for its tanglefoot and the hill face for its steepness ... <i>(1964,Deer Shooting Days,75</i>)

>  <b>MiLLS ET AL</b> A simple illustration is to imagine one sheep on a hill-face. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,35</i>)

>  <b>MCCASKiLL</b> On open plains and sunny hill faces this [soil diminishing] has led to accelerated wind and frost erosion.. <i>(1973,Hold This Land,4</i>)

>  The Perendales will run up the hill-face no trouble. [hill-face] <i>(Aug 23, 1984,NZ Farmer,20</i>)

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> .. after days of rain whole hill faces wrinkle, sliding slowly a few feet. <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,42</i>)

>  <b>MiLLS ET AL</b> He is making a great job of getting up the steep hill-face, giving it all he's got. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,47</i>)

### hill flax
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Phormium colensoi</i>, a smallish variety of NZ flax found in dry hillsides

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> ... a smaller species is found in both islands on dry hill sides] <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,111</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Phormium Colensoi</i> [Andersen] <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Stewart island.]

>  <b>GUTHRiE-SMiTH</b> (And) <i>Phormium Colensoi</i> <i>(1921,Tutira</i>)

>  <b>VAiLE</b> <i>Hill flax</i> Ph. Colensoi [Central North island] <i>(1938,Pioneering the Pumice,299</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary)]

>  <b>M'DONNELL</b> The plant throws an abundance of seed. The hill-flax is of a finer texture, whiter, and stronger, than that grown in the valleys, though the staple may not be quite so long. <i>(1834,Journal,18,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The hill flax is met with on the steepest and most exposed part of the coast, where the soil is not of sufficient depth to produce any other kinds of vegetation; ... <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),36</i>)

### hill flock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a flock of sheep grazed on blocks of hill country, rather than in paddocks [often store sheep.]

>  A sub-lease of part of the mountain run also affords Mr Revie scope for a small hill flock. <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,35</i>)

### hill gang
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mustering gang.

>  <b>MCMiLLAN</b> .. the hill gangs come no more to camp and boil their billys and sleep on the earthern floor. <i>(1981,Tales of the High Country,28</i>)

### hill hut
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a musterer's hut.

>  <b>MCMiLLAN</b> The woolshed stood in peril [from a tussock fire] and the hill huts stood the same. <i>(1981,Tales of the High Country,41</i>)

>  <b>MCMiLLAN</b> in a lonely hill hut you might dream now and then. <i>(1981,Tales of the High Country,45</i>)

### hill lamb
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Politics) a lamb of a hardy breed that can be grazed on hill blocks rather than in paddocks.

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The hill lambs were weaned on to a block of country which had been spelled since shearing time, and did remarkably well on this clean run. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,113</i>)

### hill lambing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [high country] the lambing of ewes on unfenced hill blocks.

>  The paddock lambing is over and the results on the whole are fairly good. Prospects for hill lambing are good. <i>(Oct 3, 1903,Farmers' Union Advocate,4</i>)

### hill man
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pastoralist, shepherd or musterer who works on hill blocks. (See [hill shepherd](../H#hill-shepherd))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> However, both the old hands were good men, one in particular being one of the best hillmen in the district. <i>(1949,High Country Days,12</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> One of the real wayback stations, it takes in some of the roughest sheep country in the island and like Mt Algidus, it is essentially a place for a real hillman. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South island,230</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The well-dressed hillman always dagged his trousers. <i>(1983,Other Side of Farming, The,1</i>)

>  <b>BROSNAHAN</b> The hill man didn't do anything on the flat and flat guys didn't go on the hills. <i>(1998,Glenaray 1898-1998,71</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> The fencers fenced, the paddock men mustered paddocks, the hill men like me mustered hill country ... [hillman] <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,73</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A down-country farmer knows every paddock of his farm like the back of his hand, and the hill man should know his country too. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,54</i>)

>  <b>GiBSON</b> He was a great hill man and a fast climber who never seemed to tire. <i>(1990,Among the High Hills of Canterbury,83</i>)

### hill paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large,open and often steep hill pastoral area.

>  in general, hill country carrying native grasses is safe, but some hill paddocks do produce the disease and these should be avoided. <i>(Mar 15, 1943,NZ Journal of Agriculture,146</i>)

>  in hill paddocks with uneven cover, more readings need to be taken. <i>(Sep 2, 2002,in the Paddock</i>)

### hill run
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Duppa tells me the buying mania will spread into the [Canterbury] hill runs even to the snowy country. <i>(Dec 4, 1862,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/807,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hill-country or high-country pastoral property.

>  Sheep on the hill runs have had a hard time during the winter. <i>(Sep 1889,NZ Farmer,359</i>)

>  Stores are brought down off the hill runs to be finished off on the luscious paspalum-white clover pastures on the flats. <i>(Jan 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,15</i>)

### hill sheep
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep grazed entirely on hill blocks with light management [as distinct from paddock sheep.]

>  <b>GORDON</b> The hill sheep's performance is better than our paddock sheep. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,109</i>)

>  <b>NEWPORT</b> When i was young i did see hill sheep calling and mobbing up together when a storm was approaching. <i>(1989,Footprints Farewell,91</i>)

>  <b>BROSNAHAN</b> With the development of the downland country came the need for better utilisation of the improved pastures. But hill sheep could not be turned into paddock sheep. <i>(1998,Glenaray 1898-1998,71</i>)

>  <b>WATERS</b> .. the halfbreds were a better hill sheep. <i>(1976,Mostly a Farmer,36</i>)

### hill shepherd
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shepherd who looks after sheep on large open hill blocks, as distinct from a paddock shepherd working on fenced land. (See [hill man](../H#hill-man))

>  <b>BARKER</b> Very different was Ned Palmer, the most diminutive and wiry of hill shepherds. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,138</i>)

### hill station
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high country or hill country farming property.

>  On the hill stations, and along the coast, the tallies were much the same as last year ... <i>(Dec 1, 1928,NZ Farmer,1471</i>)

>  HiLL STATiONS ... Cull run cows not required for breeding. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,292</i>)

>  On smaller flat-land farms it is ... easy to muster sheep back into the shelter ..., but on the bigger hill-stations ... the task can be almost hopeless. <i>(1960,Sheep-O,95</i>)

### hill team
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mustering team.

>  <b>MACNiCOL</b> The third addition to our family, a second musterer for Archie's future hill-team, was expected late in the winter of 1947. <i>(1965,Beyond the Skippers Road,64</i>)

### hill work
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) mustering and shepherding on large, steep blocks.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> After the hill work, shearing began ... <i>(1947,Wayleggo,28</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A 'day for the King' is usually hailed with delight .. But at that time of the year all hands are keen to get the hill work finished. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,72</i>)

>  <b>MACNiCOL</b> The playful pup grew to a large.. dog. He proved too easy-going and too much of a 'sun-downer' for hill work. <i>(1967,Skippers' Canyon,129</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. when age forced him to give up hill work he continued on as a paddock shepherd. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,186</i>)

### Hill, the
 
 <i>n.</i> Parliament (Buildings).

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> During the coverage of events leading up to the Marginal Lands Board inquiry, some pretty hard things were said about the way Journalists were digging into the business affairs of the Fitzgeralds. Quite plainly the men on the `hill' regarded it as prying; ... <i>(1981,Straight from the Shoulder,99</i>)

>  Numerous battles with Greenies have obviously made someone on the Hill acutely aware of the dangers of tangling with tree lovers. <i>(Nov 14, 1986,Contact (Wgtn),7</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> Boys on the Hill, the Members of Parliament, when they were all male. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,16</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Mount Eden Prison, Auckland.

>  The Hill is just about permanently short of screws, which incidentally shows what sort of place it is. <i>(Nov 1950,Here & Now,15</i>)

>  it's typical of the prison system that the clothes at The Hill, where there are plenty of visitors, are passable, and you'll even get into trouble up there for not reporting a tear in your coat. <i>(Nov 1953,Here & Now,41</i>)

### hill-country
 
 <i>adj.</i> [attrib.]

>  Hill country <i>(Jul 15, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,67</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> A hill-country flockowner keeps all his ewe lambs, turning a percentage of eighteen-month-old ewes - two-tooths, as they are called - into his main flock every year ... <i>(1962,Country Occasions,51</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> There has been rural de-population in remoter hill-country areas - <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/135</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Now they could relax in a headmaster's residence ... a dignified home that was once the town house of a hill country squatter ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,18</i>)

>  <b>LETHBRiDGE</b> A good hill-country or high-country heading dog was Ash's Moss. <i>(1971,Sunrise on the Hills,118</i>)

>  This line of annual draft hill country steers display good bone and have the ability to grow out. <i>(May 30, 2005,NZ Farmers' Weekly,25</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of stock] raised and grazed in broken pastoral areas.

>  Stortford Lodge Ewe Fair .. Auctioneer's note: Annual line of Hill Country Ewes known for their breeding potential .. auctioneer's note: An outstanding line of well grown Hill Country Steers. <i>(Dec 8, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,30</i>)

>  Plus many more annual lines of well bred hill country cattle. <i>(Apr 6, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,19</i>)

>  .. any lines of shorn well bred hill country lambs. <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Dominion,33</i>)

>  Annual draft of top quality hill country cattle to be drafted. <i>(Apr 20, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,28</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land or property] composed of broken hills and downs [at a lower altitude than high country.]

>  Heifers bought in from hill-country farms to make up deficiencies in home-reared heifers who failed to breed were much more satisfactory in regular breeding. <i>(Apr 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,353</i>)

>  .. their 835ha hill-country farm 25km east of Stratford was .. a picture. [hill-country] <i>(Oct 3, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,18</i>)

>  Over a period of seven years these [breeding ewes] have been procured as two-tooths from the same hill-country station in Gisborne. <i>(May 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,501</i>)

>  High hill-country farms are mainly to be found in the South island, where they cover approximately ten million acres. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,50</i>)

>  Lower hill-country farms form a very important section of our sheep farming in both North and South islands. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,51</i>)

### hill-country farm
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  'Lamb's Wool' comes mostly from cross-bred lambs on hill country farms. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,710</i>)

>  The ewe flocks are maintained by annual drafts ... drawn largely from the hill-country farms. <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,24/201A</i>)

>  The lower hill-country farms are a very important section of sheep farming in both the North and South islands. <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,59</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> Fagan's hill-country farm went as far as the bushline of the Paeroa Range ... <i>(1987,Swagger on Our Doorstep,79</i>)

### hill-country man
 
 <i>n.</i> a pastoralist and stock breeder on hill-country land (as opposed to high or down country).

>  The only course open to the hill-country man then is to use super sparingly but often. <i>(Nov 2, 1939,NZ Farmer,6</i>)

### Hillary Commission
 
 <i>n.</i> [superseded by Sparc]

>  Having been advised, by a length inquiry conducted by Sir Ronald Scott, about the desirability of dispensing with the old Ministry of Recreation and Sport, Moore has come out with the Hillary Commission. <i>(Mar 28, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>) [NOTES: superseded by Sparc]

>  Why are our major sports funding organisations, the Hillary Commission and the Sports Foundation, falling over themselves to give money to New Zealand rugby? <i>(Aug 7, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

>  it is important to counter Dr Peter Snell's claim that Hillary Commission cash is going to the wrong places. <i>(Jun 18, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

### hillpole
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mustering stick. (See [mustering stick; hillstick; nibby; pole](../M#mustering-stick;-hillstick;-nibby;-pole))

>  Boots and a hill pole are - along with a team of dogs - a high country musterer's stock in trade. <i>(Sep 1985,NZ Journal of Agriculture,43</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> i was leaning on my hillpole in an attempt to push myself up. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,49</i>)

>  <b>MCRAE</b> The musterer's stick is known as a pole or hillstick or hillpole. Many of the mustering beats were on high and difficult mountain country with only hillpoles and hob-nailed boots for assistance. [hill pole] <i>(1998,On the Run,30</i>)

### hillside paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fenced hilly area set aside for grazing.

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> .. a station holder turned 800 Romney wethers on to a small hard fern-ridden hillside paddock. <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,33</i>)

>  <b>BEACH</b> We crossed the paddocks for six hundred yards until we came to a hillside paddock. From here we climbed up the hillside paddock for three hundred yards when we came to a fence. <i>(1993,Over 30 Years Pig Hunting,148</i>)

### hillside plough
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a single-furrow reversible plough especially designed to work on hillsides.

>  <b>HAWKiNS</b> A hillside plough appeared in 1884, digger ploughs about 1890, a mole plough for boring drains in 1893, and in the same year, a water-race plough which was dragged behind a traction engine or a dozen horses. <i>(1957,Beyond the Waimakariri,321</i>)

>  <b>MENZiES</b> The hillside-plough jumps and skids about in the first furrow. <i>(1970,Story of Menzies Bay, The,80</i>)

>  <b>EWERS</b> in the winter of 1943 i broke in twenty-two acres with a hillside plough. <i>(1993,Lifetime on the Land, A,34</i>)

### hillside ploughing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the using of a hillside plough to cultivate contoured land.

>  Tests are to be held next summer and one of them is to be in hillside ploughing. <i>(1918,Report,102</i>) [NOTES: First AGM General Council of Agric]

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Thurston to hillside ploughing on hillside next C's boundary. <i>(Aug 1936,Alexander Mowat Diaries,1</i>)

### hillsiding
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the using of a hillside plough to cultivate contoured land.

>  <b>MOWAT</b> The plowmen went hillsiding. <i>(May 1907,Alexander Mowat Diaries,6</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Had a look to see how Thurston getting on with hillsiding. <i>(Aug 1936,Alexander Mowat Diaries,1</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Thurston hillsiding until mid-day. <i>(Aug 1936,Alexander Mowat Diaries,8</i>)

### hillstick
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mustering stick. (See [hillpole; nibby; mustering stick; pole](../H#hillpole;-nibby;-mustering-stick;-pole))

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> hillstick: in Otago, a musterer's staff. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,42</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> When the [manuka] wood is dry, it's extremely strong, strong enough to spring you back from a heavy fall, and its smoothness and familiar curves make your hill stick much more than a mere piece of wood. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,15</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> The stick he's carrying is a 'nibby' or hill stick, made from a seasoned piece of straight manuka stripped of its bark. The hill stick is used as a sort of third leg when you're working on steep country - and occasionally to whack a disobedient dog. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,22</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> He was riding Niki, a bay mare, and across the pommel of his stock saddle he carried a stout manuka hillstick. [hill-stick] <i>(2001,Riding Country,134</i>)

>  <b>MCCASKiLL</b> Apart from his dogs, the musterer's most used piece of equipment was his hill-stick, a six-foot piece of manuka with a ferrule fitted at the end to reduce wear. <i>(1969,Molesworth,101</i>)

>  <b>MCRAE</b> The musterer's stick is known as a pole or hill-stick or hillpole 41 We suggested that he try giving her a small whack with a hillstick straight after the act had taken place. <i>(1998,On the Run,30</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> i'd act as berserk as possible, flapping my arms, scraping my hill stick wildly on the dirt or beating it on the fern, and all the time encouraging my dogs to bark. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,51</i>)

### hilly country
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) farmland that is broken and undulating to steep.

>  The hilly country of central and northern Taranaki, after it has been cleared of forest covering and partly grassed, shows a strong tendency to revert to fern and second growth. <i>(Mar 20, 1933,NZ Journal of Agriculture,154</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> On 22 February they reached the banks of the Moawhango River, and travelled steadily over hilly country broken occasionally by heavy bush ... <i>(1959,Great Days in NZ Exploration,39</i>)

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFiELD</b> The most obvious symptoms of soil erosion in hilly country are scarred hillsides due to slips, mass flowage and gullying. <i>(1961,Agriculture,251</i>)

### hinaki
 
 <i>n.</i> eel trap [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>iHiMAERA & LONG</b> Who will gaff the tuna stream.. / and collect the kina / kutai and pipi / pick the puha and bitter cress / set the hinaki <i>(1982,into the World of Light,196</i>)

>  They showed me, with kiekie, hignaki [sic] and stories what the river meant to them. <i>(Oct 1998,Mana,No. 24: 55</i>)

>  <b>58</b> ...it may be because he has captured an almost erotic blend of contemporary and traditional form, with echoes of Polynesian canoes or hinaki (eeltraps) detectable in these sinuous shapes. <i>(Jul 6, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Just before he'd left Okahu Bay one of Matapihi's cousins had given him an eel net, a hinaki, that he'd made. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,97</i>)

>  You know what a hinaki is? it's a thing that catches eels, eh. They also use that word for prison. <i>(Feb 1993,North & South,89</i>)

>  My empty stomach squirms like a hinaki full of eels when i realise she has heard everything i've said. <i>(Nov 18, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C4</i>)

>  The best is mingimingi, a vine that settlers amd gold-diggers would pull from thet trees to stuff their mattresses with or make hinake for catching eels. <i>(Dec 18, 2005,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),40</i>)

>  i dumped the hinaki in tall grass by a culvert. <i>(1952,Landfall,24 / 288</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> solitary confinement cell

>  Boobslang terms of Maori origin include .. <i>henake</i> for the <i>pound</i>, solitary confinement (lit. 'eeltrap'). <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 3</i>)

### hinau
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TOWNSEND</b> The principal forest trees of any value are red and white pine - Mano-ka - very hard wood - Bukkateer, very good floor boards - honeysuckle and Eno - from the bark of the last i have obtained a fine dye of deep scarlet colour; the wood is as hard as iron. <i>(Nov 16, 2004,Letter,8</i>) [NOTES: in <i>Absolutely Positively Wellington</i> 0/9/2004, p. 9]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> A shorthaired collie.. superb at bailing up wild cattle or Captain Cook pigs, plump from hinau berries. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,134</i>)

>  The hinaus' creamy raceme resembled [a] spray of lilies-of-the valley. <i>(Mar 16, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

### hinau cake
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ANGUS</b> The children soon afterwards began to cram themselves with hinau cakes, - a black, filthy mass, consisting of the fruit [24] of the hinau tree compressed together, and kept till quite rotten and musty, which they eat with avidity. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/23</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> One of the women of the pa ... presented my natives with two hinau cakes tied up in leaves. in former days this was a kind of food which was very much admired and considered most excellent ... it is of a dark olive color and it is of this that the saying is used, that no one has a right to awake his neighbour in the night except it be to feed him with hinau bread. <i>(Aug 1, 1850,Journal,7/115</i>)

### Hinenui-te-Po
 
 <i>n.</i> the Maori goddess of death and the underworld. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>ihimaera ed.</b> Hine-nui-te-po, the great woman of the night, is drawing me close at last. <i>(2007,Get on the Waka,143</i>) [NOTES: (Paula Morris)]

### Hinuera stone
 
 <i>n.</i> a soft stone from Hinuera much favoured in the 60s and thereabouts for house cladding, fireplaces, etc.

>  The palate of materials is earthy with good use of Hinuera Stone and natural aggeregates in the landscape. <i>(Nov 9, 2000,City Voice,12</i>)

### hip hole
 
 <i>n.</i> [(blasters')]

>  <b>McARA</b> The usual method of drilling was to start with the right-hand lifter, then the centre lifter, next the bottom right-hand cut hole, the knee hole, the stab (to break the outer part of the face and assist the cut) the bottom cut easer, then the hip hole, the right-hand cut easer, the two shoulder holes, the top right-hand cut hole, the top easer, the centre and right-hand back holes. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi,189</i>)

### hip over
 
 <i>v.</i> to switch a paddle from one side to the other

>  The hip over is the act of switching your paddle from one side to the other. <i>(Mar 26, 2003,Capital Times,5</i>)

### hip pocket
 
 <i>adj.</i> relating to monetary considerations

>  The "hip pocket" election has divided New Zealanders into two camps: Deserving families (60,000) who stand to benefit from Labour's tax relief package, and single or better-off people who will gain from National's plan to cut personal taxes (85 per cent will pay only 19c in the dollar). <i>(Sep 6, 2005,NZ Herald,D3</i>)

### hip spica
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Once traction was finished, Kathryn was fitted with a hip spica - a big plaster cast covering both hips - that she had to wear for three months. <i>(May 1, 2001,independent Herald (Wellington),4</i>)

### hip-rafter roof
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  i dropped across a couple of families who had camped together since February, under a hip-rafter roof, the lower side of which rested on the ground, and over which a few nikau palm leaves had been loosely spread. <i>(May 7, 1887,25 Years of Care,24</i>)

### hippah
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pa](../P#pa))

### hippie-tanga
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Baxter's Jerusalem commune ] was also one of the great attempts in our religious history to re-invent a relevant spirituality from a strange blend of Catholicism, Maoritanga, youth culture and hippie-tanga. <i>(Apr 5, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

### hippie-trippie
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  You walk into a setting and become - it sounds hippie-trippie - able to transform what's there because you've become part of it. <i>(Oct 1995,North & South,140</i>)

### His Ex
 
 <i>phr.</i> His Excellency, the Governor-General

>  <b>BAKER</b> The terms His Ex or the G.G. for our Governor-Generals have long since passed into colloquial speech. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,62</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> Ex, his. Also New Zealand, as is the G.G. (B., 1941.) <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1042</i>)

### His Satanic Majesty Afloat
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  He'd a toothsome little dainty, with the very vulgar name / Of "His Satanic Majesty Afloat". <i>(Sep 6, 1915,Stratford Evening Post,6</i>) [NOTES: From a poem about a station cook]

### hisser
 
 <i>n.</i> [(trampers') ca1940 (Tramping) (R. Mason) A little hisser or a hisser is one who travels at great speed without great effort. To hiss along is to tramp at a fast pace. Not heard since ca1945.]

>  Many trampers today like the flexibility of planning their own routes, of not having the pace set by others. Some are put off by the "hisser" mentality, which is apparently still alive in some clubs. Hissers sprint from point to point, seeing nothing. At the hut they bore everyone senseless with their boasts about record times. <i>(2006,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://masseynews.massey.ac.nz/magazine/stories/going_bush.html Accessed 1 Mar 2006.]

>  Visitors to Wellington comment on the brisk pace of the pedestrians, and Wellington trampers have achieved a certain notoriety for the speed with which some of them travel in the hills. it is not surprising, therefore, that its hillmen and hillwomen should hiss along, that hissers and little hissers should flourish, and that little hisser should sometimes be used as a term of approbation - `stew a hisser', `a real hisser of a day'. The Tramper first gives to hiss in 1934, and hisser with reference to a stew appears on 1940, but to a person not until 1943, though it must have been so used long before. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,29</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hisser term of approval, mostly among trampers, in such contexts as `a hisser of a day' or `a hisser of a stew'. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,56</i>)

### historic claim
 
 <i>n.</i> = Treaty claim

>  Tribunal director Morrie Love said the Waitangi Tribunal did not expect to finish reporting on historic Treaty claims till 2014 at the earliest, after the Government forced it to tighten its belt. <i>(Jul 8, 2000,Dominion,10</i>)

>  it is very clear that the Government policy... [is] it will not entertain oil and gas as part of historic claims. <i>(Oct 19, 2000,Evening Post,9</i>)

### historic hut
 
 <i>n.</i> each of three buildings erected on Ross island in Antarctica during the pioneering expeditions (of Scott and Shackleton) of the early 20th century.

>  [Hdg] Maintenance of Historic Huts in Antarctica. Between 1902 and 1911, three prefabricated huts were constructed on Ross island during expeditions led by Robert Falcon Scott and Ernest Shackleton. <i>(1978,NZ Antarctic Record,1(1) : 10</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> any of a large number of older back-country huts built for mustering, tramping, etc.

>  The back country of New Zealand contains a heritage of about 1400 huts that is unequalled anywhere in the world. These huts were built for the following reasons: wild animal control (~750) sheep mustering (~500) recreation (~150) a range of other purposes including prospecting, scientific studies and castaway sustenance. Others began life as schools and homes and later became huts. These huts have heritage value as the only physical remnant of these activities. ... DOC is identifying a representative selection of key historic huts that can be managed long term to conserve this heritage and allow its appreciation. it is intended to have interpretation signs provided for each historic hut. <i>(2009,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.doc.govt.nz/conservation/historic/historic-heritage-topics/huts]

### historic place
 
 <i>n.</i> a building or site of historical significance as scheduled by the Historic Places Trust.

>  A chunk of concrete pipe from the construction of Mid Canterbury's Rangitata diversion race 65 years ago has been listed by the New Zealand Historic Places Trust as a category one historic place. <i>(May 4, 2005,NZ Herald</i>)

### historical claim
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of historic claim = Treaty claim

>  Petroleum, said the prime minister on October 20, 2000, should be dealt with as a contemporary, not historical claim. <i>(May 27, 2003,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### historical settlement
 
 <i>n.</i> the settlement of a historic (Treaty) claim

>  On the opening day of the petroleum hearings in 2000, the chairman was told by the ministers that the Government was not prepared to un-stitch other "full and final" historical settlements no matter what the tribunal decided. <i>(May 27, 2003,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Graffiti Speak... Hit: to tag up any surface with paint or ink. <i>(Nov 30, 2000,Evening Post,19</i>)

 <i>v.</i> to join; (? take up a job)

>  <b>MONEY</b> The wages were trifling, but as i was curious, i hitched on. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,104</i>)

### hitout
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He's looking really good but if he went to Hastings he would have to have a couple of hard hitouts and that would risk flattening him. <i>(Sep 19, 2003,Dominion Post RaceForm,2</i>)

### hiwi
 
 <i>n.</i> a grass

>  <b>POLACK</b> The marshes were filled with a wiry grass called hiwi. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/142</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Galaxias</i> spp., inanga, whitebait.

>  <i>Galaxias attenuatus </i>(Jennyns). MiNNOW; HiWi or iNANGA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,13,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> MiNNOW or WHiTEBAiT (iNANGA) <i>Austrocobitis attenuatus</i> (Jenyns) ... For a long time it has been known to the Maori and to Europeans that our New Zealand Whitebait is the young of a native freshwater fish commonly known as Minnow, or inanga or Hiwi to the Maori, and erroneously called a Trout. ... The early settlers called it Maori Trout, Native Trout, Rock Trout and Minnow. But it is not a Trout. Nor is it a Minnow, through it resembles the English freshwater Minnow. While the name Trout is not correct, it is and will always be known as such by farmers and naturalists. in the Bay of Plenty, Whitebait is known to the Maori as Porohoe. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,118,2nd ed.</i>)

### hiwihiwi
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Chironemus marmoratus</i>, a fish.: an olive-brown sea-fish brindled with green, commonest in far northern waters [Maori]; (northern) kelpfish, goffy, also called goffy, kelp fish.

>  More sedentary are red moki, goatfish, marblefish and solitary hiwihiwi, tucked into cracks. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/20</i>)

>  HAPLODACTYLUS FERGUSSONi. sp.nov. Native name - Hiwihiwi. <i>(1875,TrNZi,7/243</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  CHiRONEMUS FERGUSSONi ... Native name - Hiwi-hiwi. <i>(1877,TrNZi,9/467</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  <b>SHRRiN</b> HiWi HiWi (<i>Chironemus Fergussoni</i>). ... it is highly prized by the Natives as a food fish, who say it is principally caught about the East Cape and Tologa [sic] Bay. ... They describe it as a `rock fish', freckled, with white spots, ... <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,45</i>)

>  <i>2. Mokohinou islands</i>. - Hapuku (or groper), hiwihiwi, barracouta, maomao, spotty, parrot-fish, leather jacket, korokoro-pounamou. <i>ibid.</i>24/206 4. Hiwihiwi - Chironemus fergusssoni. This fish is only once recorded, a single specimen having been taken at Mokohinou in September, 1887. <i>(1892,TrNZi,24/203</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  The hiwihiwi (<i>Chironemus fergussoni</i>), tupuku, ngutere, tangahangaha, and koeaea (<i>Corridodax pullus </i>Forster) (butterfish), are amongst those caught <i>(1926,TrNZi,58/646</i>) [NOTES: (Buck)]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Chironemus marmoratus</i> Guenther. KELPFiSH; HiWiHiWi. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,38,1971 ed.</i>)

>  hiwi hiwi n [Maori] : a small marine spiny-finned food fish (<i>Chironemus fergussoni</i>) of New Zealand <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1075</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> KELPFiSH ... <i>Other names: Chironemus marmoratus</i>; hiwihiwi, ngakoikoi (Maori). <i>(1988,Sea Anglers' Guide,250</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> KELPFiSH [<i>Chironemus marmoratus</i>] ... Other names include the Maori hiwihiwi and ngakoikoi. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,26</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family CHEiLODACTYLiDAE. [The] goffy or hiwihiwi or northern kelp fish, <i>Chironemus marmoratus</i>, an olive-brown fish brindled with green, red or brown, is commonest in the far north; <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,222</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> HiWiHiWi Maori name for Kelpfish. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,120</i>)

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> HiWiHiWi (Kelpfish) <i>Chironemus marmoratus</i> ... Hiwihiwi are most abundant on the north-east coast of the North island between North Cape and East Cape but stragglers are found as far south as Cook Strait. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,239,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] hiwihiwi 1548-53 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### Hobart
 
 <i>n.</i> we communicate/correspond with (post to) Hobart, (Melbourne), a euphemism for `Tatts tickets sold here'; to post to, to be in touch with Hobart, to sell (then illegal) tickets in Tattersall's lottery; notices in tobacconists' etc. shop windows: `We post to Hobart' to sell tickets (illegally) in Tattersall's Tasmanian Lottery; see also Tatts, ticket. [obs.]

>  <b>BRASCH</b> A little news-agent's window on Plimmer Steps, ... bore the same message in its oracular pronouncement that We Communicate Weekly with Hobart. <i>(1980,indirections,408</i>) [NOTES: [1940's]]

>  `We communicate with Hobart every week' [heading] <i>(Jan 1951,Here & Now,25</i>)

>  their realisation staked on the/ still intangible ticket from Hobart./ <i>(Jan 1952,Here & Now,31</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The Golden Kiwi was established partly to prevent money being sent out of New Zealand for tickets in Tatt's, a Tasmanian lottery with a name abbreviated from Tattersall's and quite unconnected with the English cant word tatts `dice'. it was illegal to sell tickets in Tatt's in New Zealand but many barbers' shops displayed a notice, `We communicate with Hobart', which was universally understood.... it was clear that in the context of a barber's shop window the notice meant `we sell tickets in Tatt's', but legally a man can announce that he writes to Tasmania. Newspapers might report the good fortune of winners but the illegal source of the winnings would not be specified. The money came from `an overseas consultation'. <i>(1966,Austral English,175</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### hobnail
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to walk (See [hobnails](../H#hobnails),[ride one's; hobnail express](../R#ride-one's;-hobnail-express),[go by](../G#go-by))

>  <b>MCMiLLAN</b> .. we hobnailed our way round the height. <i>(2000,Country Bloke and Other Verse, The,229</i>)

### hobnail track
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Tales told of a route to the west, that had been the barefoot track of the Maori seekers of greenstone in ancient times, and the hobnail track of the seekers of gold in the days of the West Coast rush, had so intrigued Arthur Sykes and me ... as to cause us to plan, ... , on carrying our swags up over the range ... <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,263</i>)

### hobnails
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) farm work-boots with metal studs fastened to the soles, suitable for working on hill slopes.

>  <b>DAViS</b> Mud squelching under our hobnails, we floundered through the soggy darkness of the paddocks. <i>(1962,Mutton on the Menu,121</i>)

>  <b>STAPLES</b> Peterson rode his horse and Shiny rode his hobnails. <i>(1964,Cooks and Shepherds Come Away,42</i>)

>  <b>DAViS</b> We climbed the boundary fence and our hobnails bit into the smooth-stalked danthonia grass of the lower slopes of the Kitchener Flat. <i>(1962,Mutton on the Menu,144</i>)

### hobo
 
 <i>n.</i> in NZ after WW1, often applied to a rough-and-ready fellow; and also used ?humorously) ca1947; as a term of familiar address to a group of companions: `Your hoboes ready?' (? cf bo).

>  <b>EADDY</b> ... there's a hobo up there who cant lay out a yard. <i>ibid.</i> 116 Wilhelm, the German hobo, had been disrated to boy for bad steering. <i>(1933,Hull Down,103</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> Hobo, pl Hoboes. 3. in New Zealand and Australia, in post-G.W. days, it is often applied to a rough-and-ready fellow. The etymology remains a puzzle: see esp. irwin, who quotes a tramp's C. 20 distinction: `Bums loafs and sits. Tramps loafs and walks. But a hobo moves and works, and he's clean.' <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,394</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> [refce c1920s] Sitting opposite were several typical Fielding [sic] hobos, each perhaps but certainly not in the studbook [sic] ... One of the hobos gurgled, and said, `Want to make an impression on that pretty icicle, ...'. Jim quietly replied `Thanks for your advice, but i do not want any information from `hobos' nor even from `quadroons'.' `Hell! call me a half-caste?' flushed the main offender, sitting opposite. ... `Dad is very taken with you, and with the fact that you skittled those Maori hobos has tremendously advanced your stocks. ...' <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,185. 195</i>)

### Hobson's choice
 
 <i>n.</i> Auckland, as Hobson's choice of capital, by NZ Company supports, esp. in Wellington. [applied by first settlers, usu. derog., to various settlements: a play on Governor William Hobson's name + the phrase meaning no choice at all.]

>  <b>WOOD</b> An Auckland paper rests [17] the prosperity of that place on a firmer foundation. `... The entrances and exits to `Hobson's choice' are not circumscribed. <i>(1843,Twelve Months in Wellington,16</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> `Hobson's Choice' as Auckland was very generally called. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,2/28</i>)

>  [Col. Gazette heading] `Hobson's Choice' <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/44/237</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Nelson

>  Hobson ... limited the choice for the [New Zealand Company] settlement to Blind Bay ... Hence Nelson was for years [from 1841] known as `Hobson's Choice'. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,67</i>)

### Hobson's folly
 
 <i>n.</i> Auckland, as capital: a coinage (?) by Chapman or the NZ Company Port Nicholson colonists. (See [Hobson's choice](../H#hobson's-choice))

>  the Hobson's folly. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,23/290</i>)

### Hochstetter's frog
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The group was anxious to stop the fungus spreading to the other three native species, particularly Hochstetter's frog, which shares habitat with Archey's frog. <i>(Oct 3, 2001,Dominion,16</i>)

>  it is understood most concerns centre on its threat to the small hochstetter's frog, one of New Zealand's rarest creatures, which also lives in the Waitakere Ranges. <i>(Nov 16, 1999,Dominion,11</i>)

>  The Hochstetter's frog is now found only in the upper half of the North island and Archey's frog only on the Coromandel and at one site near Te Kuiti. <i>(Nov 2005,Open Space,22</i>)

>  Modelled on the native hochstetter frog <i>Leiopelma hochstetteri</i> found in the Waitakere Ranges, Frogg was aimed at youngsters. <i>(Jul 1991,Metro,71</i>)

>  Hochstetter frogs - <i>Leiopelma hochstetteri</i> - a rare native species, were still present in the area, but, each year, in warm, humid, cloudy, windless night conditions the frogs migrated across the road, and many were killed by traffic. <i>(Jun 17, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  it is also habitat to nationally endangered wildlife species including the North island brown kiwi, the Norht island kaka, Hochstetter's frog and Archey's frog. <i>(Mar 2005,Open Space,No 63 p10</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [(prison slang)]

### hockey club
 
 <i>n.</i> a venereal hospital, cf. hockey stick [(WW1 hist.) slang]

### hockey stick
 
 <i>n.</i> a tree planted too shallow, allowing roots to club over in the shape of a hockey stick. ca1954 NZ Forest Service coll. [(forestry)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a mutton chop [(chiefly station). ca1941 Nelson College Boarders (W.Brownlee)]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Mutton chops, or hockey sticks as they are generally known [among musterers]. <i>(1946,Wayleggo,114</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Watty [the Tarndale cook] fed us again, chops and more chops. He called them `hockey-stocks'[sic: ? misprint hockey-stick]; but there was never anything in the world more toothsome than that merino mutton, killed a week previously and hung in the clear, rarefied air of the Tarndale uplands. <i>ibid.</i>32 We were tired, and ate our two `hockey sticks' in silence, and big lunches, expecting a hard day. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,4</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Hockey Sticks: Musterers [sic] name for mutton chops. <i>(1972,Mountain World,310</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Hockey Stick. Mutton rib chops popular for breakfast. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,96</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hockey sticks hogget chops. [Shepard] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,56</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an instrument used in the treatment of VD, cf. hockey club. [(WW2)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He had listened to Jock, who had the grave misfortune to contract V.D. on final leave, giving his celebrated account of how the MO had thrust an instrument nicknamed the hockey stick into him. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,68</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mutton chop [staple mustering breakfast food.] (See [three-six-five](../T#three-six-five))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Mutton chops, or hockey sticks as they are generally known, are day after day and month after month, the back country man's breakfast. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,114</i>)

>  She preferred rib chop, she didn't mind leg chop, but she did not want any more hockey sticks. <i>(Sep 1, 1949,NZ Farmer,60</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. i had never met him but we had 'chewed hockey sticks' in some of the same mustering camps and i was hoping i'd run into him. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North island,89</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> These [mustering] gangs were made up of men who'd taken up the "burnt chops", ate "hockey sticks", wore Bill Masseys, slept in a scratcher and rode pigskin..hockey sticks are chops.. <i>(1983,Other Side of Farming, The,1</i>)

>  <b>MARTiN</b> The musterers would be up very early to be on the 'tops' at daylight. The musterer's breakfast consisted of a couple of mutton chops (known as 'hockey sticks') ... <i>(1990,Forgotten Worker, The,63</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Watty fed us again, chops and more chops. He called them 'hockey-stocks.' <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,4</i>)

>  .. no matter what meal it was, the killers would have contributed. Breakfast: hockey sticks and cackleberries ( chops and eggs.) <i>(Mar 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,19</i>)

### hod
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Sa-eeda Ahmed, Akers, arrak,/ Bints, Bomonti. backsheech, barrak;/ Centipedes and Cleopatra,/ Dust and desert, djinns and Fatima;/ `Eggs-cook, ana miskeen,'/ Fever, flies, and felaheen;/ Gehenam, Galabeyah, gods,/ Harems, hooshters, heat and hods;/ `igri wallah, imshi Y'allah.'/ Jackos, jackals, `gala gala';/ Khamseen, kan kan, tala hena,/ Lice and locusts, love and Lena;/ Mirage, Mahomet, mosquistoe,/ Niggers, T.A.B. and C2;/ Olives oranges, askarie./ Peanuts, pyramids, and gharry;/ `Queens' and `Quacks' and `k'weis kateer,'/ Ruins, rum, and Gyppo beer;/ Scorpion, sand, sun, sin, and Scarab,/ Tibbin, taube, and tricky Arab;/ Whizz-bangs, wadis, `yin-al-deenak,'/ Yids, and yesterday, yeh meenak;/ Kestrels, kingfishers, and kites,/ Torrid days and sleepless nights;/ Mosques and minarets--maleesh;/ `O'ariglak,' `feluce--mafeesh'--/ ookra, badein, ack-ack-emma,/ The soldier's farewell--ma-es salemma; <i>(Jul 10, 1919,Quick March,33</i>)

### hoe in(to)
 
 <i>v.</i> to attack vigorously (esp. an opponent; or food); to get stuck in (to).

>  ...i was just so proud of the way the guys hoed into it. <i>(Apr 25, 2004,Sunday Times (NZ),B2</i>)

>  The policeman's friend then headbutted the mate and the policeman himself "hoed into another mate." <i>(Jan 11, 2001,Dominion,3</i>)

>  <b>BOLiTHO</b> i was `hoeing in' [to tucker]. <i>(1918,Book of the C1 Camp, Tauherenikau,9</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> i'm taking the [rugby team] for some road work. They didn't hoe in enough last night so i'm taking them on a good long run <i>ibid.</i> 161 He gives me the tat-tas so i hoed into him - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,115</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> Rationing ... did not make things easier [during WW2], but everyone `hoed in' as we say, and made the best of it. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,117</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hoe in(to) to do something vigorously, like eating or work; of agricultural origin; eg `Jack was starving and, without waiting for grace to finish, started hoeing into the hockey sticks.' c. 1920. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,56</i>)

>  finished drafting wethers which were put accross [sic] Wedderburn ... Number including hogs about 1540 <i>(Nov 11, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a gen. term usu. used to describe young pigs. May be used for castrates or barrows but not specific (American) (CCR notes: US not young: a domestic swine weighing more than 120lbs).

>  Hog: General term usually used to describe young pigs. May be used for castrates or barrows but not specific (American). <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,45</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(secondary schools') ca1951 Nelson College (and possibly other boarding schools) -->1960 16C F B1 Nelson Girls C. 22A CHEWS (ALSO HOG) -->1960 16C F B1 Nelson Girls C. 22A HOG [2 occurrences]]



 
 <i>n.</i> a shilling piece [obs. ca1930 Havelock.: occasionally used, either hog or ogg.]

 <i>n.</i>

>  in the larger modern mills, many of the pieces are used as "hoggings". They are thrown down a chute called a "hog," in the bottom of which are a number of knives revolving at high speed. <i>(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),27</i>)

### hog weed
 
 <i>n.</i> wireweed

>  <b>HiLGENDORF</b> WiREWEED (<i>Polygonum aviculare</i>), also called road weed, mat weed, knot grass, [pig weed,] and hog weed, is common in cultivated land all over the country. it is an annual, with stems up to 2ft. in length trailing over the ground. The stems have swollen nodes (`knot grass') and are very tough (`wireweed'). <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,61,1967 ed.</i>)

>  The news from Britain that hogweed or cow parsnip as it is more commonly called, has been added to the list ... The New Zealand Weed and Pest Control society in recent publication `Standard common Names For weeds in New Zealands' records the name cow parsnip without giving the specific name. This plant could be H. sphondlylium a native of the British isle and at one time widely advocated as a suitable stock food because of its thick fleshy tap root. Hogweed in New Zealand is a popular descriptive name for four different weeds. Two of these, namely the twin cress and wart cress, belong to the same genus. The other two are the wild portulaca and wire weed, which are in no way related. The popular names of the latter illustrate how confusing the use of names like fire weed or wandering willie can be as a means of identifying weeds and plants. These common names of plants frequently have only local significance and in cases may even become obsolete. For this reason botanical names are ... <i>(Jul 23, 1970,Dominion,15/1-3</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> wild portulaca [[NB: purslane is usu. in dicts difft plant f. iceplant.]]

>  <b>HiLGENDORF</b> PORTULACA (Portulaca oleracea), called also pig weed, hog weed, purslane, and ice plant, is very common on garden paths etc., in Auckland, becoming less and less common as far south as Nelson. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,72,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HiLGENDORF</b> PORTULACA (<i>Portulaca oleracea</i>), called also pig weed, hog weed, purslane, and ice plant, is very common on garden paths etc., in Auckland, becoming less and less common as far south as Nelson. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,72,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>hogweed [=] twin cress: wart cress: wild portulaca: wireweed [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,38</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Heracleum</i> spp (esp. <i>H. mantegazzianum</i>), cow parsnip.

 <i>adj.</i>
