### hogback
 
 <i>n.</i> [see also hogsback/hogback]

>  <b>BUiCK</b> The larger of the adzes, 9 1/2 inches long, was made of a hard, fine-grained black stone commonly used for this purpose ... in shape it was what is known as the `hog-back' type, and had been brought to a high degree of finish. <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,204</i>)

### Hogburn head
 
 <i>n.</i> [(Otago mining, ?obs.)]

>  A `Hogburn head' is a stream of water passing through an orifice 16' by one inch, and running for the working day of eight hours ... . the Government head ... . is equal to 2.5 times the Hogburn head ... . and flows for 24 hours. <i>(1872,AJHR,G/4/29</i>) [NOTES: Ulrich Report Goldfields Otago]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Soon after the diggings got going properly the Hog Burn was made a sludge channel. The old pastoral interests had to give way before the new mining interests. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,171</i>) [NOTES: (Otago mining, ?obs.)]

>  <b>COWAN</b> From January 11 to March 11, 1870, the miners of Naseby went on strike, and finally induced the `water squatters' to lower their rates from five pounds a `Hogburn' head to two pounds. [fn] A `Hogburn' head was the stream of water which would run through a box 17 inches wide by 1 inch in depth with 5 inches of pressure. - J.J.T., `Otago Daily Times,' July 25, 1932. <i>(1948,Down the Years in the Maniototo,43</i>)

### hogger
 
 <i>n.</i> a sawmill mincing machine

>  <b>BiRD</b> The floor underneath contained the drive-shafts and belts that drove the mill, together with the hogger that minced up waste wood for feeding to hungry boilers.<i>ibid.</i> 42. When additional sawdust is required sawmill slabs are fed into the 'hogger' which minces them up for use as fuel. <i>(1998,Viaducts against the Sky,32</i>)

### hogget
 
 <i>n.</i> in NZ used widely on and off (esp. for the meat) the farm (cf hog n1.) (note that animal and meat definitions differ); a) a young sheep between weaning (4 months) and first shearing(14-16 months). b) meat from a yearling sheep between 14 and 24 months. [(farming)]

>  <b>ELDRED-GRiGG</b> The hogget and vegetables, served with gravy and mint sauce, were quickly cleared from our plates and the last traces scraped and then licked off. <i>(1995,Mum,31</i>)

>  His visual sense records the black singlets, the stretched cords and arms of the shearing blades, the scramble of the crewcut hogget to get back upright. <i>(1973,islands,6/360</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Names. - Some of the words used to define the sheep were different then, and we read of `hogs' or `wedder hogs,' and `hoggets' or `ewe hoggets.' A hog (or hogg) was a yearling wether, a hogget a yearling ewe. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,85</i>) [NOTES: [1858-1869] Puketoi Station Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 85]

>  [first use - previously hog - may represent a different journal keeper] Cut and tailed 130 Ewe ... lambs drafted off Hoggits - tally of whole flock ... 1813. <i>(Aug 30, 1860,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  At the Dunedin Exhibition (1859) the second prize was awarded to the three ewe hoggets shown by Mr. Grigg, ... the first prize being awarded to a fully matured English ewe. <i>(Oct 26, 1865,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: 1865 Oct 26 Nelson Examiner in 1952 Stevens John Grigg 15]

>  Ewe hoggets (5 in pen) [pounds]6-15-0 to [pounds]7-10-0 each. <i>(Dec 14, 1865,Southern Cross (Wgton)</i>) [NOTES: 1865 Dec 14 Southern Cross in 1952 Stevens John Grigg 17]

>  <b>PHiLLiPS</b> [the first use i have noticed in either journal : lamb - wether, ewe, ram frequent] All hands mustering Mount Misery - took hoggets to woolshed <i>(Dec 29, 1868,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,172</i>)

>  Daging and doseing 530 hoggets. <i>(Jun 8, 1894,Chudleigh Diary,386,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Hogget See Ages of sheep. <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The,17</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>AYSON</b> The wethers were out on the Waiwera hills, and the hoggets ran near the homestead. <i>(1937,Pioneering in Otago,41</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Some of the words used to define the sheep were different then [1859] and we read of `hogs' or `wedder hogs', and `hoggets' or `ewe hoggets.' A `hog' (or hogg) was a yearling wether, a hogget a yearling ewe. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,85</i>) [NOTES: (quoting Roberts Diary 1859)]

>  <b>newton</b> HOGGET: A sheep at the stage between weening and when it gets its two teeth, i.e., from eight months to eighteen months of age. <i>(1949,High Country Days,195,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LiNKLATER</b> i have seen their admirable mutton brought upon the table in such miserable shape that the hogget - so they call a sheep of uncertain age - appeared to have been killed by a bomb. <i>(1953,Year of Space, A,207,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> This in turn led to a dearth of suitable winter pasture country for hoggets. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,35</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Hogget: Lamb after weaning until shorn. <i>(1972,Mountain World,310</i>)

>  The products from this type of farm are wool, some store lambs, a few fat wether lambs, some culled hoggets, store cattle, varying numbers of store or fat wethers, and a draft of `cast-for-age' (c.f.a.) ewes. <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,59</i>)

>  ~ 8 temporary teeth - centre pair of permanent teeth start to erupt about 12 months... Hogget: Young sheep between weaning (4 months old) and 14-16 months of age. The hogget stage usually ends when the animals are shorn (around 14 months old). There are ram hoggets, ewe hoggets and wether hoggets. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,50</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> We will be on hoggets all day. They are big and well wooled, but will be good cutting. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,75</i>)

>  hogget ... NZ lamb from when it is weaned to its first shearing. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,356</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 532 & 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 234]

>  <b>BURTON</b> New Zealanders are alone in distinguishing hogget from lamb or mutton. Lamb is the meat of any animal from six weeks until it grows its first two teeth, from which time it becomes hogget. When a full set of teeth is grown, the meat from the sheep becomes mutton. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,104</i>)

>  Each year, he buys in 1000 replacement ewes as hoggets amd two-tooths, going out of his way to source the best genetics. <i>(Nov 13, 2003,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Ages of sheep. Although there are many variations, the following names are usually used: A sheep is a lamb until weaned. After weaning a lamb is called a hogget until shorn. A male lamb is a ram lamb until castrated, then a wether. A female lamb is a ewe lamb. After shearing, a ewe or ram lamb is usually called a shorn hogget until cutting the first two teeth. it is then called a two-tooth. Some use the term two-tooth as soon as the lamb is shorn. Next year the sheep is a four-tooth, then a six-tooth and then an eight-tooth or full-mouthed sheep. it is full-mouthed as long as the teeth remain perfect but then becomes broken-mouthed and gummy <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)

>  i got two bob a week pocket money and for that, i had to feed the chooks, collect firewood, dag the hoggets, dynamite the swamp and drench the bulls. <i>(Nov 2001,Cuisine,132</i>) [NOTES: Daryl Crimp]



 
 <i>n.</i> a hogget carcass comes from a sheep between 12 and 24 months of age, i.e. from yearling sheep. -->2. (butchers') Of meat from hogget; but often as a general (or euphemistic) term for mutton (on hotel menus, etc) (R. Mason).

>  <b>SiMON</b> Behind me, Kitty fusses, taking a leg of hogget out of the freezer for tonight's dinner, rinsing the morning tea dishes, adding things to her shopping list, humming. <i>(1979,Harper's Mother,155</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> `How's this hogget wool going, Bill?' `First class wool, just first class but it's tender - fleece after fleece. it's heartbreaking.' ... He plucked a specimen of the fine wool from the tender bin ... and gave a sharp tug. The wool parted along a line of weakness in its growth. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,5</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The purchase included 421 goats - 105 bucks and the rest does carrying kids and hoggets. <i>(Aug 11, 1986,Dominion,14</i>)

### hogget block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) hilly land where hoggets are grazed with little management.

>  The hogget block on Tara Hills consists of four faces, two sunny and two dark. <i>(Nov 21, 1963,NZ Farmer,54</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... the former, of 4,000 acres, is the hogget block and although i only saw it from a distance it looked to be warm sweet country. <i>(1972,in the Wake of the Axe,35</i>)

>  <b>DREAVER</b> The really big days were when we mustered the Hogget Block for the summer shearing. <i>(1994,Catlins River Wisp Run,57</i>)

>  The hogget block on the property of Messrs Evans is fairly steep. <i>(Jun 7, 1951,NZ Farmer,12</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... Grays Hills itself is now the hogget block. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,44</i>)

### hogget country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) hilly land suitable for hoggets or dry stock requiring little management. (See [hogget block](../H#hogget-block))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> This block, which is fenced off from the high tops, is the station's hogget country. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,158</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Ewe and hogget country is fairly safe all year round, winter wether country is safe for that time of the year and summer wether country is safe only in the warmer months, when there's little chance of heavy snow. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,46</i>)

>  <b>PLUMMER</b> ... it was in good grass, no scrub and was good hogget country. <i>(1994,From Drover to Director,60</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> .. to drive them up past the Clarence accommodation house, and up the Aacheron Valley for ten to twelve miles till we joined another valley to the right leading up into the Alma hogget country. <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,30</i>)

### hogget ill-thrift
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  This term `hogget ill thrift' ... refer[s] ... to lambs that waste away and die in the midst of plenty ... The fundamental reason has not yet been uncovered. [OEDS-omit] <i>(Jul 31, 1973,Weekly News,38/3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a seasonal metabolic or malnutritional disease of hoggets.

>  <b>DU FAUR</b> The disease known as hogget ill-thrift is fairly widespread throughout New Zealand, and occurs mainly over the late autumn and early winter months. <i>(1966,Sheep Farming for Profit,135</i>)

>  <b>HiNDMARSH</b> Lambs went off fat to the works while the talk round about was of hogget ill thrift. [hogget ill thrift] <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,197</i>)

>  <b>EDGAR</b> .. they [calves] are liable to suffer from an ill-thrift condition similar to that in sheep known as Hogget illthrift. <i>(1959,NZ Farmers' Veterinary Guide,14</i>)

>  Hogget ill-thrift is still common. <i>(1970,Department of Agriculture Report,13</i>) [NOTES: Report: Farming in Hawke's Bay]

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> By the time Peter reached hoggethood it was clear that he was going to be an immense animal. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,193,1963 ed.</i>)

### hogging
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  in the larger modern mills, many of the pieces are used as "hoggings". They are thrown down a chute called a "hog," in the bottom of which are a number of knives revolving at high speed. <i>(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2990 : 27</i>)

### hogsback
 
 <i>n.</i> a storm cloud

>  Hogsbacks were forming as we left the cave at 8.30 am. <i>(1974,NZ Alpine Journal,27/45</i>)

>  At dusk i observed many of the dreaded "hogsbacks" rolling across the Divide. <i>(1934,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,iii/64</i>)

>  When we had seen a "hogsback" (storm-cloud) the day before we had not grabbed it by the tail. <i>(1936,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,V/55</i>)

>  Before darkness had settled in the river many evil-looking "hog's backs" had taken up their stations in the lurid sky. <i>(1936,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,V/73</i>)

### hogsback/hogback
 
 <i>n.</i> 1. a long, sharply crested ridge. 2. a similar shape, especially a cloud heralding a northwest storm in the Si mountains. [(chiefly Cant. mountaineers and trampers)]

>  ... dark clouds ... . a bevy of `hog-backs' <i>ibid.</i> 235 ... hog's back <i>(Jun 1933,NZ Alpine Journal,V/20/180</i>)

>  At sunset the silence of the evening was broken by one of the party dashing into the hut with the cry, `hog's back,' the dreaded harbinger of a N.W. storm. <i>(1934,NZ Alpine Journal,V/21/336</i>)

>  <b>GiLKiNSON</b> ... he showed me a hogsback and told me what it meant. [fn] Term applied to a particularly unwelcome cloud only too well-known to climbers, and almost invariably heralding a north-west storm. An unpleasant sight - but a valuable weather sign. <i>(1940,Peaks, Packs & Mountain Tracks,25</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> The most infallible warnings of a nor' wester are the very high clouds of a peculiar torpedo shape, with a hard, rounded upperside like a whale's back - `hog backs' is the usual name among alpine men. <i>(1945,Camping and Bushcraft in NZ,39</i>)

>  it is scarcely neccessary to mention the well-known hogsbacks, those torpedo-shaped clouds which form on the east side of the Southern Alps during a nor'wester and presage bad weather in the ranges, so [28] named by the late A.P. Harper. ... <i>ibid.</i> 40 From our camp on the grassy meadows some battled their way up to the snowline, retreating pellmell when they came face to face with a herd of hogsbacks; ... <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,27</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Hogsbacks are torpedo-shaped clouds in the southern [sic] Alps during a nor'wester. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,161</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dark cloud with a rounded top that heralds bad weather in S.i. high country areas.

>  <b>GiLKiSON</b> He showed me a hogsback ..Term applied to a particularly unwelcome cloud only too well-known to climbers, and almost invariably heralding a north-west storm. <i>(1940,Peaks, Packs & Mountain Tracks,24</i>)

>  <b>VANCE</b> its distinctive cloud formation, the lenticular alto-cumulus 'hogback,' has become the warning signal to mountaineers as well as musterers, and the 'nor'west arch' is one of the features of Canterbury. <i>(1965,High Endeavour,41</i>)

>  <b>ASPiNALL</b> i have many vivid memories of storms that would start with the wellknown hogsback clouds in the sky ... [hogback] <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,74</i>)

>  <b>ASPiNALL</b> Even a warm sunny day can show signs of a coming storm when 'hogsbacks', which are large dark clouds with rounded tops, appear over the head of a valley. <i>(1976,High Country Sheep Station,54</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a spur or ridge of a hill so shaped, cf razor back, whale back; a kind of hill-ridge, cf pigroot. [(chiefly station)]

>  <b>1958</b> Wills and i started for `Hog's Back' ridge, a detached group of the Malvern Hills. <i>(Jan 25, 1849,Torlesse Papers,50</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS & REEVES</b> Only think how much more graceful all the landscape will appear. / No more pigroots, whalebacks, hogbacks - these names that shall not last; / ... Negro-heads shall be re-christened. i for one will take no heed, / While to me there yet remaineth Negrohead, the fragrant weed. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,3</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> On the fifth day Walt climbed to a hog-back ... and looked down on the shapeless gullies <i>(1905,Sons o' Men,14</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> The right and half the centre company of Canterbury in the hollow were exposed ..., but on the hogsback of high open ground further west, the inner flank [was] ... raked by machine gun fire from Gird. <i>(1921,NZ Division,112</i>)

>  Hogsback. Spur or ridge of that shape. A common local name both here and in English [?England]. (G.P. W.) <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The,17</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

### hogshop
 
 <i>n.</i> a tuckshop [(secondary schools') 1960 16C F B1 Nelson Girls C. 22A HOG-SHOP -->1960 18C M B11 Nelson Boys C. 22A HOGSHOP] (See [hog](../H#hog))

### hoha
 
 <i>adj.</i> fed up, bored; being a nuisance [ORIGIN: Maori, ca1963 Vincent O'Sullivan p.c. Used frequently by Northland Maori children in sense `fed up']

>  So now we sit in the front pews, wanting to be fed, and trying to contain our hoha children. <i>(Mar 1999,Tui Motu interislands,18</i>)

>  Mum was really hoha about that. <i>(Jun 1998,Mana,No. 22: 35</i>)

>  if his cousin didn't get so hoha about all the rellies asking for a hair-cut, Hona Kingi would have what he wants by now. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 11 : 79</i>)

>  Sometimes i get very hoha with my whanaunga and their attitudes towards simple tikanga, te reo or even the Treaty. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 14 : 26</i>)

>  May i inform those people that the Te Teko community is hoha with two-faced rubbish that has obviously become a cycle? <i>(Sep 20, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,2</i>)

>  & let's just say it was good / to sit & smoke / & talk there / & not be hoha. <i>(Jun 18, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> After breakfast felt so extremely hoha that i accompanied Mr. Baker up the river for an airing. <i>(Sep 3, 1834,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,388,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> ... what a waste of a good sofa it was, what a waste of a good house for those two nuisance things. i hope when i have kids they won't be so hoha. <i>(1975,NZ Short Stories,186</i>) [NOTES: 1972 Grace `A Way of Talking' in 1975 NZ Short Stories iii 186]

>  [tiresome, nuisancy] - Hey! When you coming to pick up your hoha kids! They're wrecking the place! <i>(1984,Some Other Country,159</i>) [NOTES: 1972 ihimaera `Game of Cards' in 1984 Some Other Country 159]

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> `Why didn't Tuku come for it?' `How do i know? too hoha i suppose.' <i>(1978,Glory and the Dream, The,190</i>)

>  <b>MiTCALFE</b> Riki ... was tripping and pushing ... `Why you so hoha!' said Gary, mildly, when Riki punched him on the arm. <i>(1985,Hey Hey Hey,82</i>)

>  He looked at me and i think he was starting to feel sorry for me because i was beginning to get a bit hoha with the whole caboosh. <i>(May 8, 2005,Sunday Star Times,A13</i>)

>  "i get so hoha [weary] with all the negativity around." <i>(Mar 21, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Most of all, i'm HOHA with some of the well-off and respected among us causing the rot and getting away with it ... <i>(Jul 2006,Pu Kaea,7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> boredom [noun]

>  <b>iHiMAERA & LONG</b> the shining cuckoo spreads his wings / and flies this hoha <i>(1892,into the World of Light,263</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> fuss [Noun. **(is this a Maori use; or is it gen. Eng. hooha (see Partridge); ?? is KH confusing the two.]

### hohere
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [houhere](../H#houhere))

### hoheria
 
 <i>n.</i> houhere

>  No imported tree surpasses in lavishness of white blossom the New Zealand hoheria, variously called ribbonwood and lacebark, nor equals in beauty of shape or colour its intricately dentate and translucent green leaf. <i>(Oct 12, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,14</i>)

>  The hoherias out the back of our garden were watered and after six months they're huge, while others that weren't watered are still struggling. <i>(Oct 20, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F7</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> Hoheria... it is asserted that at one time "Lace-bark" bonnets were quite fashionable in Nelson. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,55</i>)

>  The mallow family, which includes lavatera, abutilon, okra, hollyhocks and the New Zealand lace bark (hoheria) also includes the splendidly flamboyant hibiscus family. <i>(Aug 16, 1992,Dominion Sunday Times,21</i>)

>  hoheria: tree, modification of houhere, lacebark, ribbonwood. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> They had barely time to hide in the undergrowth behind a hoheria before Ed ... appeared, coming straight towards them. <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,120</i>)

### hoho
 
 <i>n.</i> ?hohoeka (=horoeka) [(Chathams)]

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Pseudopanax ... chathamicum</i> ... A small tree whose original home was probably New Zealand ...; of the young unbranched plants the leaves are horizontal or erect not hanging straight down as in the New Zealand species (and therefore known as the umbrella tree) ... HO-HO. <i>(1952,Chatham islands,65</i>)

### hoick
 
 <i>v.</i> to spit (possibly from its sound) [also hoik]

>  <b>Dave Armstrong</b> Just because you had a brown face, hoiked a bit and paused before speaking, they immediately assumed that what you were going to say was extremely profound. <i>(2007,Six Pack 2, The,101</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The summers were hot then, earthquake weather my mother always said, and Tank's gang spent their desperate days ... rocking the swing bridge, hoiking goobs smack into the river. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,230</i>)

>  <b>MciNNES</b> .. listening to [the men] yarning, joking, teasing, watching them hoick, watching them blow their noses without handkerchiefs, ... <i>(1969,Castle on the Run,161</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hoick to spit, possibly from its sound, or even from its sound, or even from its relation to `hoick' as jerking, such as at the start and end of a rowing stroke. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,56</i>)

>  Writing haiku in the bush / Hoick! / a passing hiker. <i>(Mar 12, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),55</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (a) spit; a gob of phlegm ca1935 (esp.schoolboys').

>  TVNZ's delay is effectively a corporate hoik on the grave of a genius. <i>(Apr 26, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  The ramifications of establishing an Expectoration Police and a scale of fines per hoick size were not explored, even by us idle hacks. <i>(Oct 30, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> i had the job of hosing down/ The hoick and sludge and grit <i>(1969,Collected Poems,439,1979 ed.</i>)

### hoicking
 
 <i>n.</i> clearing throat and spitting

 <i>n.</i> (See [houhere](../H#houhere))

### hoiho
 
 <i>n.</i> horse

>  <i>hoiho n.</i> yellow-eyed N.Z. penguin; more commonly, Maori slang for horse. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  <b>BENTLEY & FRASER</b> A cow cocky up Tokoroa/ Rang his agent to order a moa./ Told `They're dead as a dodo!'/ He said, `Quocumque modo?/ Then you'd best send a hoiho, e hoa!' <i>(1985,Grand Limerick Tour, A,No. 8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Megadyptes antipodes</i>, the yellow-eyed penguin (See [yellow-eyed penguin](../Y#yellow-eyed-penguin))

>  Behind wild images of battery kiwi, fenced herds of hoiho penguins and weta abbatoirs may be a serious debate on several fronts, of which Act's farming idea is just the extreme end. <i>(Mar 24, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> 66. <i>Eudyptes antipodes</i>. - ... Auckland's island. M.Lesson refers to a species of this family under the native name of Ho-i-ho. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/199</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 157. EUDYPTES ANTiPODES. Homb. et Jacq. Great Penguin. Hoiho. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,53</i>)

>  <b>FALLA at al.</b> YELLOW-EYED PENGUiN <i>Megadyptes antipodes</i> Maori name: Hoiho. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,20</i>)

>  <i>hoiho n.</i> a New Zealand penquin [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  <i>hoiho n.</i> yellow-eyed N.Z. penguin; more commonly, Maori slang for horse. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  There may be many reasons why we find the hoiho (noise-shouter) so irrestibly magnetic, the humanoid posture, the Chaplinesque gait, the immaculate dress code contrasting with a peroxide coiffure. <i>(Jan 2004,NZ Geographic,No. 67/82</i>)

### hoka
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [red cod](../R#red-cod))

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The <i>Hoka</i> is a fish with thick lips and very prominent eyes, small scales, having long dorsal and ventral fins, in addition to its pectoral and caudal ones, it is a foot long, and is found in Queen Charlotte's Sound. <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,628</i>)

>  PHYSiCULUS RACHUS (Forster). <i>Red Cod; Hoka. </i> <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,iV/121</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> 23 <i>Physiculus bachus</i> (Bloch & Schn.). RED COD; HOKA <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUiCK</b> Next in progression came the shoals of red cod - the <i>hoka</i> of the Maori - chased from the [Waitaki] river-mouth by the assaults of the voracious kahawai (<i>Arripis trutta</i>). <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,176</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> <i>Physiculus bachus</i> (Bloch and Schneider). Red cod. Hoka. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/405</i>) [NOTES: Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> The above list of fish to be caught along the Canterbury coast has some notable omissions, as hoka (red cod) ... though i am not so sure about the rawaru (blue cod). Others omitted are the tarakihi, or terakihi, the moki, the komutumutu (trevalli), the puhaiwhakarua (red-jacket), koiro (conger eel), tuere (blind eel), whai (skate), and the koekohe [sic] (trumpeter). <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> RED COD (HOKA) <i>Physiculus (Pseudophycis) bachus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,166,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>Doogue & Moreland</b> RED COD ... <i> Other names: Physiculus bachus</i>; red rock cod (Australia); hoka (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,208</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> RED COD [<i>Physiculus bachus</i> ] ... Red rock cod is an Australian name, hoka is Maori. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,36</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> HOKA <i>Maori name for Red Cod. </i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,120</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> This stout, reddish-grey fish, known to the Maori as hoka, more closely resembles its English counterpart than other New Zealand `cod'. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,70</i>)

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> RED COD <i>(Hoka) Pseudophycis bachus (Physiculus bachus)</i> ...in life the colour is grey-pink above and whitish beneath with pink fins, but this becomes a more uniform pink-red when the fish is dead. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,142,1984 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> LiNG ... <i>Other names: Genypterus blacodes</i>; rock ling (Australia); hokarari, hoka (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,285</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> [<i>Genypterus blacodes</i>] ... Also known as rock ling (Australia) and hokarari and hoka (Maori). <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,36</i>)

### hokarari
 
 <i>n.</i> ling

>  GENYPTERUS BLACODES (Bloch and Schneider). <i>Ling; Hokarari.</i> A deep-water fish, to be secured during all months of the year. Common around Stewart island and the South island generally. Distribution: Taken as far north as the Bay of Plenty and Hauraki Gulf where it is considered rare. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,iV/123</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> 52 <i>Genypterus blacodes</i>(Bloch & Schn.). LiNG; HOKARARi. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,1971 ed.</i>)

>  46. <i>Genypterus blacodes</i> Bloch and Schneider. Ling. Maori: Hokarari <i>(1929,TrNZi,61/148</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  <b>POWELL</b> LiNG (Genypterus Blacodes), Hokarari of the Maoris, grows up to 4 feet in length, with a weight of 30 lbs, and looks just like a giant tadpole. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,72</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> LiNG (HOKARARi) <i>Genypterus blacodes</i>(Bloch and Schneider) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,337,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> The Ling was known to the Maoris as Hokarari. Some years ago ago large catches were made in Cloudy Bay, and were placed on the Wellington market as `Cloudy Bay Cod'. The scientific name <i>Genypterus blacodes</i>, freely translated, means `a flabby fish, with a fin on the chin.' The New Zealand Ling is also found in Australia where it is known as the `Rockling.' <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,159</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> LiNG ... <i>Other names: Genypterus blacodes</i>; rock ling (Australia); hokarari, hoka (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,285</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> LiNG [<i>Genypterus blacodes</i>] ... Also known as rock ling (Australia) and hokarari and hoka (Maori). <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,36</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> HOKARARi <i>Maori name for Ling. </i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,120</i>)

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> <i>LiNG</i> (Hokarari) <i>Genypterus blacodes</i>.. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,152,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> An offshore species which is most abundant from Cook Strait southwards, ling (or <i>hokarari</i>) is a long, slender fish which weighs up to 14 kilograms. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,77</i>)

### hoke
 
 <i>v.</i> to dig up (turnips) [(Levin, ?obs.)]

>  <b>BROWN</b> ... rough my epidermis, / By `hoking' turnips (this a local [i.e. Levin] term is) / For cows whose appetites are never off them. <i>(1910,Lay of Bantry Bay,32,1917 ed.</i>)

### hokey-pokey
 
 <i>n.</i> an ice cream flavoured with small pieces of hokey-pokey [hokey-poley sic in 2003 cit.?]

>  That dessert is truly a bit of New Zealand (or Australia, depending on the native) as is the hokey pokey ice cream, which the young investment banker seated beside me.. declares is just about the best dessert he has ever eaten... i daren't tell him that hokey pokey ice cream is found in every dairy in every village in New Zealand. <i>(Feb 7, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  it drips from the hairy corner of her mouth / like hokeypokey iceceam / left too long to shine in the sun <i>(Sep 25, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

>  First you scoff a packet of Jaffas. then knock back a couple of TT2s and a scoop of hokey pokey, followed by a gob of milk bottles, spearmint leaves and some pineapple chunks. <i>(Aug 13, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  i had a brief look at the accompanying map and then discarded that publication to focus on my hokey-pokey ice-cream. <i>(Mar 4, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  i can't remember stopping to eat - except for occasional hokey-poley icecreams at a shop at the far end of the beach - but i suppose we did. <i>(Jan 2, 2003,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  A chance glimpse of The Concise Oxford Dictionary has you wondering. it says: "ice-cream formerly sold esp. by italian street vendors." The Concise's bigger sibling, The New Shorter Oxford Dictionary, is even worse: "A kind of cheap ice-cream sold by street vendors." These English lexicographers would find nothing cheap or italian about the best ice-cream, in the world sold at any Kiwi dairy. <i>(Jul 3, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Before that, Rodneyites had to put up with weak signals from Auckland stations, and you can bet your triple-scoop hokey-pokey ice-cream that the good people from Warkworth, Helensville and Snells Beach didn't rate that too highly. <i>(Jun 26, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

>  On certain days when the wind was blowing in the right direction we could smell the peppermint or hokey pokey or chocolate. <i>(Oct 1990,North & South,37</i>)

>  'And whole rooms filled with ice cream?' i'd say, and she'd nod and say , 'Hokey Pokey.' <i>(2001,Huia 4: Contemp. Maori Fiction,87</i>)

>  <b>ViRTUE</b> Hokey Pokey's the best in the world, i reckon. <i>(1987,Redemption of Elsdon Bird, The,67</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a brittle porous toffee often sold in (chocolate-coated) lumps (and Crunchie bars); an aerated toffee-like sweet made with bicarbonate of soda [see DNZE sense 1 for more cits.]

>  <b>MANSFiELD</b> We always gave him the same presents..three cakes of hoky-poky. <i>(1939,Scrapbook,3</i>)

>  hokey pokey: 'sea foam' candy covered in chocolate <i>(Jul 2000,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: From N.Z. English to U.S. English Dictionary at http://nz.com/NZ/Culture/NZDic.html]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a dance [also US (ODE); cf. Brit. hokey-cokey]

>  <b>KEiTH & MAiN</b> Baloo baloo balight,/ All on a Saturday night,/ Put your right hand in,/ Put your right hand out,/ Shake it a little a little/ And turn right about./ This curious song had a revival in another form as the boisterous dance-hall craze of World War ii, the hoki toki, or as many South islanders called it, the hokey pokey. <i>(1984,NZ Yesterdays,289</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> the sea fish <i>Macruronus novaezealandiae</i> with long tapering body and tail, valuable commercially [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hokianga, a thick mutton sandwich. [Shepard] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,57</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a thick mutton sandwich [a staple musterer's or shepherd's paddock lunch].

>  <b>MCGiLL</b> hokianga, a: a thick mutton sandwich. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,57</i>)

### hokio
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The <i>hokio</i>, says Williams, is 'the messenger bird'. <i>Hokio</i>, the verb 'to descend', is also 'a bird, probably the same as <i>hokioi</i>, with variant spellings of <i>hakuai</i> and <i>hokiwai</i> ... an extint bird of nocturnal habits, held in superstitious regard by the Maori, said to be never seen. <i>(Aug 2000,NZWords,No. 4 : 6</i>)

>  The Hokio is a spiritual messenger bird and, because all things arise out of sacredness, the source of which is God, everything is sacred. <i>(Jan 30, 2000,NZ Catholic,17</i>)

### hokioi
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [hakuwai](../H#hakuwai))

>  Many legends are associated with the mythical bird "hokioi"... in the South and in some parts of the North island it is identical, apparently, with the "hakuwai" which has seven joints in its wing, lives in the sky, and presages trouble... "its cry was Hokioi! Hokioi! Hu!" The last word represented the whizzing sound made by the bird in flight. <i>(Sep 11, 1912,Otago Witness,76</i>)

>  A newspaper called "The Hokioi," the name being derived from a semi fabulous bird of evil omen had been started at Ngauruawahia... "The Hokioi" would brook no rival in its nest in the rearing of its cantankerous brood. <i>(Feb 12, 1864,Otago Witness,2</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> The clouds are long and black and ragged, like the wings of stormbattered dragons. Or of hokioi... huge birds ... <i>ibid.</i> 448. Hokioi = unknown (and maybe legendary) kind of bird. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,250</i>)

>  <i>Hokioi</i> (or <i>hokioe</i>) is, according to Kerry Hulme in <i>The Bone People</i>, 'an unknown (and maybe legendary) kind of bird'... The name <i>Hokioi</i> was given to a Ngaruawahia newspaper in 1862. 'The name was derived from a semi-fabulous bird of evil omen.' <i>(Aug 2000,NZWords,No. 4: 5</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> it is a moot point whether the name <i>hakuwai</i> was derived from <i>hokioi</i>.. or whether <i>hokioi</i> has been adopted., as an imperfect rendition of <i>hakuwai</i>, to provide a name for the eagle legend. <i>(1989,Prodigious Birds,92</i>)

>  Hokioi was the name of a fabulous bird, never seen by mortal eyes and known only by its scream which was supposed to be an omen of war and pestilence... For some time the Natives of Waikato had been printing.. a small news sheet, known as the Hokioi. <i>(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),3(9) : 20</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> He stated further that a similar bird [to the Great Frigate Bird, Fregata aquila] had been killed by the natives at ihuraua, ... and that all who had seen it pronounced this the true `Hokioi' of Maori tradition - a long-winged bird that is supposed to soar in the heavens, far above the range of human vision, and to descend to the shore at night to feed upon shell-fish. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,340</i>)

>  Dr. Hector suggested to me that the Harpagornis might possibly be the Hokioi of the Maoris, which ... according to Buller, is the Great Frigate Bird (Fregata aquila). <i>(1874,TrNZi,6/64</i>) [NOTES: (Haast)]

>  <b>HAAST</b> At the same time it is possible, that the large bird of prey met with in the heart of the Alps, and to which i alluded on pages 37 and 135 may be the Movie or Hokioe of the Maoris, or even the Harpagornis of which the bones were first obtained in the turbary deposits of Glenmark. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,449</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> The hokioi is described as a bird with wings two fathoms long, and that as it flies it makes a swishing noise or booming sound. <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,51</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> Hokioi = unknown (and maybe legendary) kind of bird <i>(1983,Bone People, The,467</i>) [NOTES: 1983 Hulme Bone People (Gloss) 467 [p] 262]

### Hokitika swindle
 
 <i>n.</i> (also Hokitika ellipt.) a hotel bar game, orig. popularised on the West Coast (at Hokitika), but known throughout NZ.

>  <b>BAKER</b> The Hokitika Swindle is an hotel bar game played in order to create a jackpot from which payment of drinks may be made. it is based on a method of counting, whereby the person who calls a certain number in sequence pays a specified sum into the jackpot [or shouts the round]. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,57</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> Hokitika swindle. `Hotel bar game played to create a jackpot from which payment for drinks may be made,' B., 1941: New Zealand: C. 20. Of topical origin. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1077</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> Hokitika swindle calling of a certain number in sequence with the aim of creating a jackpot, which is used to buy rounds of drinks in a pub. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,57</i>)

>  `Hokitika' Game Led To Fight. A fight outside a hotel in Lower Hutt follow[ed] a game of `Hokitika.' ... in a statement to the Police Parascuk said that `Hokitika' consisted of guessing the numbers on a 1 [pound] note. He lost 15/- this way and accused Cloake of cheating. <i>(Sep 23, 1953,Evening Post,16</i>)

### Hokitikaite
 
 <i>n.</i> one born or living in Hokitika

>  The West Coasters had plenty of barrackers from their own country, and these went [mad] whenever a Hokitikite dropped over the chalk mark. <i>(Aug 12, 1905,NZ Truth,8-2-8</i>)

>  Aggrieved Hokitikaites wondered if it was a Greymouth person who wrought this shameful deed. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,187</i>)

### hokoa
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> Fam. GADOiDEAE 69. <i>Lota baccha</i>. Cuv., ... <i>Gadus bacchus</i>, G. Forster, ... inhabits Murderer's Bay. it is probably the `haddock' of the settlers: its native name in Queen Charlotte's Sound is `Ehogoa.' <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/221</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Richardson's Fish) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/221-222]

### Hokonui
 
 <i>n.</i> illicit home-distilled spirits, liquor; moonshine (orig. from the Hokonui Hills, Southland, famed for the illicit whisky distilled there (during local prohibition).

>  What are they going to do now the Americans have stopped eating cheese and wool and hokonui and all our other primary products. <i>(Dec 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,41</i>)

>  A number of samples alleged to be Hokonui, or illicit whisky, were submitted to the laboratory by the police. <i>(Jul 12, 1943,NZEF Times,iii/107 : 4</i>)

>  <b>Davin</b> .. the women all gabbling and the men outside in the wash-house tasting the jar of Hokonui that Tom MacDonald had brought. <i>(2007,Gorse Blooms Pale, The,80,2nd (1st 1947) ed Janet Wilson ed.</i>)

>  i wouldn't change places with you for all the Hokonui on the West Coast. <i>(Mar 1985,Landfall,8(1) No., 29/17</i>)

>  <b>MiLLER</b> Other people were in the game too, and all called their brew Hokonui. <i>(1967,ink on My Fingers,171</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKiNS</b> But getting back to the Hokonui whisky, it found it way into all the local hotels, and it got there with the milk deliveries, in milk cans. <i>(1896,Southern Gentry,67</i>)

>  The unlabelled whisky, delivered in bottles, cans and milk billies, became known simply as Hokonui. ... [p.72] Hokonui became the common name for illicit whisky right across Southland. ... [p. 77] Bottles of Hokonui turn up every now and then in farm sheds and attics, and people continue to come forward with stories. <i>(Dec 2005,NZ Geographic,71</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKiNS</b> And of course it would be a lot cheaper than the legal brands would be, so at that time the pocket might be able to run to a bottle of Hokonui now and again. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,68</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> in one case the box that had held the liquor still held the packing, but the bottles had been distributed, ... though undoubtedly they had held Hokonui. <i>(1980,NZ Whisky Book,47</i>) [NOTES: c1934 H.S.Cordery (Collector of Customs) in 1980 Perry NZ Whisky Book 47]

>  <b>STUART</b> Mr Macalister, `You do know of the existence of Hokonui in the district?' Mr Findlay, `Not at Hedgehope. Hedgehope is not Hokonui.' Mr Macalister, `Have you ever tasted Hokonui whisky?' Mr Findlay, `Not for many years.' <i>(1982,Satyrs of Southland,89</i>) [NOTES: a1935 Court verbatim evidence in 1982 Stuart Satyrs of Southland 89]

>  [NB: invercargill went dry at 10 p.m., 30 June 1906 to 1946] There is nothing unpleasant or poisonous about the genuine `Hokonui' manufactured in the forest of the mountain range from which its name is derived. it sells at a price considerably lower than the imported article on which duty was paid, and some connoisseurs regard it as better than Scotland's best.' <i>(Aug 5, 1936,Auckland Weekly News</i>) [NOTES: 1936 Aug 5 Auckland Weekly News in 1980 Perry NZ Whisky Book 30]

>  [Cartoon caption] Hokonui <i>(1937,Otago Capping Book,64</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> Hokanui ... the local [? West Canterbury] equivalent of potheen. <i>(1945,Died in the Wool,185</i>)

>  <b>SMiTH</b> Whisky used to be distilled in Dunedin ... More enterprising settlers further south, however, carry on the good work under the brand name of Hokonui - <i>(1947,From N to Z,32</i>)

>  <b>DAViN</b> [Southland] `What is it?' `it's the best Hokonui.' `And what's Hokonui?' `it's the poteen they make up in the Hokonui Hills that you can see from the door there. ... Not that the peelers can catch them. ...' <i>(1959,No Remittance,66</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... and the smoking chimney of the Hokonui still, SUPPORT LOCAL iNDUSTRY, ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,226</i>)

>  [Caption] Ex-Policeman Who Hunted Hokonui Brewers. A man now living in quiet retirement ... once tried to control the wild Hokonui brewers in the hills of Southland. ... He participated in many raids on whisky stills around invercargill during the depression days of the early 1930s when the city was `dry' ... . Mr Abel still has a bottle of genuine Hokonui. it carries the label, `Awarua Special, 85 per cent proof'. <i>(Sep 14, 1963,Dominion,12</i>)

>  <b>WATT</b> Another former whaler, McShane, also settled at Sandy Point and became Southland's first `Hokonui' manufacturer. He distilled spirits from the edible part of the cabbage tree .. <i>(1971,Centenary of invercargill,1</i>)

>  <b>MiTCHELL</b> Hokonui: import substitute Scotch. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,182</i>)

>  <b>STUART</b> To a reasonably large section of the community in invercargill and surrounding districts, however, it was common knowledge that illicit supplies of Hokonui existed. The spirit was generally known as `Hokonui Brand', because of the widespread belief that it was distilled somewhere in the hills of that name. <i>(1982,Satyrs of Southland,29</i>)

>  Did the McRaes who made Hokonui make much from their whisky? Roddy has no doubts. <i>(Jul 23, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> Some kids [9] reckoned he had a secret whisky still hidden in the bush, where he made hokonui to slygrog to the local people. <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,8</i>)

>  hokonui n. NZ colloq. illicit spirits. [Hokonui in Southland <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,356</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 234]]

>  <b>OWEN &  PERKiNS</b> (Bob Edwards, bushman) Of course, down around Croydon, where i worked in the bush, that was the Hokonui country, and i could tell some stories about Hokonui whisky. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,65</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hokonui illicitly distilled whisky, from its most notable production in the Hokonui hills of Southland. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,57</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib. uses]

>  There are two series [of paintings] and the more interesting one deals with the making of Hokonui moonshine. <i>(Apr 12, 2006,NZ Herald,B5</i>)

>  Now they've got down to the Hokonui moonshine, they're meeting a different class of people. <i>(Nov 30, 1942,NZEF Times,ii/75 : 6</i>)

>  i am surprised, more than somewhat, to see Hokonui Joe in company with such a character. <i>(Mar 22, 1943,NZEF Times,ii/91 : 10</i>)

>  You might as well include the six o'clock swill or a bottle of Hokonui whisky. <i>(Jan 21, 2001,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

>  Legends of Hokonui moonshine abound in the south, of illicit stills deep in the Hokonui mountains, of the Macrae family who brought the tradition from the Scottish Highlands in the 1870s, and the 50 years of prohibition in Gore, only voted out in the mid 1950s. <i>(May 2000,Cuisine,33</i>)

>  <b>MiLLER</b> The story of the original Hokonui whisky is really the saga of the McRae family. <i>(1967,ink on My Fingers,168</i>)

>  Legends of Hokonui moonshine abound in the south, of illict stills deep in the Hokonui mountains, of the Macrae family who brought the tradition from the Scottish Highlands in the 1870s, and the 50 years of prohibition in Gore, only voted out in the mid 1950s. <i>(May 2000,Cuisine,No. 80 : 33</i>)

>  "There will be a significant choice of whisky available, including Hokonui Moonshine Whisky, and other boutique beverages," he says... Geddes says this also celebrates the illicit whisky making past. "The first incidence of aerial survey by customs was done in the Mandeville area as they looked for the Hokonui stills." <i>(Dec 2002,Travel Times (Wellington),10</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wild, fine-wool Merino-type breed of sheep that has roamed the Hokonui Hills in Southland. [ORIGIN: toponym]

>  <b>GORDON</b> For Leon that's meant ducks, for Lynley, her hokonui flock, and all of them have a few Coopworths ... <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,103</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Hokonui sheep are out of the same hills [as Hokonui whisky] and although not illegal, are certainly a curiosity. A fairly original form of Merino, they've run wild for well over a century and have developed, or reverted to, quite a few distinct characteristics. There's the varying shades of black, brown and grey with a wee dash of white on the forehead and a white tip on the tail...'They're just different,' he says. 'Things like positioning of bones and teeth, everything from the traditional farm animal breeding world, you can forget about when it comes to Hokonuis. They're as tough as old boots and interesting in their grazing habits. They prefer rough feed and will eat heads and thistles before they touch the clover.'..interested though he is, there's no way Peter will reduce the Coopworth flock in concession to the Hokonuis. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,101</i>)

>  The first record of the feral Hokonuis was in 1863, a flock of about 200 animals, so they have had 150 years to adapt. The Hokonuis' ancestors are believed to be smooth-skinned Saxony Merinos .. When and how Hokonuis came to be here is unlikely to be proven. <i>(Feb 2008,Growing Today,58</i>)

>  Whenever i hear the words wild sheep i instantly think of the Hokonuis in Southland ... <i>(Jun 30, 2007,internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.fishnhunt.co.nz/issues.cgi?action=hand&forum=32&thread=3387&msgid=1&query=1]

### Hokonui rock
 
 <i>n.</i> geol. classification [-]

>  Eric is making a detailed comparison of Alpine and Hokonui sequences of rocks of Kaihikuan age. <i>(Dec 1967,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,24/11</i>)

>  The field trips were to the Hamilton basin where Quaternary deposits, surface features and soils were examined; to Cambridge for geomorphology, ignimbrites and Hokonui rocks. <i>(Jul 1967,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,23/2</i>)

### Hokonui Swindle
 
 <i>n.</i> error for Hokitika swindle, a bar-game played with the numbers on bank-notes. ca1974 Wellington: used in De Brett hotel bar

>  included in Hodges's study is the truly macho battle of alcoholic consumption - the drinking game. ... There'll be some inane feat of manliness to perform, such as a lewd tongue-twister, or a physical show of strength, and he who loses has to down his glass in a gulp. in Otago alone, Hodges found 42 varieties of the theme, with names such as `One Fat Hen and a Couple of Ducks' or the `Hokonui Swindle'. <i>(Oct 19, 1983,NZ Times,3</i>)

>  Other well-known [drinking] games are Hokonui Swindle, Bottles, Fluffy Ducks, Bunnies are Hovering, Cardinal Huff, All Blacks, Matches, and The Amazing Grimaldi Brothers. All these games involve either a complicated intellectual exercise, tongue twisters, mathematical calculations or aerobatics in a confined space; High Candelabras for example, involves intoxicated individuals attempting to traverse the four walls of a room without touching the floor. <i>(Mar 13, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,16</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> Hokonui Swindle more recent name for and/or version of the Hokitika Swindle, a pub game aimed at financing further drinking. Baker identified the Hokitika version as based on a method of counting `whereby the person who calls a certain number in a sequence pays a specified sum into the jackpot'. Glenn Johnston defines the Hokonui version as a drinking game that combines elements of chance with the ability to hold your liquor and follow a few simple rules - though the deviousness of the caller can also influence proceedings. The aims are simple: to liven things up, get a bit of alcohol consumed quickly and sort the stayers from the pikers. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,45</i>)

### Hokonuis
 
 <i>n.</i> abbreviated form of Hokonui Fashion Design Awards, an annual event held in Gore since 1988. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  it's the second time Sylvester has judged the Hokonuis. With two sell-out shows, the Hokonuis have once again given a boost to New Zealand fashion as well as putting Southland in general and Gore in particular on the map. <i>(Aug 2, 2007,Dominion Post,D3</i>)

### hokopapa
 
 <i>n.</i> (Moriori) genealogy or whakapapa

>  The pair were further aided by the establishment of a "hokopapa" (whakapapa/genealogy) centre in Wellington, whose historian helped them trace their origins back to the Chatham islands. About 300 remaining Moriori people had been traced, said Mrs O'Donnell, but there could still be more. <i>(Jan 20, 2005,Gisborne Herald</i>)

>  The Cultural Development Manager (CDM) will be responsible for planning and implementing a major development programme in language, carving, weaving, rongo (waiata), hokopapa, history, waahi tapu, repatriation, artistic expression, and taonga. <i>(Oct 1, 2005,Dominion Post,H17</i>)

>  Lin Entwistle, chairwoman of the trust's hokopapa unit, said her group offered support to people who thought they had Moriori links. <i>(Dec 12, 2005,NZ Herald,A5</i>)

### hold
 
 <i>v.</i> Sc. funds; see holding.

>  <b>BURGESS</b> `He's a bloody artful old cove : i think he holds it, for i noticed something pretty bulky in his trouser's pockets.' <i>(1866,Confessions,117,1983 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> give me/us a hold of, esp. gis a hold of, (to) give one a share of, give me a part of (the action).



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [mustering, shepherding and dog trials] to hold sheep in a stationary position without work from a dog or shepherd.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> As it happened the bottom man, Jack Mciver, had them safely held ... <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,17</i>)

>  Hold - To h. sheep is to keep them together, or to h. them where they are. <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),17</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [(drug users')]

>  [Junkies hanging out] could pay $100 for something that would not get them stoned, but would `hold them'. <i>(Apr 5, 1986,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  There is a big difference between being stoned and `being held'. Addicts have to be at least held for their body responses to be `normal'. This is how the methadone programme works. it ... holds addicts, depending on their dosages, for about 24 hours, before they start hanging out. <i>(Apr 5, 1986,Evening Post,19</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [(?obs. criminals')]

>  <b>BURGESS</b> Sullivan remarked to me that he thought the old man, `Held it', which is a cant term meaning he possessed something <i>(1866,Maungatapu Murders</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> be in possession of drugs or contraband [(drug users', ?or continuation of 1.).]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> HOLD (v) Being in possession of drugs or contraband. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,249</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sheepdog trials] the final stage of the long pull or head contest when a dog is stationary.

>  Hold: the final stage of the long pull or head. To show you're taking a hold, you must put your arms out. <i>(Oct 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,25</i>)

>  When the sheep have entered the ring, the next and last task is to gain a hold: sheep, trialist and dog all stationary and 'on balance,' A good hold has the dog round to stop the sheep, with the sheep facing the dog. <i>(1991,Constitution of NZ Dog Trial Assoc,5B</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> On his way to complete the run by getting a balanced hold in the ring, Pauline's Boy ran rather wide. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,16</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [land] to sustain a crop or pasture.

>  in one instance a strong growth of meadow foxtail was noticed, but the average Taranaki country does not hold this grass very well. <i>(Mar 20, 1931,NZ Journal of Agriculture,42 3 180</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> Many kinds of dogs have been introduced by the Colonists; including Bull-dogs, by the whalers, Kangaroo-dogs, (a mixture of greyhound and mastiff) from New South Wales, and various crosses between these and the natives' dogs, which go under the general name of pig-dogs, and combine courage with speed, being used chiefly to hunt the wild hog, and taught to hold him, when caught, by ear or jowl, until the hunter comes up. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,166</i>)

>  <b>MEREDiTH</b> ] it sometimes takes up to half an hour to scramble through the rough gullies to where the dogs have the pig stuck up. if the bulldog were released before reaching the quarry, he would `go in' at once, and would probably be exhausted with holding the pig before we could get there, and possibly get killed. A bailed-up pig makes periodical rushes at the baiting dogs, ... <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,64,1935 ed.</i>)

 <i>v.</i> in NZ, `to have a job' rather than `to keep a job with difficulty', as P. and ?OED [check]. [ca1914 [W.H.B. Orsman]; also sc. a claim (goldmining)]

 <i>v.</i> Sc. sheep, of a sheep-dog at trials.

>  <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/493</i>) [NOTES: see entry heading (class)]

 <i>phr.</i> [around since at least the 1940s]
