 <i>n.</i> [(sailing) 1939]

### holder
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pig-dog that bails wild pigs by holding on to ears, tail or testicles in the case of a boar. (See [holding dog](../H#holding-dog))

>  <b>AXBEY</b> Two of us were hunting near Waverley; my mate had a couple of holders and a finder, while i still had just the one. <i>(1991,Along the Track: a collection of NZ hunting yarns,10</i>)

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> Some people have a finder and then they have bailers and they might even have dogs known as holders. <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,122</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a pig-hunter's dog specially trained to hold wild pigs with its teeth, a holding dog.

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> When the dogs had worried [the pig] for some time we let the `holders' go. <i>(1935,New Zealand, Land of My Choice,89</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> Pig-dog types are many ... The writer's [J.P.Lewin's] considered classification is straight finders, straight bailers, straight holders and straight killers. ... inside it we have finder-bailers; bailer-holders; finder-bailer-holders; bailer-holder-killers and holder-killers. <i>(Dec 1951,Here & Now,89</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Harry's dogs were bully holders. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,113</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> Tony gave Abungus a bull terrier bitch called Tuki. `She'll be a good holder,' Abungus said. <i>(1965,Stand in the Rain,132</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Pigdogs are classified into three group: finders, bailers, and holders. The name of each type indicates perfectly what they are expected to do. <i>(1982,Wild Pig,156</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a dog that bails and holds a wild pig

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Wait till you see my dogs Crump ... Picked up a champion holder-bailer down country. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,107</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an owner of rural property.

>  Holders of the back country at Waiau state that the winter has been the best known for a number of years. <i>(Oct 10, 1914,Hawera Star,3</i>)

>  Mr Yalden sees the trees as being useful for small block holders who have wet areas. <i>(Aug 31, 2001,Dominion,Supp 26</i>)

### holder dog
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Finder-bailer? That's the dog who can sniff out a boar, track it down, and bail it up, standing off and barking, until the hunter's holder dogs arrive, or the hunter. <i>(Feb 1998,Mana,No. 20: 36</i>)

### holding
 
 <i>adj.</i> be holding: to have ready cash, to be in funds

>  in the Pub: `What ho, Jerry, how yer holding?' / `Blime, can't you see i'm bent? / i haven't made enough this week / For to pay the bloomin' rent.'/ <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,11</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... how are you holding?, how much money have you; <i>ibid.</i> 288 C. How are you holding, D? D. All right. <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> holding, ppl.adj. in funds: Australian:C. 20: s.> coll. See hold, 4. [To be in funds: low coll. ...:at first, Cockney: from ca 1870. in C. 20, mostl;y Australian. Esp. in do you hold?, C. 19, and, C. 20 Australia, are you holding?] Esp. How are you holding?, how much money have you?: also New Zealand. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,397</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> [Australianisms include] financial and holding (descriptive of the state of well-supplied with cash.) <i>(1941,NZ Slang,61</i>)

>  <b>HiSLOP</b> As i was holding very well on the day, i did not mind [losing out on a winning dividend]. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,34</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> `Tell you what, we'll split up what [money] we've got left between us - are you holding okay, Scrubby?' `Yeah, i've got plenty, thanks.' <i>(1962,One of Us,169</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the controlling of sheep by a heading or holding dog.

>  Moss' cast and pull were not so good, but his holding was excellent. <i>(Jun 27, 1946,NZ Farmer,28</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a piece of owned or occupied land.

>  Owing to her humid climate and fertile soil New Zealand is peculiarly well adapted for small holdings. <i>(1905,NZ Official Yearbook,607</i>)

>  <b>TAiT</b> The original holding was thickly covered with fern and scrub. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol i,29</i>)

>  <b>MCLAUCHLAN</b> .. hacked a holding out of the bush. <i>(1981,Farming of NZ,13</i>)

### holding block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large paddock on a station where sheep are mustered off the tops and kept or held [before being taken into the station for drafting etc. ]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> After smoko, three of the men were detailed to take the mob to a holding block where they would wait until the rest of the ewes were dipped. <i>(1949,High Country Days,105</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Ten days brought them back into the station with 8000 wethers to start the shearers off - the long strings of woollies winding across the open downs till they joined in one great wheeling mob which had to be steered carefully through a wide gateway into the holding block. <i>(1960,Tall Tussock,46</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> To his satisfaction the sheep were still in the holding block ... <i>(1972,Sheep Thief,140</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The boys are mustering out a holding block this morning ... <i>(1972,Sheep Thief,30</i>)

### holding chain
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Only [a cow] moved into the bail and stood quietly while John hooked the holding chain round her backside, and pinned her tail to the bent nail in the wall. Only gave him her leg and John made a leg-rope hitch first time. <i>(1978,Elver, The,37</i>)

### holding dog
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dog that will catch and hold another dog.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Holding dog: one that will catch and hold another dog (such as a pup that refuses to come up to chain or kennel.) <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,42</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> (Glossary) Holding dog: A dog which will catch and hold another dog [??]. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,168</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pig-dog that will hold a pig once bailed.

>  <b>HiNDMARSH</b> With no holding dogs present, he was lightly bailed by the sheepdogs. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,94</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> ... when out mustering, the sheepdogs would bail up a pig, and as the shepherds would have no proper holding dog, ... it was not an easy matter to finish it off. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,255,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANNERiNG</b> With two good holding dogs, one on each ear, we would tackle any boar with spears, or knives only. <i>(1943,Eighty Years,45</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> At the end of two days' hunting, aided by a arge holding dog ..., Jim still knew nothing of Red Boar's whereabouts ... <i>(1955,By Reef and Range,10</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dog trained to hold sheep in a mob [in a mustering situation or on the road.]

>  <b>CHiNN</b> So the day wore on, though i was beginning to think that holding dogs were a necessity in this game, not only one or two but half a dozen so that some could be rested. <i>(1963,Packtrack to Highway,31</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... champion holding dog. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,111</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> Jim, who is much quicker to detect trouble than i am, both in the yards and in the paddock, and who has a holding dog, missed a ewe two or three days before he dipped, ... <i>(Apr 17, 1952,Shepherd's Calendar,74,1961 ed.</i>)

### holding paddock
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [joc.] pensioner flats or elderly rest home, or boarding school. (See [accommodation paddock](../A#accommodation-paddock))

>  <b>MCGiLL</b> holding paddock: pensioners' flats or old-age housing settlement; from paddock stock held in on way to slaughterhouse ... <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,57</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a paddock used for holding stock for a short time before they are moved elsewhere; a reserved paddock for holding and feeding winter stock.

>  One afternoon we spied all the cows hanging about on the fence loooking at the lush grass in the sheep holding paddock. <i>(Oct 2000,Growing Today,74</i>)

>  To reduce this excitement [in cattle] holding-paddocks are necessary. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/342</i>)

>  Holding paddock. A small p. close to yards, woolshed, or mustering hut, for holding (not feeding) sheep. sometimes called a camping p. (C.) <i>(Oct 17, 1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  On inland northerly and westerly situations ... these breaks develop successively in definite steps or benches. ... This problem is very real in holding-paddocks and on hillsides that are frequently used as access routes. ... Near the crests of hills sheep aggravate the position by camping on these terracettes ... <i>(1946,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,27/159</i>)

>  ... holding paddock. <i>(1948,Landfall,6/117</i>) [NOTES: Hart-Smith]

>  <i>(Oct 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,35</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS]]

>  <b>LEVY</b> Strathmore weed, a low-growing shrubby plant found on poor, friable soils, if eaten when stock are forced on it in a holding paddock, may cause staggers and death ... <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,305,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> in the neighbourhood of the station there must be several holding paddocks varying from 50-500 acres, for holding mobs of sheep during shearing and other times. <i>(1951,High Country,12</i>)

>  <b>DARE`</b> There were holding paddocks at convenient intervals all the way [to the freezing works] ... No-one ever kept their stock in these paddocks longer than twenty-four hours, as there was no feed in them: they were just for convenience in holding animals overnight. <i>(1956,Rouseabout Jane,189</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> i was called `the new chum' by the station hands, who were always trying to take a rise out of me. i was sent over to one small holding-paddock, not far from the woolshed, and told to grub out all the rushes - ... <i>(1964,Doctor in the Mountains,30,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> At the McKerrow hut the holding paddock [for cattle] was about an acre of scrub enclosed by two strands of wire <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,42</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> ... holding paddock and accommodation paddock, alternative names for a paddock where a drover might leave stock overnight, had more permanent status until heavy trucks replaced drovers. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,144</i>)

>  <b>McCASKiL;L</b> There [at Tarndale station] he built a shearing shed for fifteen men, fenced a holding paddock of 2,000 acres and ordered a boiling-down plant from Andersons in Christchurch. <i>(1969,Molesworth,53</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> For another day Stewart worked in the yards, helping to load sheep out, and doing short droves to holding paddocks. <i>(1970,Tat,105</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> Often the shepherds' horses had little time for grazing ... and the holding paddocks were never very lush at this time of the year. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,34</i>)

>  <b>BRUCE</b> in 1935 i decided to build a holding paddock on the top of Blo'ard and another holding paddock on island Hill ... To get the sheep netting and iron standards packed up by horses was a mighty big undertaking ... <i>(1977,Life in the Hinterland of the South island,86</i>)

>  <b>NEAVE</b> Before the railway ..., the area where Kurow now stands, was a holding paddock for sheep that had got too wet for shearing ... and any wet sheep used to be held there until they were dry enough to shear. <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,72</i>) [NOTES: 1980 [????] J.C.H.Chapman in 1980 Neave The Land of Munros 72]

>  holding paddock n. Austral. & N.Z. a paddock in which cattle or sheep are kept temporarily, as before shearing, etc. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,533</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 357]]

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Mustering is a big job, using two huts and holding paddocks. <i>(1982,Historic Sheep Stations of NZ,7</i>)

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> i remember a young islander ... telling me how he used to gallop through the sheep in the holding paddock at Flower Pot ... just to annoy Gabriel. <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,61</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> Before leaving home for the sale Dad would have arranged for a holding paddock reasonably close to the saleyards where the mustered sheep could have a feed and a rest for the night. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,53</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> We fled across the [blacksmith's] holding paddock . <i>(1988,Rainshadow,80</i>)

>  <b>DiCK</b> This fencing of suitable areas for winter holding paddocks - Pringle country of 4000 acres - made the working of winter months much more practical and sensible. <i>(1964,High Country Family,25</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an enclosure where stock are held temporarily [in preparation for drafting, shearing or similar work or where animals are held en route to a sale.] (See [accommodation paddock](../A#accommodation-paddock))

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Holding paddock: one designed not for the grazing of stock, but for retaining a mob close by the woolshed, or drafting yards, or dip yards, or near a mustering hut. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,43</i>)

>  <b>Kerr</b> At the end of the block the mob are put in holding paddocks and the gang retire to the homestead for a big feed and, unless it's a crook place, a beer. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,45</i>)

>  <b>MiLLS ET AL</b> Woolly sheep held in a holding paddock overnight will work through the yards fresh and free-moving. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,71</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. little holding paddocks had been erected at the various mustering camps. <i>(1972,in the Wake of the Axe,211</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> At that time, there were only seven fenced-off blocks on the place, little different from any similar run. Today there are 49, plus some holding paddocks near the yards. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,42</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The over-night stop for all mobs approaching Gisborne from the Coast was the four holding paddocks at Otiku, a couple of kilometres north of Wainui.. <i>(1996,Trouble with Stock Agents, The,102</i>)

>  Subdivided in 30 main and 4 holding paddocks by a very high standard of conventional fence aided by a central race. <i>(Nov 13, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,32</i>)

### holding pen
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [joc] a boarding school attended by pastoralist offspring.

>  <b>DOMiNY</b> .. boarding-school masters would refer to the colleges as 'holding pens' for high country children who (like sheep) would be sure to return to their patch. <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,196</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> -

>  <b>PERRY</b> The shearing board should be of good width, and be separated from the holding pens by a solid wall, so as to facilitate penning up. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,16</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Holding pen. Small pen close to the woolshed for holding the sheep. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,96</i>)

>  <b>LAY</b> And he would always think that it wasn't much of an end for a man ... living out his last days in a geriatric holding pen, picking up other people's rubbish. <i>(1988,Fools on the Hill, The,38</i>) [NOTES: figurative use]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pen situated near a shearing board, a docking bench, a dip or a diamond pen.

>  <b>PERRY</b> The shearing board should be of a good width, and be separated from the holding pens by a solid wall, so as to facilitate penning up. The floor of the pens should be of battens, with sufficient space between to allow droppings to fall through. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,16</i>)

### holding yard
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>(Apr 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,377/2</i>) [NOTES: 377/2 (11 June 580/1 July 7/2 OEDS)]

>  The check pens in turn usually open into much larger Holding yards, which correspond in size to the receiving yards. <i>(Jul 15, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,7</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> This drafting race divides sheep into the separate lots required, and these are then handled by the drafting pens, check pens, and holding yards, which should correspond in size to the receiving yards. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,120</i>)

>  <b>McCASKiLL</b> The hessian, supported by standards, was used for the holding yard, the lambs never charging it as they do with netting. The docking yard was made of hurdles in the conventional way. <i>(1969,Molesworth,99</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Audrey and Peggy wet down the concrete of the bails and yards with a few buckets of water from the trough in the corner of the holding yard. <i>(1978,Elver, The,36</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> There were holding and drafting yards at the back of the shed, but the `counting out' pens opened out directly into a 100 acre paddock. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,65</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an extensive yard or small paddock used for overnight accommodation for stock being driven.

>  <b>HUNT</b> .. then next day you'd go to the top of the hill where we had some holding yards. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,63</i>)

>  <b>DAHM</b> The holding yards were the overnight accommodation for the stock ... <i>(2000,People of the Axe, The,47</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large sheep-yard into which sheep are mustered and held prior to drafting, dipping, shearing, docking or treatment etc

>  <b>RiDDOLLS</b> Holding yards may be provided to hold the whole flock in separate mobs after it has been drafted. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,333</i>)

>  <b>SALiSBURY</b> We had to run out docking nets on to posts that were in the ground ready to make the holding yards for the shepherds to yard into. <i>(1994,i Was So Lucky,23</i>)

>  A wing should be erected, starting from the entrance to the holding yard, and widening as it extends into the paddock to form a lead into the yard. <i>(Oct 1, 1936,NZ Farmer,1349</i>)

>  The check pens in turn usually opened into much larger holding yards, which correspond in size to the receiving yards. <i>(Jul 21, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,7</i>)

>  <b>GASCOiGNE</b> .. So by a simple process of keeping the former constantly replenished from the main holding yards which led into it, there was always a waiting penful for the attention of the man with the raddle. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,155</i>)

>  <b>BATES</b> An approach race and holding yards were built at one end and at the other end a sloping draining pen leading up the bank to a holding paddock. <i>(1988,Okoroa,36</i>)

### holdover
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) farm stock, usually empty dairy cows, that are carried over from one season to the next.

>  Holdover cow grazing available... Grazing available, holdover cows or long term heifers. <i>(Apr 14, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,28</i>)

>  Over the past few years Harmony has wintered 675 Romney ewes, 180 ewe hoggets, 55 ram hoggets, 22 holdover lambs, 31 rams and killers ... <i>(Nov 29, 2002,Dominion,C9</i>)

>  Currently milking 500 cows and grazing 92 yearlings, 96 calves, 8 bulls and 8 holdovers. <i>(Dec 18, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,20</i>)

>  Rearing excessive numbers of replacements, selling budget cows, keeping hold-overs, rearing a few beefies is not core business [for dairy farmers.] <i>(May 2, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,18</i>)

### Holdsworth climbing irons
 
 <i>n.</i> [(? trampers') ca? 1940 (R. Mason) Irons developed in Mount Holdsworth bush warfare training camp in the Tararua Ranges.]

### hole
 
 <i>n.</i> a miner's excavation in the ground [(goldminers')]

>  <b>Walker</b> (Dunstan Diggings) Monday, got in stores at the following rates and pitched tent. Flour 9d. tea 4/6 a good handful ... Pick 15/6 Shovel 10/- Tin dish 6/- Billy 3/6 small swing pot for boiling Mutton 1/- prospected about 2 miles down the bank of the river, opened a hole Tuesday 9th bottomed the hole, found colour, ... <i>(Jan 8, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>)

>  ... they reached a bend of the Creek, where they found Ring's party at work, sinking into the bed of it, the water having been turned off a short distance above. Your Committee had a quantity of the stuff taken out of this hole, and washed ..., and every panful of it produced a few specks of gold. <i>(Nov 9, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/26/165</i>) [NOTES: (Report of Sub-Committee on Recent Gold Discovery in the Coromandel District)]

>  From every one of the holes sunk we obtained gold in paying quantities, ... <i>(Jun 29, 1861,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  <b>28</b> About one hole in every three appeared to be occupied ... No hole at the lower end of the gully, the part i first visited, appeared to have cost much labour. <i>(Sep 27, 1861,NZ Goldfields,1976</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Sep 27 Tuapeka in NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976) 28]

>  in that dip gold has been found in the majority of holes sunk, but to a considerable less extent than at Tuapeka. <i>(Oct 4, 1861,NZ Goldfields,41</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Oct 4 Dunedin in NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976) 41]

>  One hole has been bottomed on this range. [check=claim] <i>(1862,Otago: its Goldfields and Resources,26</i>)

>  <b>WEKEY</b> fn `To bottom a hole,' is a peculiar expression used on the mines in Australia; it means to convey the sinking of a shaft down to the bed-rock on which the auriferous drift generally rests. <i>(1862,Otago As it is,64</i>)

>  <i>(Jun 23, 1887,Chudleigh Diary,356,1950 ed.</i>) [NOTES: Got samples [of quartz] from several holes to take away and have assayed.]

>  <b>HOPEFUL</b> [Colonial slang] if a man isn't `up to the mark' he is called `a regular shicer!' This phrase originated from the gold diggings. Supposing a man is digging, but without success, and he therefore abandons his hole, it is called a `shicer' - that is, the ground was called a `shicer' or duffer, thus the origin. <i>(1887,Taken in,135</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> i bought and sold flour ..., got a 3-1/2 oz. nugget from the bottom of a hole that had been left, made a rise of [pounds]300 out of a pocket of gold like horse beans. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,115</i>)

>  <b>MiLLER</b> i was on gold all the time. i had sunk my first prospect hole at the lower end of a shallow depression ... and had struck the deepest part of the lead. it was a narrow gutter, no more than a foot wide at the most, and rich in heavy gold. <i>(1946,There Was Gold,69</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [in phrase sink a hole]

>  in the one or two holes that have been sunk gold has been obtained. ... Waipori has no `pile claims' ... <i>(1862,Otago: its Goldfields and Resources,21</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> (Central Otago) Found ourselves in the evening on a high gulley where somebody had sunk a hole but found nothing. <i>(Jan 11, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> Comes a miner treasure seeking,/ .../ Sinks a hole with pick and shovel;/ <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,66</i>)

>  <b>SCOTTER</b> The men [c1860's] kept sinking holes and trying out the gullies as they went, but found no gold nearer than Stotfold homestead. <i>(1948,Run, Estate and Farm,21</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(gumdiggers')]

>  <b>THOMAS</b> To see a fit and experienced gumdigger sinking a hole large enough to allow freedom of action, deep enough to get the gum, ... was to see something special as a test of competence, fitness and endurance. <i>(1970,Way Up North,30</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a dredge paddock or pond [(dredging)]

>  <b>KiTTO</b> if you are working a bank claim it is important that you keep the hole or paddock wide. ... This can only be done by taking out the corners every time you move ahead, and ... to do this it is necessary to keep the stern of the dredge out towards the centre of the paddock ... <i>(1900,Practical Dredgeman's Manual,18</i>)

### hole in/through the hill
 
 <i>phr.</i> the Lyttelton railway tunnel [(Canterbury)]

>  For thy precious zig-zags are too steep for a cart,/ And we're hopeless of holes in thy hill. <i>(Nov 1856,Canterbury Rhymes,38</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Nov Crosbie Ward `Road Lines' in 1883 Canterbury Rhymes 38]

>  <i>(May 11, 1867,Lyttelton Times</i>)

>  <b>MOSLEY</b> ... until the `hole through the hill' was decided upon. <i>(1885,illustrated Guide to Christchurch,27</i>)

>  <b>Slatter</b> The unreality of the tunnel, of this half-carriage with people chatting or reading the morning paper ... as they are jolted along the cross-section of a volcano, drawn by an electric locomotive which would have astounded the pioneers who dug this hole through the hill. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,18</i>)

>  <b>Bruce</b> The [Lyttelton-Christchurch] tunnel was ... thrown open to the public for inspection on June 10, 1867 ... On that auspicious day two thousand people passed through the `hole in the hill', ... <i>(1932,Early Days of Canterbury,175</i>)

### hole out
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>MASTERS</b> `Must be a pig, We're [sic] right out of meat,' remarked Tom. `How about you going in with Nell and holing it out. it will probably come along that track. i go [sic] over and wait and get it as it comes.' <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,160</i>)

### hole-in-the-wall justice
 
 <i>phr.</i> -

>  First there were hole-in-the-wall money machines - now we have hole-in-the-wall justice. <i>(Jan 25, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>)

### hole-in-the-walling
 
 <i>n.</i> using an ATM machine

>  After all no one got hurt [in a Wellington ATM burglary], it kept us all riveted over the dead holiday period, and hole-in-the-walling in this burgh will never be quite the same again. <i>(Jan 6, 2001,Evening Post,2</i>)

### holes
 
 <i>n.</i> a marbles game (See [also holesy](../A#also-holesy))

>  Colin Houlbrooke recalled the two main areas for battle with marbles as being the sealed area for 'rings' and the dirt behind the shed for 'holes'. <i>(1987,Alfredton,75</i>) [NOTES: Refers to c1935]

>  <b>Turner</b> Marble games, with terminology far too extensive to go into, included follies, the simplest, or holes, in which one had to roll a marble into the last of a series of holes, called poison, before attacking an opponent's taw. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,159</i>)

### holesy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGiLL</b> At the orphanage we played holesy... You had four holes in a row about a metre apart... The first to get his marble into all four holes became a killer. <i>(1989,Kiwi Baby Boomers,43</i>)

### holey
 
 <i>n.</i> a marble game [cf. holes, holesy]

>  <b>FRAME</b> `Treasure' i played ringie and holie. i played funs and keeps. i had pee-wees and bully taws and changers that weren't made of glass mind you. And i won nearly every game because the kids were so dazzled by the look of my stinkers. ... They came for miles to play marbles with me ... <i>(1951,Lagoon, The,116,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> There is only one record of a New Zealand game in which a formal penalty element entered. This was a game of marbles called Dumps and was played at Wakefield in 1875. in this game the loser, in a marble game of Holey, was required to hold his marble partially exposed between the fingers of his closed fist [for others to shoot at]. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/719</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> in the nineteenth century the ring game seems to have been the most popular marble game. (in more recent years marble games with holes have had the greater role.) There was Big Ring, in which a circle a yard or more in diameter was drawn on the ground; there was the mullibar variation, in which an outsize taw was catapulted amongst the marbles in this circle. There were Little Ring, Liney, Follows, Follow the Taw, Holey, and then, on a less important scale, Butcher's Board, in which the marbles were bowled through the holes in a board, Eye-Drop, Knock-Backs, Leggings-Out (or Leggin's-Out), Span-'Em, and Backits. The last was more usually a girls' game. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

>  <b>KEiTH & MAiN</b> ] [Photo caption] Marbles became `in' or `out' capriciously but someome always knew the rules of the various games - `holey,' `liney' and, seen here, `ringey' - which could be played for `funs' or `keeps.' <i>(1984,NZ Yesterdays,288</i>)

### holey dollar
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> in New South Wales, in 1813, this difficulty [of shortage of coin] became so acute that Governor Macquarie, in an effort to check the outflow of Spanish dollars, then the main circulating medium, caused the centres of these coins to be struck out, the centre-piece (about the size of a shilling) being stamped NEW SOUTH WALES, 1813, around a central crown, and on the other side FiFTEEN PENCE, the remainder or `ring' part of the dollar being impressed around the new inner rim with NEW SOUTH WALES, 1813 on one side, and FiVE SHiLLiNGS on the other. ... [67] 3,008 Dollars (mainly Holey or Ring Dollars) [pounds] 752 0 0/ 725 Dumps (centres of Spanish Dollars) 45 6 3 <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,66</i>)

>  holey dollar, holy dollar n. Spanish dollar out of which a dump was punched. See dump. (Historical, about 1815.) <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

 <i>n.</i> Holloway's crystalwort (<i>Atriplex hollowayi</i>)

>  As a consequence, few New Zealanders would be aware of the other native strand plants, and even fewer would be lucky to see the rarest of them all, and the only one endemic to these shores, Holloway's crystalwort (<i>Atriplex hollowayi</i>) - one of the most threatened species of our native flora. <i>(Mar 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 50/24</i>)

>  The extremely rare coastal plant Holloway's Crystalwort (<i>Atriplex hollowayi</i>) has received a much needed helping hand from a keen band of Te Hapua school children. ... Holloway's Crystalwort is a small New Zealand annual plant which grows near the high tide level on sandy beaches over summer. <i>(Jul 2005,Conservation Action,p 45</i>)

 <i>n.</i> <i>Olearia illicifolia</i>; holly-leaved olearia, an evergreen shrub with red berries and sharp, pointed, shiny leaves used as a Christmas decoration [check Maori holly]; Andersen (7 entries) Native Holly 1890 MLN 1925 DDD2, Andersen (4 entries) N.Z. <b>3</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Olearia macrodonta.</i>

>  <b>CHAPPLE</b> A wide variety of plants and trees crowded the track as i made my way up to Harpers Pass. Mountain holly, the Mount Cook lily, tree fuchsia, the turpentine shrub, mountain pine. <i>(2002,Te Araroa: the New Zealand Trail,191</i>)

### hollywood
 
 <i>n.</i> a feigned injury; esp. in to do/pull/throw a hollywood, to feign or exaggerate the seriousness of an injury during a game [(sporting) (informal) 1984 16 F E8 Pakuranga Coll. 31 HOLLYWOOD -->1984 14 F E125Wgton Girls C. 35C HOLLYWOOD: FAKING SOMETHING]

>  The cries of "Hollywood" have been known to ring out from sports crowds as they complain about a player pretending to be injured. <i>(Apr 30, 2003,Capital Times,14</i>)

>  i think it was all a bit of a Hollywood by the Belgian who [had] already been yellow carded earlier in the game for his play. <i>(Jul 23, 2001,Evening Post,26</i>)

>  All Blacks v Lions, (Griffiths Collection) a rugby term for an assumed injury, originally any acting and explained as such by the TV commentator <i>(Jul 10, 1971,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: 1971 July 10 chant at Lancaster Park]

>  A `Hollywood' is, of course, a performance by a [here rugby union] player feigning injury. `Hollywoods' happen on defence but never on attack. <i>(Jul 18, 1971,Sunday Times (NZ),22</i>)

>  Hollywood: act of showing off <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  a Hollywood: an act, a pretense to do a Hollywood: to pretend to be injured <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> i said, `it's a Hollywood. He's sticking his finger down his throat. ignore him.' But she said, No. it's genuine.' <i>(1985,Nest of Cuckoos,69</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> `it's a giant Hollywood ... How come he's hugging his guts? i hit him on the ear.' <i>(1985,Gumboots and Goalposts,59</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hollywood dubious sporting injury demonstrated with transparent acting of a kind associated with Hollywood films; do a Hollywood perpetrate such dubious acting. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,57</i>)

>  He accuses players of pulling Hollywoods. `Sometimes, players have even feigned injury just for a few minutes spell.' <i>(Jul 18, 1972,NZ Truth,37</i>)

>  do a hollywood: For those folks who come from the Los Angeles area, it won't surprise you that this term means TO EXAGGERATE. it usually refers to the histrionic exaggeration of an injury on the playing field. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,?52</i>)

>  Police escorted him from the ground ... The accused, ... shouted and argued with police for the benefit of his friends. `in other words, you did a Hollywood,' said Judge Gilbert. <i>(Mar 9, 1983,Evening Post,5/5</i>)

>  <b>McLEAN</b> Welsh players nearby were soon telling the All Blacks not to worry about the incident ... because `Clive is only "throwing a Hollywood"'. <i>(1964,Willie Away,115</i>)

>  Recently i found myself dealing, in a relatively public place, with a young man who, in the words of the immortals, was `throwing a Hollywood'. <i>(Nov 23, 1988,Evening Post,37</i>)

>  <b>McCarthy</b> The opponent rose to the surface and turned on a "Hollywood." <i>(1973,it's a Goal,139</i>)

### holocaust
 
 <i>n.</i> (controversially) applied in NZ contexts, esp. to the Parihaka operation against Taranaki Maori [phrase originally used in Waitangi Tribunal Taranaki report; later approved & adopted by Tariana Turia]

>  The Tangahoe (South Taranaki) holocaust has yet to be acknowledged and fully researched. <i>(Dec 1996,North & South,35</i>)

>  if the obviously intended comparison with the Holocaust suffered by European Jews is justified (which it isn't), then the slaughter and enslavement by Taranaki Maori of the Chatham islands Moriori in 1835-36 is certainly a "holocaust" also. <i>(Jun 29, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  Alan Ward's study of the pattern of Maori dispossession is of lasting value - and [the Tribunal] has made bad errors. its use of the word "holocaust" to describe the official theft of Taranaki land was ludicrous and inept and opened the process to needless controversy and ridicule. <i>(May 18, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C8</i>)

>  When asked about the usage of the word "holocaust" to describe the oppression of Maori, [Fr Lapsley] said it is good that it caused debate, but it is a mistake to get fixated on this term. <i>(Dec 7, 2000,City Voice,3</i>)

>  The graphic muru of most of Taranaki and the raupatu without ending describe the holocaust of Taranaki history and the denigration of the founding peoples in a continuum from 1840 to the present. <i>(1996,Waitangi Tribunal Report,143 : 312</i>)

>  [Hdg] i'm sorry ... but it was a holocaust... Miss Clark told Mrs Turia that she must never again compare an event in New Zealand history to the Holocaust "where six million people were herded in trains and cattle trucks and put in gas chambers where they were exterminated." <i>(Sep 6, 2000,Dominion,1</i>)

>  The Waitangi Tribunal also came under fire in 1996 for describing the 19th century's Taricha land confiscations as the "holocaust of Taranaki history", in its interim report on the Taranaki claim. <i>(Aug 31, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  "What seems not to have received similar attention [as the Jewish holocaust] is the holocaust suffered by indigenous people including Maori as a result of colonial contact and behaviour," she said. <i>(Aug 29, 2000,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  in fact, the only indisputable "holocaust" to blight New Zealand history was the mass enslavement and murder of the Moriori people of the Chatham islands by Te Atiawa during the 1830s and 40s. <i>(Sep 1, 2000,Dominion,8</i>)

>  [She] understood that she was not to use the word "holocaust" in relation to what has happened in New Zealand. <i>(Sep 6, 2000,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  There <i>was</i> a Holocaust in New Zealand in European times and it was visited on the Chatham islands Morioris by Taranaki Maoris. <i>(Sep 18, 2000,Dominion,10</i>)

>  [Ms Lee] said: "Holocaust? in 1840 there were 40,000 of our people in this country. Forty years later there was one quarter left. if that is not a holocaust how does a holocaust word apply to anything?" <i>(Nov 6, 2000,Evening Post,2</i>)

### holt
 
 <i>n.</i> a wrestling hold

>  <b>SEDDON</b> The boys learnt a form of wrestling called `Collar and elbow' brought by miners from Lancashire. A Cumberland form of that sport was not so popular. in `Collar and elbow' the opponents clutched one another in strange `holts' - a right hand grasped an opponent's left shoulder, the other hand grabbed his adversary's right elbow. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,22</i>)

### Holy Roller
 
 <i>n.</i> the vessel Cobar, a small ferry plying Wellington Harbour from Wellington wharves to Days Bay. [ca1925 p.c. H.A.L. Laing 1982 Aug 2 used in especially Canterbury and Otago)]

>  <b>HYDE</b> The little girls are pleased at having crossed the harbour in the Cobar, known in Wellington as `The Holy Roller'. `Oo, didn't she roll, Em'ly?' they pipe. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,113,1970 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> waterside worker [(fig. obs)]

>  <b>BOWMAN</b> The watersiders of the 'eighties were called "Holy Rollers", possibly because much of the cargo they handled was rolled along the wharf or about the hold, and it is said that they became really expert at trundling bales of wool or other suitable packages in this way. <i>(1948,Port Chalmers,43</i>)

### Homai
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> i remember two essentially Maori games, Cat's Cradle and Homai, but the others were all traditional English games i think, ... (1912-20; Ohau) <i>ibid.</i> 231 There are games recorded in this period for which there is no record before 1900 though they may well have existed under other names. West Coast: Dunk and Davey, Spang Weazling; Wellington: Who Goes Round My Stone Wall, Beg o' My Neighbor, Bellahonie, Run a Mile, On and Off the Bridge, Here Come Two Nuns, Colours, isaac and Rebecca. Scrag, Diabolo, Bumble Puppy, Homai, Fives; Auckland: My Fair Young Lady, Rushes and Reeds, Surrender the Tower, Walls, Ball Hopscotch, ball games, Solomon Says; Hawkes Bay: Wolf Wolf Go Home, Homai; Canterbury: Poor Old Tom, Rats and Rabbits and Rips, Steps, Skin the Snake, Steal a Peg, Toodle-em-Buck, The Gag; Golden Bay: Who's That Walking Round My House Tonight, i've Come to See Poor Mary Jane, Steps and Stairs, Creeping, Branding, O'Leary; Otago: Sir Roger de Coverley, Who's That Knocking at My Door. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,230</i>)

### homai nomai
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  The principle of "Homai Nomai" is very popular in the Church, as well as out of it. <i>(Jul 9, 1904,NZ Observer,3</i>)

### Homaiacky
 
 <i>n.</i> a former children's game

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Of the Help-He games, Kick the Tin, is the most widely known. A tin is placed in the centre of a circle. One He is in charge of the tin. He can only tag the other players when his tin is in the centre of the circle. Any players he catches must stay in the circle. Any non-caught player can help and free the other caught players by running through the circle and kicking the tin out of it. Other names for this game are: Kick the Boot (Gisborne [18]90), Knock the Block or Homaiacky (Nelson [18]95), Kick the football or Piacky (Mangatainoka). <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/513</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> in a few tagging games the players joined hands with the He when they were caught and helped him catch the other players. These games were known as The Bellahonie (E-21) (Wellington, 1910), Kick Post One (Hampden, Otago, 1880), and Ballyhooley (Wellington, 1920). More popular were those games in which the players helped one another to fight the He, and of these the most widespread was the game known as Kick the Tin (e-3), Kick the Boot, Kick the Block, or Homiacky. in this game the He was put on guard of a jam tin placed inside a small circle marked on the ground. [Other players hid: those He could see he named and tapped the tin three times and `captured': other hiders waited till they had chance to kick tin out of circle: then all were free again.] <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,80</i>)

### Home
 
 <i>adv.</i> to Britain

>  Miss Nelle Scanlon, a vivid reporter and journalist.. is going Home to widen her sphere of work. <i>(Sep 7, 1927,NZ Tablet,29</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the Home: the maternity home, hospital [ca1920 at least. (Havelock) Widespread usage among women, esp. in express. e.g. I'm going into the Home `I'm going to have a baby'; `I'm about to have a baby', for maternity home or hospital. Sts called the nursing home.]



 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib. uses]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> [British] ... in making known to the deputation [of Wellington settlers] the arrangements and plans he [Sir George Gipps] would propose for them to the home Government, he assured them of the interest which he took in the welfare of the colony, ... <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),11</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> We have had no home letters for two months. When they do come i fancy the slush lamp will be kept going half the night. <i>(Jan 27, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>) [NOTES: (Central Otago)]

>  <b>WiERZBiCKA</b> Got my letters from Victoria but not my home letters, which is strange especially as i got home papers. <i>(Apr 21, 1874,Wilson Family,179</i>) [NOTES: 1874 Apr 21 J.G.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 179 [=English]]

>  <b>WESTON</b> Any steamer leaving the city for any southern, Australian or `home' port is sure to be visited by a large assemblage of people. <i>(1890,Ko Meri,303</i>)

>  Any steamer leaving the city for any southern, Australian or `home' port is sure to be visited by a large assemblage of people. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,123</i>)

>  Now, i think if you leave England alone, and just look after your own country, you will find plenty of matter in it to fill your paper, including immorality. You seem to have a derry on the Home people. <i>(Jun 1, 1912,NZ Truth,4</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The reputation of `Prime Canterbury' on the Home market is one of long standing ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,67</i>)

>  A decision to send a delegation to the Old Country was arrived at. ...What is the delegation going Home for? What is it to say to the Home producer of like food-stuffs? Some ass has said that it is a delegation of good-will to lay the position of the poor Antipodean dairy-man before his relative and talk to him of the `Empire' and `loyalty'! <i>(Mar 25, 1934,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/145</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> Meeting home vessels was always an uncertain business; you could never tell to an hour or two. <i>(1934,Winds of Heaven,35</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> The Otago Witness made a welcome if irregular appearance, and Home papers occasionally came,... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,125</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Take ... the land which we New-Zealanders still speak of under the familiar term of `Home,' though many of us have never lived there ... <i>(1895,TrNZi,27/240</i>)

>  in these colonies especially the proportion of land occupiers and owners is so large that most of us are directly or indirectly interested in `section pegs,' `trigs,' and such-like, while at Home the expression of this interest is practically limited to the impersonal `beating the bounds of the parish.' <i>(1901,TrNZi,33/492</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> He also wrote Home letters very often ... <i>(1902,Happy Endings,110</i>) [NOTES: 1902 Lancaster Our Lady of the Plains in Happy Endings 110]

>  <i>(Jan 4, 1929,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/163,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>GUTHRiE</b> if there is one desire common to those adventurers among the people of the bight ... it is to see what they still call the Old Country ... and, in common speech, Home. <i>(1935,Little Country, The,1,1937 ed.</i>)

>  They have told of `Maori scares,' when they had to flee for their lives from hostile natives, and of other `Maori scares,' which proved to be merely neighbourly visits ... and the wonderful joy of letters from Home (always spelt with a capital H) ... <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,vii,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> When we left Home we were armed with a list of names ... <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,47</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> G.B. and ireland; esp. and gen. England : Colonial, prob first in U.S. (cf Thornton) in 18c. : 20c Aust and NZ esp. go, at home.] (it is perhaps possible to distinguish among the earliest recorded uses, a specific reference to England, rather than other parts of the British isles, or to the British isles themselves. This would be expected, as most early Company settlers were English born. My evidence suggests that 1840-1860 usage was mainly by settlers in Canterbury, Nelson, and Wellington. There seems no early evidence of its use in Otago.) By the first settlers, and the early generations of colonially born, England; the British isles; the Old Country; the mother country; one's home country. A. Referring to the homeland left. 1. a. Chiefly `England'. (i) initial cap. [?more freq. proportionally after WW1].

>  The fellows that frequently hang round hotels, / Out here are called loafers, at home are called swells. <i>(1875,NZ Songster,3/88</i>)

>  Everyone in New Zealand speaks of England as "Home", even those who were born in the colony and have never visited the old country. <i>(Sep 10, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  What will the grocery department of John Nathan & Co. do when Mr James Muir goes Home? <i>(Mar 2, 1912,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  <b>CADEY</b> The old dart is the old country, home. <i>(1933,Broken Pattern,93</i>)

>  Everyone in New Zealand speaks of England as "Home", even those who were born in the colony and have never visited the old country. <i>(Sep 10, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  At Home we should have had to manage as best we could. <i>(Mar 26, 1927,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,34</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> 3. The Boyd came out here with convicts from ireland ... and was returning to England by way of New Zealand, with intention to carry Home some timber and spars from that country, .<i>ibid.</i>. 1/297 ...the projectors of the flax speculation on New Zealand still intend to prosecute their design; and ... send a party of men ... on board the brig Experiment, to New Zealand, where that vessel is to remain long enough to give them time to collect a quantity of dried flax to be sent Home for the inspection of His Majesty's Ministers ... <i>(1908,Historical Records,1/296</i>) [NOTES: 1810 Mar 12 (Sydney) Governor Macquarie to Viscount Castlereagh in 1908 McNab Historical Records]

>  <b>ELDER</b> `i have in my last letter to Mr. Woodd written Home for assistance. <i>(1934,Marsden's Lieutenants,112</i>) [NOTES: 1815 Oct 19 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 112]

>  i have this day suspended the Rev. J. Butler from all connection with the Church Missionary Society until the pleasure of the committee is known at Home. <i>(Jan 13, 1824,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,412,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLEAN</b> ... the public opinion at Home. <i>(1848,Papers,V/142</i>)

>  if a separate house were formed for Maori representatives, there is little doubt but that, with a few official leaders appointed direct from Home as protectors, the Maori Chiefs would be found quite able to take their full share in the representation. <i>(Mar 24, 1858,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Mar 24 T.S.Grace letter in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 77]

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... i stayed with friends in Melbourne, awaiting a passage Home in one of Money Wigram's monthly sailing ships. <i>(Dec 20, 1864,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,78,1914 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... i found myself steaming out of Wellington Harbour in the Panama Company's ship `Kaikoura,' bound for Home after just seven year's absence. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,148</i>)

>  <b>iRViNE & ALPERS</b> The New Zealanders have so for remained more distinctly English than, perhaps, is the case in some of the larger colonies. ... . . all classes, whether immigrant or native-born, habitually speak of the United Kingdom as `Home'. <i>(1902,Prospects of New Zealand, The,429</i>)

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> Visitors from our oversea dominions, when they come Home, invariably swear that they have in their own little State something better than anything in the same line to be seen in the Old Country. <i>(1912,impressions of a NZ Pastoralist on Tour,123</i>)

>  <b>CRESWELL</b> i could be a poet tomorrow if i liked to succumb to classical influences, as i was at school, and as i became much more when i first came `Home'. <i>(Mar 1917,Letters,26</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> And the third, his friend Todd, was eating his heart out because he could not get Home to Oxford. <i>(1925,Allen Adair,5,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> As far back as i can remember, it was `Home'. in the little New Zealand country community in which we lived, it was as natural to talk of England and ireland as `Home' as it was to call New Zealand a colony. <i>(1927,Home: A New Zealander's Adventure,3</i>)

>  <b>REES</b> Ann already knew that `Home' with a big `H' signified England. <i>(1928,Wild, Wild Heart,8</i>)

>  When i presumed to take on the cloak you [Buck] dropped ... and in the borrowed mantle essayed to climb Hikurangi and so catch ... views of the local conflict i was amazed that so much progress had been made in winning the regard ... of the `pakeha'. The generation for which the New Zealand Herald catered in leading articles had almost passed away. Your lectures throughout New Zealand had set the best minds among the pakeha at work. With that generation also had largely passed the pakeha who was wedded to the idea of England being `Home'. The native born New Zealand pakeha is looking nearer to his native heath for objects of study, literary effort and missionary care. <i>(Jan 4, 1929,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/163,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>Mrs LUSH</b> He called England, `Home'; and other endearing names as well; such as `The Mother land', `The land of Hope and Glory', `The Old Country', and `The Homeland'. <i>(1930,Wandering Wood</i>)

>  England (C.R., p.4(1 <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The,17</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>JONES</b> ... in 1902 i went for a trip Home. The old settlers used to speak of Great Britain as `Home'. <i>(1933,Autobiography of an Early Settler,141</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Everyone that Bobby knew spoke wistfully of England as `Home' - but in his secret heart he was not sure that he ever wanted to go Home. <i>(1934,Windy island,10</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> .. your parents vamp up another talent for you and pack you off Home. <i>(1934,Journalese,24</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> New Zealand has never established its own culture except of material things. For decades it has called Britain Home, a term that is finally being discarded. <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,121</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> `i hope to go Home some day' You mean England, i suppose. `Yes, of course,' replied Philip. He really meant the British isles with England predominant. ... i don't think of England as Home. ... if there's any `Home' for me in that sense, it's ireland. But this country is our real home. ... in thinking of England as `Home', Philip was typical of his countrymen. Nearly everyone spoke of `Home' - as naturally as, unknowingly, they sprinkled their speech with current phrases from Shakespeare. <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,18</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> in Australia its use in this sense [`England'] is practically obsolete Home to Australians means their native land; it is becoming increasingly so used in New Zealand. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,43</i>)

>  i would include among New Zealand English words: Home: England <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  People talked of England as `Home' [in the 1950s] but there was a growing Americanism in the movies ... <i>(1987,Metro,52</i>) [NOTES: Roger `Auckland Life']

>  <b>MiTCHELL</b> it was early in the year 1870 ... that i composed the following pieces, some of which appeared in the `Oamaru Times' ... [21] THE LEVANTiNG COCKATOOS./ in summer time, when work was slack/ .../ ... says C. to Jack,/ `Let's go a cockatooing'./ They leased some land upon the plain/ On very easy terms,/ And wrote Home to anxious friends/ About their stock and farms./ <i>(1889,Rhymes and Rambles,20</i>) [NOTES: [c1870] in 1889 Mitchell Rhymes and Rambles 20-21]

>  Our children do not talk of England as home; they know who they are and where they belong. <i>(May 31, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  <b>BATiSTiCH</b> She was going Home. Home was England, even to colonials who had never seen it; just your grandfather being born there was enough. The idea would die hard in New Zealand that Home was some place else.For us it was Dalmatia. For Wing, China. For the Hegartys, ireland ... for George, Greece. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,153</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> We all still spoke of England as "Home," liners were "Home boats," am immigrant "just out from Home." <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,219</i>)

>  Ny parents were stong Anglophiles, and my mother was proud to speak of her trip "home" in 1914. <i>(Feb 5, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BRODiE</b> ... which they call the Old Country, the Old Land, the Old Dart, and, in common speech, Home. <i>(1935,Little Country, The,1</i>)

>  <b>COWiE</b> The word Home on most New Zealanders' lips is no more than a synonym for the United Kingdom; it does not mean `dear old England', or `my beloved birthplace,' or `my real country'; it does not even mean what the United Kingdom means to all irish irishmen. it simply means the United Kingdom, and is used without emotion, as a word that has been handed down from father to son, and mother to daughter. it is part of the real New Zealand language, a genuine piece of Antipodean slang. And, like all slang, it has its significance. <i>(1937,New Zealand From Within,265</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> Home. Great Britain and ireland; esp., an gen., England: Colonial, prob. first in U.S. (by 1912 very ob., says Thornton) in C. 18. in C. 20, mostly Australian and New Zealand. Esp. as as at h., go h. A coll. usage bordering on-indeed, by the O.E.D. considered as-S.E. -4. (home.) A dug-out in the front trench: New Zealand soldiers': 1915-18. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,399</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> ... don't mind if you're called a Homie. After all, what does it mean? Somebody from Home. A bit sissy, but it could be worse. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,165</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> She looked like a real old-timer, but they said she was married to a toff before she came out from Home. <i>(1949,i Saw in My Dream,247,(1974 ed. Rhodes) ed.</i>)

>  <b>WALL</b> `Ayt' [for ate] is not unknown at Home, but is much commoner here than it is there. <i>(1958,Queen's English, The,14</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> How typical of Christchurch that there should be a New Brighton and a Pier too! An attempt of the marooned to remind them of Home. Why can't we forget Home and make a country of our own? <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,53</i>)

>  <b>CURNOW</b> His men friends call him plain Mandragora - the way they do at Home. Bob: Home? Where's that? Gillian: Oh, you know what i mean - ... <i>(1962,Four Plays,106,1972 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (The Overseas Expert)]

>  Home n. word used in N.Z. since 1840 for Great Britain (obs. in Australia). <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> She had never had a trip Home, she never played bridge, ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,18</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... she was an `Aussie' boat and had just come from home. <i>(Dec 15, 1918,Kia-ora Coo-ee,Second Series 6,1981 ed.</i>)

>  The soldier who has come home is not glad because there is so much to remember, and to forget, so much to regret. `Home' was a glad place to look forward to, but now that home is reached it does not shine in contrast to the light of other days ... <i>(Dec 1, 1919,Quick March,67</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> `i hope to go Home some day,' said Philip. ... `You mean England, i suppose?' asked Mark. `Yes, of course,' replied Philip. He really meant the British isles with England predominant. `Well, i don't think of England as Home,' said Mark. ... [19] `if there's any `Home' for me in that sense, it's ireland. But this country is our real home.' <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,18</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib. uses] (See [also Home boat](../A#also-home-boat),[Homeboat](../H#homeboat))

>  <b>SAM STEPHENS</b> [My] land around me is begining to have a `home' look [?? homely] <i>(1844,Journal,358</i>)

>  <b>JHK</b> He exchanged to [a regiment] in N.Z., where what are called colonial allowances, though paid by the home government, would enable him to support himself. <i>(1872,Henry Ancrum,55</i>)

>  <b>ALLEN</b> i still talk of the `Home Papers' anyhow. <i>(1936,Poor Scholar,37</i>)

### home
 
 <i>n.</i> [with or without quotation marks or italics]

>  <b>Godley</b> He is ... only partner ... with Mr. Clifford, another R.C. who is now `at home', England. <i>(Jun 27, 1850,Letters,82,1951 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> .. and it is easy to recognise a person from home, or one born in the colony, no matter what class of society, from this circumstance [sc. the pure English of the colonials]. <i>(1834,Wanderings in NSW (etc.),331</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> Today we sail for Sydney .us;`en route' for `home'. <i>ibid.</i>187 [New Zealand] is an interesting and very beautiful country too, and were it not so very far from `home', i should not be at all sorry to return to it. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,186</i>) [NOTES: [1848 June 4]]

>  <b>GODLEY</b> [?av] Arthur begged i would send this drawing `home'. <i>(Dec 19, 1851,Letters,1951 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GiLBERT</b> ... remind me of ... the chalk cliffs of dear old England -Beachy head, and other favourite localities at `home'. <i>(1861,NZ Settlers and Soldiers,33</i>)

>  <b>HODDEN</b> All are emigrants [at Nelson]; all have reminiscences about the mother-country, which is invariably designated home. <i>(1862,Memories of NZ Life,51</i>)

>  <b>LADY CAMPBELL</b> Moreover very satisfactory letters from `home' had been brought by one of the Governor's staff ... <i>(1864,Martin Tobin,3/92</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> in the colonies Great Britain is always spoken of as `home', even by colonial-born people. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a (dwelling) house; a house to live in and its material surroundings. [now general English?]

>  [You] see instead pleasant bungalows with lawns and flower beds and trees, all very tidy and peaceful, ... The homes are modern and sit comfortably on large sections. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,30</i>)

>  - <i>(1979,Heinemann NZ Dictionary,518</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> Give New Zealand but respectable settlers from home, with a moderate share of encouragement from the Government, and a [21] very short period will develope her resources, and place her among the most respectable of the colonies of Great Britain; and that, too, without expense to the English Government. <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,20,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> ... whether he sends from home a British vessel, to take on board and carry direct to England the oil thus cured [sc. at Otago] <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,264</i>) [NOTES: 1834 Statement of case: Weller's application in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 264 [=UK]]

>  .. in accordance with instructions from home. <i>(1842,NZ Gazette,(Supplement) 2/40</i>)

>  We have ... opened a store, what we should call a shop at home. <i>(1848,Chambers Edinburgh Journal,355</i>) [NOTES: (1842 Oct 2 Letter from Wellington in) 1848 Chambers' Journ `Emigrant Voices from NZ' 355]

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> ] Land jobbers at home, i suppose, considered that the culled residue would be an unmarketable commodity; ... <i>(1989,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,228</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Aug 16 Tuckett Letter in 1989 Hocken Contributions 228 [=England]

>  <b>FiTTON</b> ... i expect you would be surprised to see how very little the reality agrees with the notion formed at home of an early colonial life. ... Believe me, there is nothing wild or savage (hardly colonial), in our mode of life. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),272</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Aug in 1856 Fitton NZ 272]

>  Mr. Fyfe had found a moa's egg, at the Kai Koras, while digging the foundation for his store. it is a foot long, about 6 inches in diameter, and 27 inches in circumference. The shell is the 16th part of an inch in thickness. A hole is drilled in the end of it, and the egg must have been considered of great value by the natives, as it was found deposited at the head of a skeleton, with a number of very large poenamu axes. Mr. Fyfe, we understand, is going to send them home to the British Museum. <i>(Aug 5, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> By and by however one finds out that there are several more, and after travelling in the back country one begins almost to believe that Canterbury is not much more deficient in birds than our own island [44] at home. <i>(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,43</i>)

>  Percy and i walked on to the Port Heads [from Sumner] and i have not seen anything so much like home in N.Z. <i>(Sep 11, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,201,1950 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [=England (Cornwall)]]

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> And though he [the colonist] may not stand upon the very ground of his native country, yet at least he will live among his fellow-countrymen; and even if at times he feels inclined to regret for the pleasant places at home, he may recollect that life, even in the old country, is not altogether clear of difficulties ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,232</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> `... the family is going to England in February.' `... They are about the only station in the Wairarapa that hasn't been home.' <i>(1934,Winds of Heaven,78</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [occas. `Europe'. ca1949 A Catholic lady spoke of `Going home for the Holy year', sc. to Rome; and another of `Going home to the Vaticans'. a born colonial of Irish blood used `go home to Ireland'. ca1950]

>  Hame letters! i doubt there'll be nane for me; [home, none?????] <i>(1905,Poems & Sketches,30</i>) [NOTES: 1905 Catherine Richardson Poems and Sketches [sc. Scotland] in Centennial Treasury of Otago Verse 30]

>  When [the Ulster settlers, Katikati] spoke of `Home', as they did habitually, it was ireland - England they meant, with Scotland phs as an attachment. <i>(1948,Landfall,7/183</i>) [NOTES: (A. Mulgan)]

>  Still, i could see they wanted someone outside themselves to believe in rainbows and trout and pots of gold and that Southland would win the Ranfurly Shield ... and Dad would win on his ticket to Tattersalls and take us all home to Galway where he'd come from. <i>(Aug 29, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>) [NOTES: Davin]



 
 <i>n.</i> a dugout in the front trenches.

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> 1915-1918 : NZ Soldiers' <i>(1961,Dictionary of Slang</i>) [NOTES: (WW1 obs)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WOOD</b> Powerful forces have bound modern NZ to what is still sometimes called `home'. <i>(1944,Understanding NZ,3</i>)

>  <b>MiLLER</b> if it was only sentiment that sometimes led an educated Maori to speak of England as `home', it was something more than sentiment that in the dark days of 1914 and 1940 was to send the young Maoris forth ... to the defence of [England]. <i>(1950,New Zealand (various authors/titles),108</i>)

>  ... the colonists endeavoured to reproduce ... features of the country that many New Zealanders still, anachronistically, call `home'. <i>(Apr 1952,NZ Geographer,5</i>) [NOTES: (Murphy)]

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> The principal, a stickler for British teachers, asked her where she was educated and she replied quite honestly, `At home'. He understood her to mean England, so she was given the position ... <i>(1970,Life's Vagaries,64</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  home ... NZ & Austral. nostalgic name for Great Britain. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,358</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>McGiLL</b> home nostalgic name for Britain; c.1834. NZA. - army used for dug-out in the frontline WWi trenches. - gone home worn out clothes. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,57</i>)

### home and dried
 
 <i>phr.</i> variant of home and dry (labelled Austral. in SOED5): safe and sound, out of trouble [cf. home and hosed]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> You only need to take your shirt off like me, and we'll be home and dried [sc. win the tennis game]. <i>(1949,i Saw in My Dream,34,(1974 ed. Rhodes) ed.</i>)

### home and hosed
 
 <i>phr.</i> (informal) equivalent of Brit. home and dry [Alistair Paterson, 34B Methuen Rd, Avondale (Fax.(09)8282209 says used in 1940's at the Aurora Sailing Club, Nelson, to describe a boat that's finished a race (usually successfully) and has been hauled from the water onto the hard and hosed down. It's my recollection (DEH) that the same phrase was used then also by racing commentators and it was transferred to the winner of any competitive event. Partridge ("A Dictionary of Catch Phrases") discusses "home and hosed" as an Aus. variant of "home and dried on the pig's back." He also says: "The predominantly UK and Anglo-Irish elab. is 'home and dried with the blanket on,' which 'stems from horse-racing, but . . . is used for any mission accomplished' (Skehan, 1977)."]

>  Homin' Hosed peeled off from behind Bogan Fella in the home straight and got up for victory. <i>(Nov 10, 1999,Dominion,48</i>)

>  <b>Sue Wootton</b> Seriously it's not a problem. We've got it completely covered, you're home and hosed. <i>(2008,Six Pack 3, The,57</i>)

>  Here we are, home and hosed. <i>(1962,Four Plays,CURNOW,108 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (The Overseas Expert)]

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> Success! The venture is now completely secured against failure. .in 4;`if you done that, you're over the hard part; you're home and hosed!' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],518</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER`</b> Then you'll be jake. Pile on a coupla tries and you'll be home and hosed. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,206</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `Home and hosed,' said Spandau. `Don't be so cocky,' i tell him. <i>(1970,On the Ball,139</i>)

>  We were not home and hosed, but it gave us a little bit of an uplift as it was the seventh day in the raft and we thought we were making progress ... <i>(Jul 14, 1988,Dominion,1</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> Bob jerks, though any tension he displays now is really pre-meeting leftovers. He knows he's home and hosed. <i>(1979,Skin Deep,142</i>)

>  home ... 13. home and dry. Brit. sl. definitely safe or successful. Austral. & N.Z. equivalent: home and hosed. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,535</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 358; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 235]]

>  New Zealanders home and hosed [headline] The young New Zealand cricket team romped home to victory yesterday at Bulawayo Athletic Club, Harare, by 100 runs ... <i>(Oct 16, 1984,Dominion,19</i>)

>  <b>O'SULLiVAN</b> Of course once you do have the respect of these fellows you're virtually home and hosed. <i>(1985,Shuriken,60</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> home and hosed successfully completed; from horse racing; eg `Looks already like John Walker's home and hosed in the mile.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,57</i>)

>  Home and Hosed - The Kits - The Story So Far - The Chaps - The Archive - The Rave (pages of the beige brigade website) <i>(Jan 2006,internet - see Notes,0</i>) [NOTES: http://www.beigebrigade.co.nz/index.html]

### home block
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  i never saw the country so bare of food ... The view deteriorates as fast as the plough improves the home blocks. <i>(Apr 23, 1888,Chudleigh Diary,361,1950 ed.</i>)

>  We'll keep the home-block <i>(1902,NZ illustrated Magazine,7/117</i>)

>  Lane's out-station was 12 rough miles from the home-block. <i>(1905,Sons o' Men,3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a property on which a manager or owner lives when there is more than one property in the same ownership.

>  <b>TAiT</b> The home block was then only partly cleared and the 500-acre bush-block was barely touched ... <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol ii,495</i>)

>  They have 80 ha of run-off 10km away from the home block where they can make silage as well as rear young stock and winter up to 500 cows. <i>(Oct 1999,NZ Dairy Exporter,14</i>)

>  The 40 ha home block has lighter alluvial soil and peat. <i>(Mar 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,14</i>)

### Home boat
 
 <i>n.</i> an English ship; a ship running between England and NZ [also Homeboat (and l.c. home)]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> We all still spoke of England as "Home," liners were "Home boats," am immigrant "just out from Home." <i>(1983,North & South,219</i>)

### home country
 
 <i>n.</i> Britain

### home detention
 
 <i>n.</i>
